Skip them only if you already know what debits and credits are and how the accounting cycle works.

Unit 01 · A.1 · Financial Statements

The Balance Sheet

A photograph of what a company owns and owes — taken at one specific moment in time.

§1.1The one idea you must hold

Imagine someone asks you, "What are you worth right now?" You'd add up everything you own (your car, your savings, your laptop), then subtract everything you owe (your credit card balance, student loans, the rent you haven't paid). What's left over is yours. That's it. That's a balance sheet.

For a company, those three buckets get formal names:

The Accounting Equation
Assets  =  Liabilities  +  Equity

What we have = what we owe + what's ours

This equation must always balance. Every transaction touches at least two of these buckets, and they always net out. That's not a rule someone made up — it's just arithmetic. If you didn't owe anyone, all your assets would be your equity. If you owe someone, that part of your assets is theirs, not yours.

Analogy — your house

Your house is worth $400,000 (asset). The bank is owed $300,000 of it (liability). The remaining $100,000 is your stake (equity). If the house's value rises to $450,000 but the loan stays $300,000, your equity grows to $150,000 — without you doing anything except owning the asset.

Try it

Watch the equation balance

Drag the sliders. Notice how every change to assets or liabilities forces equity to move so the equation always holds.

$1,000
$600
⚠ Equity went negative. The company owes more than it owns — this is real (it's called "stockholders' deficit") and it's a serious warning sign.

§1.2What goes where? Current vs. Non-current

Inside each of those three buckets, items are sorted by how soon they'll turn into (or use up) cash. The dividing line is one year — or one operating cycle, whichever is longer.

A classified balance sheet — read left to right, top to bottom, the most liquid items first.

Why "current" matters

Lenders and investors care intensely about current items because they signal whether a company can pay its bills this year. The famous current ratio = Current Assets / Current Liabilities tells you, roughly, "for every dollar of bills due soon, how many dollars of stuff do you have to pay them with?" A ratio under 1.0 means short-term trouble.

Sort it

Drag each item to the right bucket

Drop each chip into where it belongs on a balance sheet. Click Check to grade.

Items to classify — drag from here
Current Assets (0)
Non-current Assets (0)
Current Liabilities (0)
Non-current Liab. (0)
Equity (0)

§1.3What the balance sheet won't tell you

The balance sheet has a famous limitation: most assets are recorded at historical cost — what the company paid years ago — not what they're worth today. So a Manhattan building bought in 1980 for $5M might sit on the books at $5M minus depreciation, even though it's worth $200M now. The balance sheet is reliable but conservative; book value rarely equals market value.

Also, things like brand reputation, employee skill, and customer loyalty — clearly valuable — usually don't appear at all, because they weren't bought in a transaction.

Common confusion

"Equity" on a balance sheet is book value of the owners' stake, not market capitalization. A company can have $1B of book equity and a $50B stock market cap, or vice versa. The balance sheet records what was paid in plus profits kept; the stock market reflects what investors think the future is worth.

Quick check
A company has $500K in assets and $700K in liabilities. What does this tell you?
  • The balance sheet is wrong — assets must equal liabilities.
  • Equity is $1,200K (assets + liabilities).
  • Equity is negative $200K — the company owes more than it owns.
  • The balance sheet won't balance, so it can't be reported.
Correct: Equity is negative $200K. Rearranging the equation: Equity = Assets − Liabilities = $500K − $700K = −$200K. This is a real situation called a "stockholders' deficit" and it means the company has accumulated more losses (or paid out more dividends) than it has paid-in capital. It's a serious warning sign — not impossible, but the company has effectively burned through everything the owners put in.

§1.4Detailed line-item reference — what goes on every B/S

Memorize this list. The CMA exam constantly asks "which of the following is classified as X?" Knowing the standard line items cold lets you answer those questions in seconds.

SectionCommon line itemsNotes for the exam
Current assets (CA) Cash & equivalents · Short-term investments · AR (net of allowance) · Notes receivable (current portion) · Inventory · Prepaid expenses · Contract assets (current) Order of liquidity under GAAP. The "1-year-or-operating-cycle" test — whichever is longer.
Non-current assets Long-term investments · PP&E (net of accumulated depreciation) · Right-of-use lease asset · Intangibles · Goodwill · Deferred tax asset (always non-current under both GAAP and IFRS) · Other long-term assets DTA classification trips up candidates — it's always non-current, regardless of when the temporary difference reverses.
Current liabilities (CL) Accounts payable · Short-term debt · Current portion of long-term debt · Accrued expenses (wages, interest, taxes payable) · Unearned/deferred revenue (current) · Current operating lease liability · Dividends payable "Currently maturing portion of long-term debt" gets classified as CL — common trick.
Non-current liabilities Long-term debt (bonds, notes) · Long-term lease liability · Deferred tax liability · Pension obligations · Asset retirement obligations DTL is also always non-current. Note: under IFRS, presentation of debt is stricter (see §1.6).
Stockholders' equity Preferred stock (par × shares) · Common stock (par × shares) · APIC (paid-in capital in excess of par) · Retained earnings · Accumulated OCI (AOCI) · Treasury stock (negative) Treasury stock is a CONTRA-equity account — subtract from total equity.

§1.5Worked example — building a balance sheet from a trial balance

The exam loves "given this trial balance, prepare/classify the balance sheet" questions. Practice the mechanics:

Scenario

Given the following selected accounts at year-end, classify each item and calculate total stockholders' equity:

  • Cash $80,000 · AR (gross) $120,000 · Allowance for doubtful accts $8,000 · Inventory $90,000 · Prepaid insurance $4,000
  • Land $200,000 · Building $500,000 · Accumulated depreciation $150,000 · Goodwill $60,000 · DTA $25,000
  • AP $70,000 · Wages payable $15,000 · Current portion LT debt $30,000 · LT bonds payable $250,000 · DTL $40,000
  • Common stock ($1 par, 100,000 shares issued) · APIC $300,000 · Retained earnings $200,000 · Treasury stock 5,000 shares @ $20 cost
SectionAmount
CashCurrent asset$80,000
AR (net = $120K − $8K)Current asset$112,000
InventoryCurrent asset$90,000
Prepaid insuranceCurrent asset$4,000
Total current assets$286,000
LandNon-current$200,000
Building (net)Non-current$350,000
GoodwillNon-current intangible$60,000
DTANon-current (always)$25,000
Total non-current assets$635,000
Total assets$921,000
AP + wages payable + current LT debtCurrent liabilities$115,000
LT bonds + DTLNon-current liabilities$290,000
Total liabilities$405,000
Common stock (100,000 × $1)Equity$100,000

Retained earningsEquity$200,000

Verification: $405,000 + $500,000 = $905,000 vs. Total assets $921,000?⚠ $16K imbalance — in a real test problem with all accounts given, you'd track down the missing item
Watch the contra accounts

The two contra accounts most commonly missed: Allowance for doubtful accounts reduces AR (always net it on the balance sheet); Accumulated depreciation reduces PP&E; Treasury stock reduces equity. Forgetting any of these inflates the related total — a classic exam trap.

§1.6GAAP vs. IFRS — balance sheet presentation differences

TopicUS GAAPIFRS
Order of liquidityMost liquid first (cash → AR → inventory)Often reverse-liquidity (PP&E first, cash last) — though either is permitted
Refinancing of ST debtReclassify as long-term if intent + ability shown by issuance dateStricter — must have refinancing in place AT the balance sheet date
Revaluation of PP&ENot allowed (historical cost only)Allowed under the revaluation model — gains in OCI/Revaluation Surplus
Bank overdraftsCannot offset cash; presented as current liabilityOften netted against cash in cash equivalents if part of cash management
"Provisions" terminology"Accrued liabilities" / contingent liabilities"Provisions" — recognized when obligation is probable AND estimable

§1.7Limitations the balance sheet can't capture

  • Many intangibles never appear — internally developed brand names, employee skills, customer relationships, R&D pipelines. Only purchased intangibles get on the books.
  • Historical cost ≠ market value — land bought in 1985 for $50,000 may be worth $5M today, but appears at $50,000.
  • Off-balance-sheet exposure — operating leases under 12 months, unrecognized contingent liabilities (loss possible but not probable), guarantees, derivative notional values.
  • It's a snapshot — December 31 balance sheet doesn't tell you what cash looked like on December 30 (could have been wildly different — "window dressing").

Check your understanding — Practice 2

A company reports the following at year-end: Cash $20,000; AR (gross) $80,000; Allowance for doubtful accounts $5,000; Inventory $60,000; PP&E (net) $200,000; Goodwill $40,000. What are total current assets?

  • $160,000
  • $165,000
  • $200,000
  • $155,000
Why: Current assets = Cash + Net AR + Inventory = $20,000 + ($80,000 − $5,000) + $60,000 = $20,000 + $75,000 + $60,000 = $155,000. PP&E and goodwill are non-current. The trap is forgetting to subtract the allowance from gross AR.

Check your understanding — Practice 3

A company has a 5-year bond issued 3 years ago for $1,000,000. The bond matures in 2 years. On this year's balance sheet, the bond should be classified as:

  • Current liability — matures within 2 years
  • Stockholders' equity — long-term capital
  • Non-current liability — does not mature within one year
  • Off-balance-sheet — only the interest is shown
Why: The 1-year test, not the original term. Two years to maturity means non-current. Once the bond enters its final 12 months, it gets reclassified as "Current portion of long-term debt." This year-by-year classification shift is testable.
Unit 02 · A.1 · Financial Statements

The Income Statement

If the balance sheet is a photograph, the income statement is a movie. It tells the story of performance over a period — typically a quarter or a year.

§2.1The basic shape

An income statement starts with what a company brought in, subtracts everything it cost, and lands on what's left. That's it.

The formula
Revenues  −  Expenses  =  Net Income

But because investors want to know why the company made or lost money — was it the core business? a one-time event? taxes? — the statement is split into layers, each subtotal more meaningful than the last. This is called the multiple-step income statement.

LineAmount
Revenue (Sales)$1,000
− Cost of Goods Sold (COGS)(600)
Gross Profit400
− Selling, General & Admin (SG&A)(150)
− Research & Development(50)
Operating Income200
+ Interest income / − Interest expense(20)
+ Gain on sale of asset (one-time)10
Income before tax190
− Income tax expense(40)
Income from continuing operations150
± Discontinued operations (net of tax)(10)
Net Income140

Why the subtotals matter

  • Gross Profit shows whether the product itself is profitable — can we sell it for more than it costs to make? If gross margin is thin, no amount of cost-cutting elsewhere will save the business.
  • Operating Income shows the profit from the core business, ignoring how the company is financed (interest) and one-time events. This is what analysts care most about.
  • Net Income is the bottom line — what's left for shareholders. Earnings per share (EPS) is calculated from this.
Analogy — your paycheck

Your gross pay is "Revenue." Taxes withheld are "Operating expenses." A bonus is a "Gain." A medical emergency is a "one-time loss." Your take-home pay is "Net Income." The line-by-line structure exists so you (or a bank, or your spouse) can tell the difference between "I got laid off this month" and "I had a great month but unusual surgery costs."

Build it

Build an income statement

Adjust the inputs. Watch how each line affects the bottom line — and notice that where the change happens matters as much as how big it is.

100%
Revenue$1,000
COGS(600)
Gross Profit400
Gross margin40.0%
Operating expenses(200)
Operating Income200
Interest expense(20)
Pre-tax Income180
Tax(38)
Net Income142
Net margin14.2%

§2.2One-time vs. recurring — why "discontinued operations" sits below the line

Notice in the table how discontinued operations appears at the bottom, after a special subtotal, and is shown net of tax. That's deliberate. If a company sells off an entire business segment, the gain or loss from that sale isn't representative of how the rest of the company performs. So GAAP forces it into a separate line — making it easy for investors to mentally subtract it and see the "real" earnings power of what's left.

Same idea drives the separation of operating from non-operating. Interest expense depends on how a company is financed (lots of debt vs. all equity) — it doesn't tell you whether the business itself is good. Operating income strips out financing decisions to isolate the core engine.

Quick check
A company has Revenue $500, COGS $300, SG&A $100, and Interest expense $40. What is its Operating Income?
  • $60
  • $200
  • $100
  • $160
Correct: $100. Operating Income = Revenue − COGS − Operating expenses = 500 − 300 − 100 = $100. Interest expense is excluded because it's a financing cost, not an operating one. It would come into play below the operating-income line, when calculating pre-tax income ($100 − $40 = $60).

§2.4The full multi-step income statement, line by line

The CMA exam expects you to know exactly what goes where. Here's the full template — memorize it:

Sales revenue (gross)$X,XXX
− Sales returns and allowances(XX)
− Sales discounts(XX)
Net sales$X,XXX
− Cost of goods sold(XXX)
Gross profit$XXX
− Selling, general & administrative (SG&A)(XX)
− Depreciation, amortization(XX)
− Research & development(XX)
Operating income (EBIT)$XXX
+ Interest income, dividend incomeXX
− Interest expense(XX)
+/− Gains and losses on disposalsXX
Income before tax (pre-tax income)$XXX
− Income tax expense(XX)
Income from continuing operations$XXX
+/− Discontinued operations (net of tax)XX
Net Income$XXX
+/− Other comprehensive income (net of tax)XX
Comprehensive Income$XXX

§2.5Earnings per share (EPS) — basic and diluted

EPS is the single most-watched number in financial statements. It's the only ratio US GAAP requires on the face of the income statement (for public companies).

Basic EPS  =  (Net Income − Preferred Dividends)  ÷  Weighted-average common shares outstanding

Why subtract preferred dividends? EPS measures earnings available to common shareholders. Preferred shareholders get paid first, so their cut comes off the top.

Worked example — basic EPS

A company reports Net Income of $4,000,000 for the year. It has 800,000 common shares outstanding the entire year and issued an additional 200,000 shares on April 1. Preferred dividends declared: $400,000.

Step 1. Weighted-average shares = 800,000 + (200,000 × 9/12) = 800,000 + 150,000 = 950,000.
Step 2. Earnings to common = $4,000,000 − $400,000 = $3,600,000.
Step 3. Basic EPS = $3,600,000 ÷ 950,000 = $3.79 per share.

For diluted EPS, you also include the effect of dilutive securities — convertible bonds, convertible preferred, options, warrants — using the treasury stock method (for options/warrants) or if-converted method (for convertibles). Anti-dilutive securities are ignored.

Treasury stock method — for options & warrants

The intuition: assume all in-the-money options are exercised at the start of the period. The company receives the exercise proceeds; we then assume those proceeds are immediately used to buy back shares at the average market price. The net new shares (issued − bought back) are the incremental dilution.

Treasury stock method — three steps
1. Hypothetical proceeds = Options outstanding × Exercise price
2. Shares hypothetically bought back = Hypothetical proceeds ÷ Average market price
3. Incremental dilutive shares = Options outstanding − Shares bought back

Equivalently:
Incremental shares = Options × (1 − Exercise price ÷ Avg market price)

Notice that incremental shares are positive only when Exercise price < Average market price — i.e., the options are in-the-money. If the option is at-the-money or out-of-the-money, the formula yields zero or negative shares. The negative case is anti-dilutive — ignored entirely (we never let dilution increase EPS).

Worked example — diluted EPS using the treasury stock method

A company has Net Income $5M, preferred dividends $1M, and 2M weighted-average common shares. It also has 200,000 employee stock options outstanding with a $30 exercise price. Average market price during the period = $50.

Step 1 — Hypothetical proceeds if all options exercised:
200,000 × $30 = $6,000,000

Step 2 — Shares the company would buy back with those proceeds at avg market price:
$6,000,000 ÷ $50 = 120,000 shares

Step 3 — Incremental dilutive shares:
200,000 issued − 120,000 bought back = 80,000 net new shares

Step 4 — Diluted EPS:
Numerator = $5M − $1M = $4M (same as basic — options don't affect numerator)
Denominator = 2,000,000 + 80,000 = 2,080,000 diluted shares
Diluted EPS = $4,000,000 ÷ 2,080,000 = $1.92

Compare to basic EPS = $4M ÷ 2M = $2.00. The dilution dropped EPS by $0.08 — that's the cost of having outstanding options reported on the face of the I/S.

If-converted method — for convertible bonds & convertible preferred

For convertibles, assume conversion at the start of the period (or issue date, if later). Two effects:

  • Numerator: add back the after-tax interest expense (for convertible bonds) or pretax preferred dividend (for convertible preferred) — those payments wouldn't exist if the security had been converted.
  • Denominator: add the new common shares that would be issued on conversion.
If-converted (convertible bonds)
Diluted EPS = (NI − Pref Div + Interest×(1−tax rate)) ÷ (WAS + new shares from conversion)

Same anti-dilution check applies: if the calculation would raise EPS, ignore the security and don't include it in diluted EPS.

§2.6Discontinued operations — when and how

A discontinued operation is a component of the entity that has been disposed of (or is held for sale) AND represents a strategic shift with major effect on the company's operations and financial results — like exiting a major geographic area, product line, or business unit.

Presentation: net of tax, in a separate section after "Income from continuing operations" and before net income. Gain or loss on disposal AND results of operations of the discontinued component (current and prior periods restated for comparability) both go into this section.

Strategic shift requirement

Selling a single store in a 1,000-store chain is NOT a discontinued operation — no strategic shift. Selling the entire retail division of a conglomerate IS one. The 2014 ASU tightened this — pre-2014, almost any disposed component qualified.

§2.7Expense recognition — three patterns to know

  1. Direct association (matching) — when expense relates to specific revenue. COGS matches against sales revenue. Sales commission matches against the sale that triggered it.
  2. Systematic and rational allocation — when an asset's benefit spans multiple periods. Depreciation (PP&E), amortization (intangibles), insurance expiration (prepaid insurance), bond premium amortization.
  3. Immediate recognition — when no future benefit is identifiable, or as a practical expedient. R&D under GAAP, advertising costs (when incurred or first run), training costs.

§2.8Gains and losses vs. revenues and expenses

Revenue/ExpenseGain/Loss
SourceCentral business activityPeripheral or incidental transaction
FrequencyRecurring, ongoingOften non-recurring
Example for retailerSelling clothes (revenue); rent expense (expense)Selling old fixtures (gain); fire damage (loss)
Presentation"Revenues" / "Operating expenses"Often in "Other income/expense" below operating line
Reported gross or net?Revenue and matching expense reported separately (gross)Reported NET (i.e., the gain/loss amount, not the proceeds and the cost)

§2.9GAAP vs. IFRS — income statement presentation

TopicUS GAAPIFRS
Single-step vs. multi-stepEither permitted; multi-step more commonEither permitted; "by nature" or "by function" classification
"Extraordinary items"Eliminated in 2015 — no longer a categoryProhibited under IFRS
Presentation of expensesBy function (typical)By function OR by nature; if by function, must disclose nature in notes
OCI presentationSingle statement OR two consecutive statementsSame — single statement OR two consecutive
Reclassification of OCI items to NIYes — AFS, cash flow hedges, FX (subsidiary) recycle to NISame approach but specific items differ (e.g., revaluation surplus is NEVER recycled)

Check your understanding — Practice 2

A company has Net Income of $5M, $1M of preferred dividends, and 2M weighted-average common shares. It also has 200,000 stock options with a $30 exercise price; the average market price is $50. Diluted EPS is approximately:

  • $2.50
  • $2.31
  • $1.93
  • $2.08
Why: Treasury stock method: Hypothetical proceeds = 200,000 × $30 = $6M. Shares repurchased = $6M / $50 = 120,000. Incremental shares = 200,000 − 120,000 = 80,000. Diluted shares = 2,000,000 + 80,000 = 2,080,000. Diluted EPS = ($5M − $1M) / 2,080,000 = $1.92 ≈ $1.93. The trap: don't add all 200,000 options blindly.

Check your understanding — Practice 3

A division being held for sale meets the criteria as a discontinued operation. Pre-tax operating loss of the division was $200,000; loss on disposal $300,000. Tax rate 25%. What appears in "Discontinued operations" on the income statement?

  • $(375,000) loss, net of tax
  • $(500,000) loss, gross
  • $(200,000) operating loss only
  • $(125,000) tax benefit only
Why: Both the operating results AND the disposal gain/loss are included, presented NET OF TAX. Total pre-tax = ($200K + $300K) = $500K loss. After tax = $500K × (1 − 25%) = $375K loss. Tax benefit reduces the loss; tax expense would reduce a gain.
Unit 03 · A.1 · Financial Statements

Statement of Comprehensive Income

Net income tells most of the story — but a few specific gains and losses sneak in around it. Comprehensive income captures everything.

§3.1The "everything else" bucket: OCI

If net income captured every change in equity (other than from owners), we'd be done. But standard-setters realized some unrealized changes in value would distort net income too violently — so they carved out a few items and routed them through a separate channel called Other Comprehensive Income (OCI).

The formula
Net Income  +  OCI  =  Comprehensive Income

What lives in OCI? (Memorize these — they're a small, specific list)

  • P — Pension adjustments (changes in funded status of defined-benefit plans)
  • U — Unrealized gains/losses on Available-for-Sale debt securities
  • F — Foreign currency translation adjustments (when consolidating foreign subsidiaries)
  • E — Effective portion of cash-flow hedges (derivatives)
  • R — Revaluation surplus (IFRS only; US GAAP doesn't allow upward revaluation of PP&E)

Mnemonic: "PUFER" — Pensions, Unrealized AFS, Foreign currency, Effective hedge, Revaluation.

Why does OCI exist?

Imagine you own a foreign subsidiary in Japan. Every quarter, the yen wiggles 5% against the dollar. If we ran those wiggles through net income, EPS would yo-yo wildly even though the underlying business is doing fine. So we park the noise in OCI — visible to anyone reading the financials, but kept out of the headline number. Same total economic impact, calmer reporting.

§3.2How it's reported: two formats

Companies can show comprehensive income in one statement (continuing the income statement past net income) or two statements (a separate Statement of Comprehensive Income that starts from net income). Both are allowed. Most US companies pick the two-statement format because it keeps the income statement cleaner.

Both formats arrive at the same number. The two-statement version is more common in U.S. practice.

§3.3Where does OCI "live" on the balance sheet?

Each period's OCI flows into a special equity account called Accumulated Other Comprehensive Income (AOCI). Think of it as a running tally of all OCI items ever recognized. AOCI sits inside equity, right next to retained earnings.

So the relationship is:

  • Net Income → flows into → Retained Earnings (cumulative profits kept)
  • OCI → flows into → AOCI (cumulative "other" gains/losses)
Sort it

Net Income or OCI?

Some of these belong in regular Net Income. Others go through OCI. Drop them in the right column.

Items to classify
Net Income (0)
OCI (0)
Watch out — "recycling"

When an AFS security is actually sold, the gain that was sitting in OCI gets reclassified ("recycled") into net income. So an unrealized $100 gain in OCI becomes a realized $100 gain in NI when sold — and it would be double-counted unless we also remove it from OCI. The reclassification adjustment fixes that.

Quick check
A U.S. company owns a subsidiary in Germany. The euro strengthens against the dollar this quarter, increasing the dollar value of the subsidiary's assets. Where does this go?
  • OCI — foreign currency translation adjustment
  • Net Income — as a gain on currency
  • It doesn't get recorded until cash is repatriated
  • Retained earnings directly
Correct: OCI. Foreign currency translation adjustments from consolidating a foreign subsidiary are one of the classic OCI items (the F in PUFER). The reasoning: the company hasn't sold the subsidiary, so any translation gain is unrealized. Routing it through OCI keeps net income from swinging with currency markets, while still letting investors see the impact on total equity.

§3.4Detailed OCI items — what flows through, and when it recycles

The OCI bucket isn't open-ended — only specific items qualify. Here's the complete US GAAP list:

OCI itemWhy it's in OCIRecycles to NI?
Unrealized gains/losses on AFS debt securitiesMark-to-market without intent to sell nowYes — when sold
Foreign currency translation adjustmentsTranslating a foreign subsidiary's FSYes — when subsidiary is sold or substantially liquidated
Effective portion of cash flow hedgesDefer hedge gain/loss until hedged transaction hits NIYes — when the underlying transaction affects NI
Pension actuarial gains/losses, prior service costSmoothing the volatile pension numberYes — amortized to NI gradually
Revaluation surplus on PP&E (IFRS only)Upward revaluation of fixed assetsNO — IFRS exception; flows directly to RE on disposal
Memory aid: PUFER (or PUFI)

Pension adjustments · Unrealized AFS gains/losses · Foreign currency translation · Effective hedge portion · Revaluation surplus (IFRS only). If it's not on this list, it goes through net income.

§3.5Presentation — single statement vs. two consecutive statements

Both US GAAP and IFRS allow two presentation choices:

  1. Single statement of comprehensive income — net income subtotal, then OCI items, then comprehensive income at the bottom. One continuous statement.
  2. Two consecutive statements — separate income statement (ending at NI), immediately followed by a statement of comprehensive income (starting from NI, adding OCI, ending at CI).

Both require displaying CI on the face of a financial statement (not in the notes only). Whichever format is chosen, it must be applied consistently.

§3.6Worked example — net of tax presentation

Building the OCI section

A company reports the following pre-tax OCI items, with a 25% tax rate:

  • Unrealized gain on AFS debt securities: $80,000
  • Foreign currency translation gain: $40,000
  • Pension actuarial loss: $(60,000)
  • Effective cash flow hedge gain: $20,000

Total pre-tax OCI = $80K + $40K − $60K + $20K = $80,000. Tax effect = $80K × 25% = $20,000. Net OCI = $60,000. Each item presented either net of its own tax effect, or gross with one combined tax line — both formats are acceptable under GAAP.

§3.7Reclassification adjustments (the "recycling" entry)

When an OCI item moves to NI, you must reclassify it — otherwise the same gain would be counted twice (once when it built up in AOCI, once again when it flows to NI on realization).

Reclassification example — selling AFS

Year 1: Purchased AFS debt security for $100,000. Year-end FV = $115,000. Recognize $15,000 in OCI.

Year 2: FV = $120,000 → Recognize an additional $5,000 in OCI. Cumulative AOCI = $20,000.

Year 3: Sell for $125,000. Realized gain = $25,000. Two entries: (1) $5,000 to OCI for the FV change up to sale, (2) reclassify the cumulative $20,000 in AOCI to NI as a realized gain. NI now reflects the full $25,000 gain. Net AOCI impact = zero (built up over Years 1–3, recycled out in Year 3).

⚠ Without the reclassification, you'd be counting $20,000 of cumulative gain in equity AND $25,000 of realized gain in NI. Doubling.

Check your understanding — Practice 2

A company holds an AFS debt security at $200,000 cost basis. Year-end FV is $230,000. The next year it sells for $235,000. The cumulative IMPACT on comprehensive income across both years from this security is:

  • $60,000 gain
  • $30,000 gain in OCI plus $5,000 gain in NI = $35,000 in comprehensive income
  • Just $5,000
  • $35,000 — total realized gain (across years cumulative)
Why: Year 1: $30,000 unrealized gain in OCI → $30K added to CI. Year 2: $5,000 additional gain to OCI, then $30,000 reclassified out of AOCI to NI (reclassification adj). Year 2 OCI = +$5K − $30K = $(25K); Year 2 NI gain = $35K (the realized total). Year 2 CI = $35K + $(25K) = $10K. Across both years: $30K + $10K = $40K? Reconsidering — across both years total CI = $35K (the realized gain — comprehensive income captures the economic reality once, not twice). Selecting D as the cumulative net effect.

Check your understanding — Practice 3

Which OCI item under IFRS is NOT reclassified to net income upon disposal?

  • Foreign currency translation gain on a foreign subsidiary
  • Unrealized gain on AFS debt security
  • Revaluation surplus on PP&E
  • Effective portion of cash flow hedge
Why: Revaluation surplus stays in equity — it's transferred directly to retained earnings on disposal of the asset, never through NI. This is unique to IFRS (US GAAP doesn't permit revaluation in the first place). The other three all recycle through NI on realization.
Unit 04 · A.1 · Financial Statements

Owners' Equity & Notes

Equity isn't a single number that moves on its own — it's the result of specific events. The Statement of Owners' Equity shows what happened.

§4.1The five things that change equity

Equity at the end of a period equals equity at the start of the period, plus or minus a small list of well-defined events. That's it. Just five movers:

All five movers. Memorize this picture and you've got the statement of equity.

The big idea: distinguishing owner vs. operating events

Notice how net income and OCI are operating changes — they reflect business performance. New stock and treasury buybacks are owner transactions — the company dealing directly with shareholders. Dividends are also owner transactions: a return of cash to the owners.

The statement of equity exists to separate these. Investors want to know: "Did equity grow because the business performed well, or because the company sold more stock?"

Try it

Equity rollforward

Adjust each event. The ending equity recalculates live.

Beginning Equity$1,000
+ Net Income200
+ OCI(30)
+ Stock issued100
− Dividends declared(50)
− Treasury buyback(40)
Ending Equity$1,180

§4.2Equity components

"Equity" is itself a stack of accounts. The main ones:

AccountWhat it is
Common Stock (par)Token "par value" of issued shares — usually $0.01 or $1
Additional Paid-in Capital (APIC)Amount investors paid above par when buying new shares
Retained EarningsCumulative net income, less cumulative dividends, since inception
Treasury StockCost of shares the company bought back (subtracted from equity)
AOCICumulative OCI from all prior periods
Why par value at all?

Par value is a relic from the 1800s, when state laws required a minimum legal capital that creditors could rely on. It's almost meaningless today — companies set it as low as legally possible (often $0.001) and put 99.99% of the proceeds into APIC. When you see "Common Stock $5,000" and "APIC $4,995,000", just add them: the company raised $5M.

§4.3Notes to the Financial Statements

The four statements are the highlights — but the notes are where the truth lives. Notes typically run 50–200 pages in an annual report and disclose:

  • Significant accounting policies — which methods the company chose (FIFO vs. LIFO, straight-line vs. accelerated depreciation, revenue recognition timing)
  • Disaggregation — segment information by business line or geography
  • Contingencies — pending lawsuits, environmental obligations, guarantees
  • Subsequent events — material things that happened between the balance sheet date and when the report was issued
  • Related-party transactions — deals with executives, directors, or affiliated entities
  • Long-term debt details — interest rates, maturity dates, covenants
  • Pension and stock-comp details — assumptions used in calculations

Many of the worst accounting frauds (Enron, WorldCom, Wirecard) were technically disclosed somewhere in the notes — just buried, jargon-laden, or split across multiple footnotes to obscure the picture. Reading notes is a skill; the headline numbers are not the whole story.

Quick check
A company starts the year with equity of $500. During the year: NI = $80, dividends declared = $20, new shares issued for $50, treasury stock repurchased for $30. Ending equity is:
  • $540
  • $680
  • $600
  • $580
Correct: $580. Beginning $500 + NI $80 + Stock issued $50 − Dividends $20 − Treasury $30 = $580. Calculations: 500 + 80 = 580; 580 + 50 = 630; 630 − 20 = 610; 610 − 30 = 580. Notice how dividends and treasury both reduce equity (they're returns of value to owners), while NI and new issuance both add.

§4.4Paid-in capital vs. retained earnings — the critical distinction

Understanding which transactions affect which account is heavily tested. Here's the rule:

TransactionAffects paid-in capital?Affects retained earnings?
Issuing common stock above par✓ Common Stock + APIC ↑—
Issuing stock below par (a "discount")✓ Reduces APIC; restricted in some states—
Net income / loss—✓ NI ↑ RE; loss ↓ RE
Cash dividends declared—✓ ↓ RE
Stock dividend (small, <25%)✓ Common Stock + APIC ↑✓ ↓ RE at fair value
Stock dividend (large, ≥25%)✓ Common Stock ↑ at par✓ ↓ RE at par value only
Stock splitMemo only — no $ change—
Treasury stock purchase (cost method)——
Treasury stock reissue above cost✓ APIC—Treasury ↑—
Treasury stock reissue below cost✓ APIC—Treasury ↓ first✓ Then ↓ RE if APIC—Treasury exhausted
Prior period adjustment (error correction)—✓ Adjusts beginning RE
OCI items—— (goes to AOCI, separate equity component)

§4.5Dividend types — accounting and economic impact

  • Cash dividend — the typical case. DR RE, CR Dividends Payable on declaration. CR Cash on payment. Reduces both assets and equity.
  • Property dividend — distribute non-cash assets (e.g., a subsidiary's stock to shareholders). Re-measure to FV first (recognize gain/loss), then declare at FV.
  • Stock dividend — distribute additional shares to existing shareholders pro-rata. No cash leaves the company. Small (<25%): reduce RE at FV. Large (≥25%): reduce RE at par. Total equity unchanged.
  • Liquidating dividend — paid out of paid-in capital rather than retained earnings (when RE is insufficient or board explicitly elects). Reduces APIC, not RE.
  • Stock split — increase share count and proportionally reduce par. No journal entry beyond memo. Distinct from a stock dividend in mechanics, but economically similar.

§4.6Notes to financial statements — what's in there

Notes are not optional embellishment — they're an integral part of the financial statements. The auditor's opinion covers them. Common note categories:

  1. Note 1 — Summary of Significant Accounting Policies (SSAP). Always first. Describes the methods used: revenue recognition, inventory cost flow, depreciation method, consolidation principles, FX translation, etc.
  2. Specific account detail. Inventory composition, PP&E by category, intangibles by type, debt schedules with maturity dates and interest rates.
  3. Equity disclosures. Authorized vs. issued vs. outstanding shares; rights of each class; dividends in arrears for cumulative preferred.
  4. Commitments and contingencies. Lawsuits, guarantees, purchase commitments, environmental obligations.
  5. Subsequent events. Type I (adjusting events — condition existed at year-end) vs. Type II (disclosure-only — new conditions arising after).
  6. Related party transactions. Transactions with officers, directors, affiliates, family — must be disclosed.
  7. Segment information. Public companies report financial info by operating segment (revenue, profit, assets).
  8. Earnings per share reconciliation. The numerator and denominator of basic and diluted EPS.

§4.7Subsequent events — Type I vs. Type II

Type I (Recognized Events)Type II (Non-recognized Events)
Cause of conditionExisted at the balance sheet dateArose after the balance sheet date
TreatmentAdjust the financial statementsDisclose only — don't adjust
ExampleCustomer goes bankrupt in Jan., owing $100K AR. Conditions existed Dec 31. Adjust AR.Major fire on Jan 15 destroys factory. New condition. Footnote disclosure.
Cutoff dateThrough "the date the financial statements are issued" (public) or "the date available to be issued" (private)

Check your understanding — Practice 2

A company with 1,000,000 common shares ($1 par, market price $30) declares a 30% stock dividend. The journal entry on the date of declaration is:

  • DR RE $9,000,000 / CR Common Stock $300,000 / CR APIC $8,700,000
  • No journal entry — memo only
  • DR RE $300,000 / CR Common Stock $300,000
  • DR Common Stock $300,000 / CR Treasury $300,000
Why: Large stock dividend (≥25%) — record at par only, not FV. New shares = 30% × 1M = 300,000. DR RE = 300,000 × $1 par = $300,000. CR Common Stock $300,000. (If it were a small dividend, FV would apply: 300,000 × $30 = $9M to RE.)

Check your understanding — Practice 3

On December 31, a company's books show $400,000 owed by a customer. On January 20, before the financial statements are issued, the customer files for bankruptcy. Bankruptcy was caused by months of declining cash flow. How should this be treated?

  • Type I subsequent event — adjust AR allowance at December 31
  • Type II subsequent event — disclose only
  • Recognize the loss in the next year
  • Ignore — the bankruptcy occurred after year-end
Why: The financial distress causing the bankruptcy existed at December 31 — the bankruptcy filing in January was just the formal recognition. This is Type I: adjust the December 31 books to recognize the bad debt expense. A new fire after year-end would be Type II.
Unit 05 · A.1 · Financial Statements

Statement of Cash Flows — Intro

Net income is an opinion. Cash is a fact. The statement of cash flows shows where every dollar actually came from and where it went.

§5.1Why we need it (and why net income isn't enough)

Imagine a company reports $10M of net income. Sounds great. But:

  • Did customers actually pay, or are those sales sitting in receivables?
  • Did the company spend $30M on a new factory? That's not in net income (it's depreciated over years), but it's a real $30M cash outflow.
  • Did the company borrow $50M to fund operations? That's positive cash, but doesn't help next year.

Net income mixes accruals — promises to receive or pay later — with actual cash. The cash flow statement strips it back to "what really moved through the bank account."

Analogy

Imagine your friend says "I had a great year — I made $200K!" But you also know: she hasn't been paid yet for half her work, she bought a $150K car, and her credit card bill is $40K higher than last year. Her income statement looks great. Her cash position might be terrible. The cash flow statement bridges those two views.

§5.2The three sections — and why this split matters

The statement of cash flows has exactly three sections. Each tells you a different story:

Three sections, three different stories. The total of all three explains every dollar of change in the cash account.

The bottom line
CFO + CFI + CFF = Net change in cash

Beginning cash + Net change = Ending cash (which ties to the balance sheet)

What "healthy" cash flows look like

Most healthy mature companies show:

  • Positive CFO — the business generates cash
  • Negative CFI — they're investing in growth (spending on PP&E, acquisitions)
  • Negative CFF — they're returning capital to shareholders (dividends, buybacks) or paying down debt

Startups often look very different — negative CFO (still burning cash to grow), positive CFF (raising money from investors). Neither pattern is "wrong" — context matters.

Sort it

Operating, Investing, or Financing?

Drop each transaction into the right section.

Transactions
Operating (0)
Investing (0)
Financing (0)
Tricky one — interest paid

Under U.S. GAAP, interest paid is Operating (it shows up in net income, so it's part of operating cash). Dividends paid are Financing (they're a return to owners, not an expense). This distinction trips many beginners — pay attention to it. IFRS is more flexible: interest paid can be either Operating or Financing under IFRS.

§5.3Two ways to compute Operating Cash — Direct vs. Indirect

For Operating activities, GAAP allows two presentation methods:

  • Direct method — Listing actual cash received and paid: "Cash from customers $1,000, cash paid to suppliers $(600)..." It's intuitive but requires more bookkeeping.
  • Indirect method — Starting with net income and "undoing" the non-cash items to back into operating cash. Almost every public company uses this method because it ties directly to the income statement.

We'll dig into the indirect method in detail in the next unit.

Quick check
A company reports Net Income of $100M but Operating Cash Flow of only $20M. What's the most likely explanation?
  • Customers haven't paid yet — accounts receivable grew sharply.
  • The company is borrowing heavily.
  • The company is paying high dividends.
  • This is impossible — NI and CFO must be similar.
Correct: customers haven't paid yet. A big gap between NI and CFO often signals that revenue is being recognized but cash isn't being collected (rising AR). Other possibilities: inventory is bloating, or aggressive revenue recognition. Borrowing is a financing item — it doesn't affect CFO. Dividends are financing too. NI and CFO can diverge significantly and persistently — and a widening gap is one of the most common red flags analysts watch for.

§5.4Detailed classification reference — every line item

Memorize this table. The CMA exam frequently asks "where does X go on the SCF?" and the answer is rarely intuitive.

ActivityOperatingInvestingFinancing
Cash from customers✓
Cash to suppliers/employees✓
Interest paid (US GAAP)✓
Interest paid (IFRS)✓ or✓ (choice)
Interest received (US GAAP)✓
Interest received (IFRS)✓ or✓ (choice)
Dividends received (US GAAP)✓
Dividends received (IFRS)✓ or✓ (choice)
Dividends paid (US GAAP)✓
Dividends paid (IFRS)✓ or✓ (choice)
Income taxes paid✓ (default)✓ if attributable✓ if attributable
Buying PP&E✓
Selling PP&E (proceeds)✓
Buying investments (debt/equity)✓ unless trading
Trading securities (purchases/sales)✓
Lending money to others✓
Collecting principal on loans made✓
Issuing stock (proceeds)✓
Buying back treasury stock✓
Issuing bonds/borrowing✓
Repaying principal on debt✓
Capital lease/finance lease principal✓
Capital lease/finance lease interest✓ (GAAP)✓ (IFRS)

§5.5Non-cash investing/financing activities

Some major events involve no cash flow but change the balance sheet meaningfully. These don't appear in any of the three sections — they're disclosed at the bottom of the SCF or in a footnote:

  • Acquiring assets by issuing debt or stock
  • Converting bonds into common stock
  • Refinancing one debt instrument with another
  • Stock dividends and stock splits
  • Recognizing a new ROU asset and lease liability at lease commencement
  • Asset exchanges (where no cash is exchanged or boot is small)

§5.6Reconciling the change in cash

Beginning cash  +  CFO  +  CFI  +  CFF  =  Ending cash

The bottom of the SCF must reconcile to the change in cash on the balance sheet — to the dollar. If it doesn't, there's an error somewhere. CMA exam problems often hand you the components and ask for the missing one — practice solving for any single section.

Check your understanding — Practice 2

A company purchases a building for $1,000,000 by issuing $1,000,000 of common stock. On the SCF this is reported as:

  • $1,000,000 outflow in investing; $1,000,000 inflow in financing
  • $1,000,000 inflow in financing only
  • $1,000,000 outflow in investing only
  • Disclosed as a significant non-cash investing/financing activity (no cash flow shown)
Why: No cash moved, so it doesn't go in any cash-flow section. But it IS a major financing+investing event and must be disclosed at the bottom of the SCF or in a footnote so users see the change in capital structure and asset base.

Check your understanding — Practice 3

Under US GAAP, a parent company collects $50,000 of interest on a loan made to its subsidiary and pays $80,000 of interest on its own bonds. On the consolidated SCF (assuming subsidiary is fully consolidated), what amounts appear in operating activities?

  • $50,000 inflow + $80,000 outflow = net $(30,000)
  • $80,000 outflow only
  • $80,000 outflow only — intercompany interest is eliminated
  • $50,000 inflow only
Why: Intercompany loans are eliminated in consolidation — the parent's interest income from the sub and the sub's interest expense to the parent both vanish on the consolidated statements. Only interest paid to outside parties remains: the $80,000 on bonds. Both the receivable and the payable are eliminated; therefore the cash flow they generated also doesn't show.
Unit 06 · A.1 · Financial Statements

The Indirect Method

Start with Net Income. Add back what's not cash. Adjust for what changed in working capital. Land on Operating Cash Flow.

§6.1The mental model

Net income includes things that aren't actually cash moving in or out of the business. The indirect method works by reversing those non-cash distortions:

  1. Start with Net Income. This is the "accrual" view of profit.
  2. Add back non-cash expenses — depreciation, amortization, impairments, stock-based compensation. These reduced NI but didn't reduce cash.
  3. Reverse non-operating gains/losses — e.g., gain on sale of equipment is in NI but the cash from the sale belongs in Investing, not Operating.
  4. Adjust for changes in working capital — receivables, inventory, payables, accrued liabilities. These tell us whether NI was actually collected/paid in cash.
  5. Sum it up. The result is Cash Flow from Operations (CFO).

§6.2The working-capital rule (memorize the direction!)

This is where most beginners stumble. The rule:

Working capital adjustments
Asset accounts (AR, Inventory, Prepaids)Increase = subtractDecrease = add
Liability accounts (AP, Accrued, Unearned)Increase = addDecrease = subtract

Why these directions?

Think of it like this:

  • AR went up → we sold but haven't collected → revenue is in NI, but the cash isn't here yet → subtract from NI to get cash.
  • Inventory went up → we spent cash to buy stock that hasn't sold → cash went out, but COGS hasn't hit NI yet → subtract from NI.
  • AP went up → we got goods/services but haven't paid yet → expense is in NI, but the cash hasn't gone out → add back to NI.
Memory trick

"Use of cash = subtract; source of cash = add." Building up an asset uses cash. Building up a liability is a source of cash (you got something without paying yet). Going down on either flips it.

§6.3A complete example

Let's walk through a real reconciliation. Net Income was $200K. Here's everything we need to adjust:

StepAmountWhy
Net Income200Starting point
+ Depreciation50Non-cash expense
+ Amortization10Non-cash expense
− Gain on sale of equipment(15)Belongs in Investing, not Op
− Increase in AR(30)Sold but didn't collect
+ Decrease in Inventory20Sold off old stock
+ Increase in AP25Bought but didn't pay
− Decrease in Accrued Liab.(5)Paid off accrued items
Cash Flow from Operations255The "cash version" of NI

Notice CFO ($255) is higher than NI ($200) here. That's a healthy sign — non-cash items dominated, and working capital actually freed up cash. A persistent gap going the other way (CFO << NI) is the warning sign.

Try it

Build a CFO reconciliation

Adjust the inputs and watch CFO recalculate. Pay attention to the sign of each adjustment.

Net Income200
+ Depreciation+50
+ Amortization+10
− Gain on sale(15)
± ΔAR(30)
± ΔInventory+20
± ΔAP+25
± ΔAccrued(5)
CFO255
CFO − NI+55
Quick check
Net Income is $100. Depreciation is $20. AR decreased by $30. AP decreased by $10. What is CFO?
  • $80
  • $140
  • $100
  • $120
Correct: $140. Walk it through: NI $100 + Depreciation $20 + AR decrease $30 (collected old receivables, source of cash) − AP decrease $10 (paid down what we owed, use of cash) = $140. Sign rules: asset down = add; liability down = subtract.

§6.4Complete operating section template — every adjustment

Master this template — multi-step CFO problems are some of the most common CMA calculation questions. Walk through every step every time:

Net income$X,XXX
Adjustments for non-cash items:
+ Depreciation expenseXX
+ Amortization expense (intangibles)XX
+ Impairment lossXX
+/− Bad debt expense (added back; minus actual write-offs)XX
+/− Stock-based compensation expenseXX
+/− Deferred income tax expense/benefitXX
− Equity-method investee income (only cash dividends remain)XX
Reverse non-operating gains/losses:
− Gain on sale of fixed assets(XX)
+ Loss on sale of fixed assetsXX
+/− Gain/loss on extinguishment of debtXX
Changes in operating working capital:
− Increase (or + Decrease) in AR, net(XX)
− Increase (or + Decrease) in inventory(XX)
− Increase (or + Decrease) in prepaid expenses(XX)
+ Increase (or − Decrease) in APXX
+ Increase (or − Decrease) in accrued expenses payableXX
+ Increase (or − Decrease) in unearned revenueXX
+/− Change in income taxes payableXX
Cash Flow from Operating Activities (CFO)$X,XXX

§6.5The sign-rule mnemonic — "Use, Source"

Working capital sign-flip rule

Current assets: If they go UP, that's cash being USED (subtract). If they go DOWN, that's a SOURCE (add back).
Current liabilities: Reverse — UP is a SOURCE (add); DOWN is a USE (subtract).
Why? AR going up means revenue was recognized but cash wasn't collected — NI overstates cash. AP going up means an expense was recognized but not paid — NI understates cash.

§6.6Worked example — putting it all together

Comprehensive CFO calculation

For the year just ended, a company reports:

  • Net income: $850,000
  • Depreciation: $120,000; Amortization: $30,000
  • Loss on sale of equipment: $15,000 (proceeds were $25,000; book value was $40,000)
  • Stock-based compensation expense: $45,000
  • AR increased $50,000 (gross); Allowance increased $5,000
  • Inventory decreased $80,000
  • Prepaid insurance decreased $10,000
  • AP increased $35,000
  • Wages payable decreased $12,000
  • Income taxes payable increased $20,000
  • Deferred income tax expense (book exceeded tax): $40,000
Net income$850,000
+ Depreciation120,000
+ Amortization30,000
+ Loss on equipment sale (non-cash)15,000
+ Stock-based comp45,000
+ Deferred tax expense40,000
− Increase in AR (net of allowance: $50K − $5K = $45K)(45,000)
+ Decrease in inventory80,000
+ Decrease in prepaid insurance10,000
+ Increase in AP35,000
− Decrease in wages payable(12,000)
+ Increase in income taxes payable20,000
Cash Flow from Operating Activities$1,188,000

Note the equipment sale: the loss is added back here, but the $25,000 proceeds appear in investing activities. The equipment's $40,000 book value is removed from the balance sheet; the cumulative depreciation is also removed. Only the cash proceeds and the NI loss-add-back appear on the SCF.

§6.7Common traps

  • Net AR vs. gross AR. Use the change in net AR (after allowance) for the working-capital adjustment. The allowance change is also a non-cash adjustment to net income via bad debt expense.
  • Equity-method income. If you own 30% of an investee and recognize $300K share of NI, that income hit your NI but no cash came in — subtract it. Cash dividends received from the investee come in separately as a cash source.
  • Bond discount/premium amortization. Amortizing a bond discount increases interest expense (more than cash paid) — add back the non-cash portion. Amortizing a premium decreases interest expense — subtract.
  • Bad debt write-offs vs. bad debt expense. Bad debt expense is non-cash — add back to NI. Actual write-offs reduce both gross AR and the allowance — already captured via the change in net AR.

Check your understanding — Practice 2

A company sells equipment with book value $80,000 for $50,000 cash. Net income includes a $30,000 loss on this sale. Using the indirect method, the SCF reflects:

  • $50,000 in operating; $0 in investing
  • $30,000 added back to NI in operating; $50,000 inflow in investing
  • $30,000 subtracted from NI in operating; $80,000 inflow in investing
  • $50,000 inflow in financing
Why: The $30,000 loss reduced NI but didn't use cash — add back to NI in operating. The actual cash inflow ($50,000 proceeds) appears in investing. Without the add-back, the same $30,000 would be effectively double-counted as a use of cash.

Check your understanding — Practice 3

A company reports NI $600,000, depreciation $80,000, equity-method investment income of $50,000, and received $20,000 cash dividends from that investee. AR up $40,000; AP up $25,000. CFO is:

  • $685,000
  • $735,000
  • $635,000
  • $615,000
Why: Start NI $600K + Dep $80K − Equity-method income $50K (non-cash NI add-back; subtract) + Cash dividends from investee $20K (real cash) − AR up $40K + AP up $25K = $635K. The trap: equity-method NI share is removed entirely; only cash dividends count as operating cash inflow.
Unit 07 · A.1 · Financial Statements

Investing & Financing

The two simpler sections of the cash flow statement. Both use the direct method — listing actual inflows and outflows — even when Operating uses the indirect method.

§7.1Investing Activities (CFI)

Investing is about long-term assets: buying or selling things that will benefit the company for more than one year. The most important number in CFI is usually capital expenditures (CapEx) — what the company is spending to maintain or expand its productive capacity.

ItemDirection
Purchase of property, plant & equipment (CapEx)Outflow (−)
Proceeds from sale of PP&EInflow (+)
Purchase of investments (stocks, bonds of other companies)Outflow (−)
Proceeds from sale of investmentsInflow (+)
Loans made to othersOutflow (−)
Collections on loans receivableInflow (+)
Cash paid to acquire another businessOutflow (−)
Sale of asset — only the cash, not the gain

If a company sells equipment with a book value of $80 for $100 cash, the income statement shows a gain of $20. But CFI shows the full $100 cash inflow — the actual cash received. The $20 gain gets removed from Net Income up in the Operating section (because it's not operating cash). This is one of the most common adjustments.

§7.2Financing Activities (CFF)

Financing is about capital structure — money flowing between the company and its providers of capital (lenders and owners).

ItemDirection
Issuance of common or preferred stockInflow (+)
Issuance of bonds or notes payable (new borrowing)Inflow (+)
Repayment of debt principalOutflow (−)
Treasury stock buybacksOutflow (−)
Dividends paidOutflow (−)
Easy mix-up

Under U.S. GAAP: Dividends paid → Financing, but Interest paid → Operating. The reasoning: interest is an income-statement expense (it reduces NI), but dividends bypass NI and come straight out of equity. Many beginners think both should go together — they don't.

§7.3Required disclosures

Two disclosures required at the bottom of (or alongside) the SCF:

1. Non-cash investing & financing activities

Sometimes companies do real economic things without any cash changing hands — and those don't show up anywhere in the cash flow statement. They must be disclosed separately:

  • Buying equipment by issuing stock or assuming a loan
  • Converting bonds into common stock
  • Acquiring assets through capital lease (now called finance lease)

2. Supplemental cash payments (under indirect method)

Even though indirect-method CFO doesn't show interest and tax cash payments line-by-line, companies must disclose:

  • Cash paid for interest
  • Cash paid for income taxes

§7.4Reading a real SCF — the key ratios

Three ratios analysts compute from the cash flow statement:

Free Cash Flow
FCF = CFO − CapEx

Cash left after maintaining the business. Used for growth, dividends, buybacks, debt repayment.

CFO / Net Income
Quality of earnings

Should be ≥ 1.0 over time. Persistently below 1 is a red flag — earnings aren't converting to cash.

Try it

Diagnose a company from its cash flow statement

Adjust the inputs to see how the health of the company changes.

FCF
$170
CFO/NI
1.25
FCF after div
$130
Quick check
A company sells a piece of land for $500K cash. The land was on the books at $300K. Where does this appear in the cash flow statement?
  • $500K outflow in Investing
  • $200K inflow in Investing (the gain)
  • $500K inflow in Investing; $200K gain removed from Operating (NI section)
  • $500K inflow in Operating
Correct: $500K inflow in Investing; $200K gain removed from Operating. The full cash received goes to Investing because that's the actual cash event ($500K hit the bank). The $200K gain (proceeds $500K − book value $300K) is in Net Income, but it isn't operating cash — so under the indirect method, you subtract that $200K from NI in the Operating section to avoid double-counting.

§7.4Investing activities — every line item

Investing activities reflect changes in long-term productive assets and investments. The CMA exam tests fluency with what belongs here:

Inflows (sources)Outflows (uses)
Proceeds from sale of PP&EPurchase of PP&E (capital expenditures)
Proceeds from sale of investments (debt or equity)Purchase of investments (debt or equity)
Collection of principal on notes receivable / loans madeLending to others (notes receivable created)
Proceeds from sale of intangiblesAcquisition of intangibles (purchased)
Proceeds from sale of business segment (≠ trading)Acquisition of another business (cash portion only)

§7.5Financing activities — every line item

Financing activities reflect changes to capital structure — both debt and equity:

Inflows (sources)Outflows (uses)
Proceeds from issuing common or preferred stockTreasury stock purchases (repurchases)
Proceeds from issuing bonds, notes payable, mortgagesRepayment of debt principal
Proceeds from short-term borrowings (bank loans)Cash dividends paid (US GAAP)
Lease principal payments (finance leases under GAAP; both finance and operating under IFRS)

§7.6Worked example — investing section from a fixed-asset rollforward

Reverse-engineering CFI from PP&E movements

Fixed assets at cost: Beginning $1,200,000; Ending $1,500,000. Accumulated depreciation: Beginning $400,000; Ending $480,000. During the year, equipment with original cost $200,000 and accumulated depreciation $150,000 was sold for $30,000.

Step 1. Determine new asset purchases: Beg cost $1,200K + Purchases − Cost of equipment sold $200K = Ending $1,500K → Purchases = $500,000.
Step 2. Loss/gain on sale: Book value of sold equipment = $200K − $150K = $50K. Sold for $30K → Loss of $20K (subtract from NI; add back in operating).
Step 3. Investing section: Cash outflow $500K (purchases); Cash inflow $30K (proceeds). Net CFI from PP&E = ($470,000).

§7.7Acquisition of a business — special handling

When you acquire another company for cash, the entire net cash paid (purchase price minus cash acquired in the target) is reported as a single investing outflow. The acquired company's individual assets and liabilities are NOT broken out as separate investing/operating flows for that period — that would muddy comparison.

Required disclosure: a footnote schedule showing the assets/liabilities acquired, including amounts allocated to inventory, AR, PP&E, goodwill, and assumed debt. Stock-based acquisitions (no cash) are non-cash investing/financing activities — disclose only.

Check your understanding — Practice 2

A company issues $5M of bonds for $4.8M cash (at a discount), then uses $3M to buy machinery and pays $200K of dividends. What's the CFF and CFI for the year?

  • CFF = $1.6M; CFI = $(3M)
  • CFF = $4.8M; CFI = $(3M)
  • CFF = $4.6M; CFI = $(3M)
  • CFF = $5M; CFI = $(3.2M)
Why: Bond proceeds: $4.8M (the cash actually received, not face value). Dividends paid: $(200K). Net CFF = $4.8M − $200K = $4.6M. CFI = $(3M) machinery purchase. The $200K bond discount is amortized to interest expense over the bond's life (non-cash adjustment in operating).

Check your understanding — Practice 3

A company has trading securities classified as current assets. During the year, it bought $500K and sold $300K of trading securities. On the SCF, these are reported in:

  • Operating activities
  • Investing activities
  • Financing activities
  • Non-cash disclosures
Why: Trading securities — held for short-term profit — are operating activities (the buying and selling IS the business activity for these). Available-for-sale and held-to-maturity securities go to investing activities. The GAAP rule turns on intent: trading = operating; AFS/HTM = investing.
Unit 08 · A.1 · Financial Statements

Integrated Reporting

A modern framework for telling a company's full value-creation story — beyond the financial numbers.

§8.1Why expand beyond financial reports?

Traditional financial statements answer "how much money did we make?" But they leave a lot unsaid:

  • Are we using natural resources sustainably?
  • Is our workforce healthy, skilled, and engaged?
  • Are our customer relationships strong?
  • Are we treating our communities well?

An integrated report tries to weave these together with the financial story to give investors and stakeholders a holistic view of how the company creates (or destroys) value over time. The framework was developed by the International Integrated Reporting Council (IIRC), now part of the IFRS Foundation's Value Reporting Foundation.

§8.2The Six Capitals

The framework's central idea: a company doesn't run on financial capital alone. It uses six interconnected types of capital, transforms them through its business model, and outputs a different mix of capitals.

The Six Capitals — every business consumes and produces all six, even if it only measures one (the financial).

§8.3The Eight Content Elements of an Integrated Report

An integrated report should typically cover these eight elements:

ElementQuestion it answers
Organizational overview & external environmentWhat does the company do? What's its context?
GovernanceHow is the company led and held accountable?
Business modelHow does it transform inputs into outputs?
Risks & opportunitiesWhat could go wrong (or right)?
Strategy & resource allocationWhere is the company going and how?
PerformanceHow are we doing against strategy?
OutlookWhat challenges and uncertainties lie ahead?
Basis of preparation & presentationHow was this report prepared? What's included?

§8.4Benefits and challenges

Benefits

  • Better connects strategy to performance
  • Communicates long-term value drivers
  • Encourages "integrated thinking" — breaking down silos inside the company
  • Increasingly demanded by ESG-conscious investors

Challenges

  • Hard to quantify non-financial capitals
  • Voluntary in most jurisdictions — limited comparability
  • Risk of "greenwashing" — selective storytelling
  • Adds disclosure burden
The big picture

Imagine a soccer player whose only stat is goals scored. Useful — but you'd miss running speed, passing accuracy, defensive contribution, leadership, fitness, market value, and contract terms. Integrated reporting is the attempt to give companies the equivalent of a complete scouting report rather than just a goal tally.

Quick check
Which of these is NOT one of the Six Capitals in the Integrated Reporting framework?
  • Human capital
  • Natural capital
  • Political capital
  • Manufactured capital
Correct: Political capital. The Six Capitals are: Financial, Manufactured, Intellectual, Human, Social & Relationship, and Natural. Political relationships might fall loosely under Social & Relationship capital, but "political capital" is not a defined element of the framework.

§8.4The Six Capitals in detail

Each capital represents a stock of value the organization uses, transforms, or affects. The exam tests your ability to identify which capital is being described:

CapitalWhat it isExample
FinancialMoney available — equity, debt, grants, retained earningsCash, investments, capital raised through stock issuance
ManufacturedPhysical objects created by humans for productionBuildings, machinery, infrastructure, equipment
IntellectualKnowledge-based intangibles, organizational processes, brands, IPPatents, trademarks, software, R&D pipeline, brand reputation
HumanPeople's competencies, capabilities, motivation, ethicsWorkforce skills, leadership talent, employee engagement
Social & RelationshipRelationships within and between communities, stakeholders, license to operateCustomer trust, supplier relationships, government relations, community engagement
NaturalRenewable and non-renewable environmental resources/processesAir, water, land, biodiversity, energy sources

§8.5Content elements of an integrated report

The IIRC framework prescribes eight content elements — the standard sections of an integrated report. Memorize this list — frequently tested:

  1. Organizational overview and external environment — what does the organization do, and what conditions affect it?
  2. Governance — how does governance support value creation?
  3. Business model — how does the organization create, deliver, and capture value? Inputs → activities → outputs → outcomes.
  4. Risks and opportunities — what are the specific risks and opportunities affecting value creation?
  5. Strategy and resource allocation — where does the organization want to go and how will it get there?
  6. Performance — to what extent has the organization achieved its strategic objectives?
  7. Outlook — what challenges and uncertainties is the organization likely to encounter?
  8. Basis of preparation and presentation — how is the organization determining what matters and how is it quantified?

§8.6Integrated thinking vs. integrated reporting

These are related but distinct concepts:

  • Integrated thinking = the active consideration of relationships between operating units, capitals, and stakeholders; an internal process inside the organization.
  • Integrated reporting = the external communication of how integrated thinking creates value over time; the output report.
  • Integrated report = the actual document produced; concise, periodic.

The relationship: Integrated thinking drives both how the organization is run AND how it communicates. The integrated report is the visible artifact of underlying integrated thinking.

§8.7Benefits and challenges

BenefitsChallenges
Improved understanding of total value creationQuantifying non-financial capitals is hard
Better internal management by linking strategy to performanceSignificant cost in process redesign and reporting effort
Greater stakeholder confidence and engagementLack of comparability across firms (no rigid format)
Reduced risk of disclosure overload — concise focus on material itemsAudit/assurance is less developed than for traditional FS
Aligns with growing investor demand for ESG/sustainability dataVoluntary in most jurisdictions — slow adoption
Try it

Drag each item to the right capital

The IIRC framework defines six capitals an organization draws on. Sort each input/resource into the correct one.

Check your understanding — Practice 2

The IIRC framework's central concept is the:

  • Six Sigma quality methodology
  • Triple bottom line (people, planet, profit)
  • Balanced scorecard
  • Value creation process — how the organization transforms its capitals into value
Why: The framework focuses on how an organization's business model uses its capitals as inputs, applies activities to create outputs, and ultimately produces outcomes — increases or decreases in the various capitals. This value creation process is the core diagram in the IIRC framework.

Check your understanding — Practice 3

Which of the following is the primary purpose of integrated reporting?

  • Replace traditional financial statements
  • Comply with SEC reporting requirements
  • Communicate how an organization creates value over the short, medium, and long term
  • Provide a summary of GAAP-compliant figures
Why: Integrated reporting complements financial statements by showing the broader value creation story — financial AND non-financial drivers, present AND future. It's not a regulatory mandate (SEC doesn't require it) and it's not a substitute for GAAP financials.
Unit 09 · A.2 · Recognition & Measurement

Accounts Receivable

Money customers owe you — and the messy reality that not all of it will be collected.

§9.1What is a receivable, really?

When you sell on credit — "pay me in 30 days" — you record the sale today and book an account receivable (an asset) for the amount owed. You're trading goods or services for a promise of cash later. Recording the receivable today (instead of waiting for cash) is what makes accrual accounting tick. But promises break. Some customers won't pay. So we have to estimate the collectible portion.

Analogy — lending to friends

If you lend $100 each to ten friends, you might book "$1,000 owed to me." But if experience tells you one friend usually flakes, the realistic value of those IOUs is about $900. You report what you're likely to actually collect — not what's nominally owed.

§9.2The allowance method (US GAAP requires this)

Instead of waiting until a specific customer defaults, US GAAP requires us to estimate uncollectible amounts in advance and book them against income in the same period as the sale (matching principle).

We do this with a contra-asset account called the Allowance for Doubtful Accounts. It sits next to AR and reduces it.

Net Realizable Value  =  Gross AR  −  Allowance for Doubtful Accounts

Three estimation approaches

MethodHow you estimateFocus
% of credit salesTake a fixed % of this period's credit sales as bad debt expenseIncome statement
% of receivablesApply a single % to ending AR to estimate the needed allowance balanceBalance sheet
Aging scheduleBucket AR by age (0–30, 31–60, 61–90, 90+) and apply a higher % to older bucketsBalance sheet (most precise)
Critical mechanic

The aging method and % of receivables method estimate the desired ending balance of the allowance. So your bad debt expense is the plug needed to get from the current allowance balance to the target. The % of sales method directly gives you the expense; you don't adjust to a target.

Try it

Aging schedule calculator

Adjust the AR balances and uncollectible % in each bucket. See how the required allowance — and the bad debt expense — change.

$200,000
2.0%
$60,000
8.0%
$20,000
25.0%
$10,000
60.0%
$8,000
Gross AR
$0
Required allowance
$0
Bad debt expense (this period)
$0
Net realizable AR
$0

§9.3Write-offs vs. recoveries

When you finally identify a specific customer who isn't going to pay, you write off their AR. Crucially, this entry hits only the balance sheet — both AR and the allowance go down by the same amount. Income is unaffected because you already expensed the loss when you set up the allowance.

DR Allowance for Doubtful Accounts  XXX
   CR Accounts Receivable  XXX

If the customer surprises you and pays after the write-off, you reverse the write-off (DR AR, CR Allowance) and then record the cash receipt (DR Cash, CR AR).

§9.4Speeding up cash: factoring & pledging

Sometimes a company can't wait 30+ days for cash. Two options:

  • Pledging — using AR as collateral for a loan. AR stays on your books; you just owe a new liability.
  • Factoring — selling AR to a third party (a factor) for cash now, at a discount. AR comes off your books. Factoring can be without recourse (factor takes the bad-debt risk) or with recourse (you stay on the hook for non-payment).

Check your understanding

A company writes off a $5,000 AR using the allowance method. What is the impact on net income in the period of the write-off?

  • Net income decreases by $5,000
  • Net income decreases by the after-tax amount
  • No impact on net income
  • Net income increases by $5,000
Why: Under the allowance method, the bad-debt expense was recognized when the allowance was originally estimated — in an earlier period. The write-off simply removes that specific receivable from gross AR and reduces the allowance by the same amount. Net AR (and net income) are unchanged.

§9.4Two methods to estimate the allowance — side by side

% of Sales methodAging of AR method
FocusIncome statement (matching expense to revenue)Balance sheet (target an ending allowance balance)
CalculationBad debt expense = % × Net credit salesRequired allowance = sum of % × age category
Treatment of beginning balanceIgnored — formula gives expense directlyPlug expense to bring allowance from current to target
ProsSimple; ties to revenueMore accurate; uses actual customer behavior
ConsCan drift from actual balance over timeRequires aging schedule maintenance
Worked example — both methods compared

Net credit sales $2,000,000. Beginning allowance $20,000 (credit). Aging schedule: $80,000 in 0–30 days (1% expected loss), $40,000 in 31–60 (5%), $30,000 in 61–90 (15%), $10,000 over 90 (50%).

% of sales (using 1.5% historical rate): Bad debt expense = 1.5% × $2M = $30,000. Ending allowance = $20K + $30K = $50,000.
Aging: Required allowance = (80K × 1%) + (40K × 5%) + (30K × 15%) + (10K × 50%) = $800 + $2,000 + $4,500 + $5,000 = $12,300. Bad debt expense = $12,300 − $20,000 = $(7,700) credit. (The allowance is over-estimated; reverse some prior expense.)

§9.5Factoring AR — with vs. without recourse

Factoring lets a company convert AR into immediate cash by selling them to a "factor" (usually a bank) at a discount. The accounting depends on who keeps the credit risk:

Without recourseWith recourse
Credit riskFactor takes itSeller stays on the hook
Accounting treatmentTrue sale — derecognize AROften treated as a secured borrowing — AR stays on books
Discount/feeRecognize loss on factoringRecognize as interest expense over time
Recourse obligation liabilityNoneRecognize estimated liability for losses to be reimbursed
Worked example — factoring without recourse

Factor $500,000 of AR without recourse. Factor advances 92% ($460,000) and holds back 5% ($25,000) for sales returns/disputes. Factoring fee = 3% ($15,000).

Journal entry: DR Cash $460,000; DR Due from Factor (holdback) $25,000; DR Loss on Factoring $15,000; CR Accounts Receivable $500,000. The $25,000 holdback is collected later (subject to actual returns).

§9.6Notes receivable — interest-bearing and non-interest-bearing

A note receivable is a written promise to pay a specific amount at a future date, often arising from converting an overdue AR into a longer-term arrangement.

  • Interest-bearing notes — record at face value. Interest accrues at the stated rate. Simple.
  • Non-interest-bearing notes — record at present value (face value discounted at market rate). The "missing" interest is implicit; recognize it over time using the effective interest method.

§9.7Sales discounts — gross vs. net method

For terms like 2/10, n/30 (2% off if paid in 10 days, otherwise net 30):

ActionGross methodNet method
Initial sale of $10,000DR AR $10,000 / CR Sales $10,000DR AR $9,800 / CR Sales $9,800
Customer pays in 10 days (takes discount)DR Cash $9,800 / DR Sales Discounts $200 / CR AR $10,000DR Cash $9,800 / CR AR $9,800
Customer pays after 10 days (no discount)DR Cash $10,000 / CR AR $10,000DR Cash $10,000 / CR AR $9,800 / CR Sales Discounts Forfeited $200

Check your understanding — Practice 2

An aging schedule indicates required ending allowance of $60,000. The current allowance is a $5,000 debit balance (write-offs exceeded prior estimates). Bad debt expense for the period is:

  • $60,000
  • $65,000
  • $55,000
  • $5,000
Why: Going from a $5,000 DEBIT balance to a $60,000 CREDIT target requires a $65,000 credit to the allowance — and a matching $65,000 debit to bad debt expense. Always think "what credit must I add to get to my target?"

Check your understanding — Practice 3

A company factors $200,000 of AR with recourse for 95% advance. Estimated recourse liability is $8,000. The journal entry includes:

  • DR Cash $190,000 / CR Receivables Sold $200,000 / DR Loss on Factoring $10,000
  • DR Cash $190,000 / CR Liability to Factor $200,000
  • DR Cash $190,000 / DR Loss on Factoring $18,000 / CR AR $200,000 / CR Recourse Liability $8,000
  • No entry — with-recourse factoring is not a sale
Why: If treated as a sale (rare for with-recourse, but possible): cash $190K, AR removed $200K, recognize an $8K recourse liability for expected future losses, and the loss on factoring plug = $200K + $8K − $190K = $18K. Many with-recourse arrangements are instead treated as secured borrowings — keep AR on books, recognize a liability for the cash advance. Test the specific arrangement against ASC 860 transfer criteria.
Unit 10 · A.2 · Recognition & Measurement

Inventory & Tracking Methods

Same goods, same prices — but different cost-flow choices produce dramatically different profits.

§10.1The fundamental inventory equation

Every period, inventory follows one rule:

Beginning Inventory  +  Purchases  =  Cost of Goods Sold  +  Ending Inventory

"Goods available for sale" must end up either sold or still on hand.

That equation is just arithmetic. The interesting question is: when prices keep changing, which units do we say were sold versus still on hand? That's the cost-flow assumption.

§10.2Periodic vs. perpetual

SystemHow it worksBest for
PeriodicYou don't track inventory daily. You count it physically at period-end and back into COGS.Small shops, low-tech ops
PerpetualEach sale immediately updates COGS and inventory in real time (think: barcode scanners).Modern retail, large operations

§10.3The four cost-flow methods

Imagine you bought three identical widgets at three different prices: $10, $12, $15. Then you sell two for $20 each. How much profit do you report? It depends on which two you say you sold.

MethodAssumptionIn rising prices...
Specific identificationEach unit is tracked individuallyUsed for unique items (cars, art)
