When the rate dropped from 35% to 21%, every existing DTA and DTL had to be remeasured at the new rate. Companies with large net DTAs took a one-time charge to NI in Q4 2017 (their DTA shrank). Companies with large DTLs got a one-time gain. The cumulative effect ran through that year's income statement.

§20.6Net Operating Losses (NOLs)

An NOL arises when allowable tax deductions exceed taxable income — the company has a "loss" for tax purposes. NOLs can be used to reduce taxes in other years.

Two NOL regimes coexist

Pre-2018 NOLsPost-2017 NOLs
Carryback2 years (refund of prior taxes)None (with limited exceptions)
Carryforward20 years, then expireIndefinite — never expire
Offset limit100% of taxable income80% of taxable income

The 80% limit means even with massive prior losses, a profitable year owes some tax. If 2025 taxable income (before NOL) is $1,000,000 and post-2017 NOLs available are $5,000,000, you can only use $800,000 (80%) of the NOL — leaving $200,000 of taxable income to be taxed at 21% = $42,000 of current tax. The remaining $4,200,000 of NOL carries forward indefinitely.

Accounting for NOLs

An NOL carryforward creates a deferred tax asset — the future tax savings if the loss is used. Recorded at the future-period statutory rate. Future tax benefit = NOL × tax rate.

But — DTAs only have value if the company will have future taxable income to absorb them. Which leads to the next concept...

§20.7Valuation allowance — when DTAs may not be realizable

Under US GAAP, you must reduce a DTA via a valuation allowance if it's more likely than not (>50% probability) that some or all of the DTA won't be realized.

Net DTA  =  Gross DTA  −  Valuation Allowance

Assessing realizability requires weighing positive and negative evidence:

Positive evidenceNegative evidence
History of profitabilityCumulative losses in recent years (strongest negative)
Strong forecasted future incomeExpiring carryforwards under old rules
Backlog of contractsUnsettled litigation that could harm operations
Existing DTL that will reverse and create taxable incomeBrief operating history

The valuation allowance is reassessed each period. If conditions improve, the allowance can be released — creating a tax benefit in that period. This is why some companies report unusually low effective tax rates after years of losses: the DTA they couldn't recognize before now becomes realizable.

IFRS does this differently

IFRS doesn't use a valuation allowance. Instead, you simply don't recognize the DTA at all unless it's "probable" of realization. The end result is similar (only the realizable portion ends up on the books), but the mechanics — and the labels — differ.

§20.8Comprehensive worked example — multi-year DTA/DTL

Setup

A company purchases equipment for $300,000 with a 3-year life, no salvage. Book: straight-line depreciation = $100,000/year. Tax: uses MACRS giving $150,000 / $100,000 / $50,000. Pretax book income (before depreciation) = $400,000 each year. Tax rate = 25%.

Year 1Year 2Year 3
Pretax book income (before dep)$400,000$400,000$400,000
Book depreciation(100,000)(100,000)(100,000)
Pretax book income$300,000$300,000$300,000
Tax depreciation(150,000)(100,000)(50,000)
Taxable income$250,000$300,000$350,000
Current tax (taxable × 25%)$62,500$75,000$87,500
Temp diff origination/(reversal)$50,000 origin$0($50,000) reversal
Change in DTL (= temp diff × 25%)+$12,500$0($12,500)
Deferred tax expense/(benefit)$12,500$0($12,500)
Total tax expense$75,000$75,000$75,000
Effective tax rate (book)25%25%25%

Note how total tax expense is constant at $75K each year (matching book income × statutory rate), even though current tax paid varies. The DTL absorbs the difference. Year 1 journal entry:

DR Income Tax Expense $75,000 / CR Income Taxes Payable $62,500 / CR Deferred Tax Liability $12,500

§20.9Effective tax rate reconciliation

Public companies must disclose a reconciliation from the federal statutory rate (21%) to the effective rate. Common reconciling items:

ItemEffect on ETR
Statutory federal rate21.0%
+ State income taxes (net of federal benefit)+3% to +5%
+ Non-deductible expenses (fines, executive comp > $1M, certain meals)+ varies
− Tax-exempt income (municipal bond interest, life insurance proceeds)− varies
− Foreign income taxed at lower rates (or higher)+/− varies
− R&D credits, foreign tax credits, work opportunity credits− varies
+ Change in valuation allowance+/−
= Effective tax ratevaries

§20.10Intra-period tax allocation

When pretax income spans multiple categories (continuing operations, discontinued operations, OCI), the total tax expense must be allocated to each. Generally:

  1. Compute the tax related to continuing operations first (using standard rules).
  2. Allocate any remaining tax to other items (discontinued ops, OCI components) in proportion to their pretax effect.

This is why each line item — "Discontinued operations, net of tax", "Other comprehensive income, net of tax" — has its own tax effect. The presentation prevents users from misjudging the tax implications of each component.

§20.11Common temporary differences — the master list

ItemDTA or DTL?Why
MACRS vs. straight-line depreciationDTLTax deduction taken faster; book deduction slower → future taxable income
Warranty accrualDTABook expense early; tax deduction when claim paid → future deduction
Bad debt expense (allowance method)DTASame as warranty: book expense early; tax deduction at write-off
Subscription revenue received in advanceDTATax recognizes immediately on receipt; book defers → future book income without tax effect
Installment sales (book recognizes at sale; tax over collection period)DTLTax recognized later; book earlier → future taxable income
Unearned rent (collected in advance)DTATax now (cash basis); book deferred → future deduction
Pension / OPEB liabilityDTABook accrues based on expected; tax deducts cash contribution
Stock-based compensationDTABook expense over vesting; tax deduction at exercise
Net Operating Loss (NOL) carryforwardDTAWill reduce future taxable income

§20.12Common permanent differences — never affect DTA/DTL

  • Tax-exempt interest income (municipal bonds) — book income, no tax
  • Life insurance proceeds on key-person policies — book income, no tax
  • Life insurance premiums on key-person policies — book expense, no tax deduction
  • Fines and penalties — book expense, never deductible
  • Federal income taxes paid — never deductible federally
  • Dividends-received deduction — corporate dividends partially exempt from tax
  • 50% of meals & entertainment — limited deductibility
  • Executive compensation over $1M (public companies) — non-deductible

Check your understanding — Practice 2

A company has pretax book income of $1,000,000. Tax-exempt municipal bond interest = $50,000 (included in book income). Non-deductible fines = $30,000 (deducted in book). Statutory rate = 21%. What is the effective tax rate?

  • 21.0%
  • 20.4%
  • 20.6%
  • 22.6%
Why: Taxable income = $1,000,000 − $50,000 + $30,000 = $980,000. Current tax = $980K × 21% = $205,800. ETR = $205,800 / $1,000,000 (pretax book) = 20.58% ≈ 20.6%. Tax-exempt income lowers ETR; non-deductible expenses raise it.

Check your understanding — Practice 3

A company has $4M of cumulative DTAs from multiple sources, no DTLs. Cumulative pretax losses over the past 3 years total $2M. Future taxable income is uncertain. Under US GAAP, the appropriate treatment is:

  • Recognize $0 DTA — cumulative losses preclude any DTA
  • Recognize $4M DTA at gross — no allowance needed
  • Recognize $2M DTA — half the gross amount
  • Recognize $4M DTA gross + significant valuation allowance — possibly reducing net to $0 or close to it
Why: Cumulative losses are the strongest negative evidence in ASC 740-10-30-21. The DTA is recognized at gross ($4M), but a valuation allowance reduces it to the realizable amount. Without strong positive evidence (existing DTLs reversing, contracts in place), the allowance might fully offset the DTA. The DTA isn't simply omitted — it's gross, then allowance applied.
Foundation F1 · Start Here

What Accounting Is Trying to Do

Before any debits, credits, or statements — the question we're trying to answer with all this machinery.

§F1.1The big idea

A business does things — sells products, hires people, takes loans, buys equipment. Accounting is the discipline of turning those activities into a small set of standardized reports that anyone can read and use to make decisions.

Why is this hard? Because business activity is messy and continuous, but reports have to be discrete and structured. The whole apparatus you're about to learn — accrual rules, debits and credits, financial statements — exists to translate the messy real world into the clean, comparable reports that investors, lenders, regulators, and managers depend on.

Analogy — a fitness tracker

Your fitness tracker takes raw activity (every step, every heartbeat) and turns it into a daily summary (steps, calories, sleep score). Accounting does the same for a business: it ingests every transaction and produces a small number of summary reports. The rules of accounting determine what gets counted as a "step" — and importantly, when.

§F1.2Who reads financial statements? (and what they want)

UserCares most about
Investors (current & potential)Future profits and the value of their stake
Creditors / lendersCan the company pay interest and principal back?
ManagementWhere to allocate resources, what's working, what's not
Employees & unionsJob security, ability to pay benefits and wages
Regulators (SEC, IRS)Compliance, disclosures, taxes owed
Customers & suppliersWill this company still be here in 3 years?

Different users want different things, but the financial statements are general-purpose — they have to serve all of them well enough.

§F1.3Cash basis vs. accrual basis — the most important split

You earn $10,000 in December for work done. Customer pays in January. When did you "earn" $10,000?

Cash basis
Revenue when cash arrives (January). Expenses when cash leaves.
Simple. But makes timing of profit look totally driven by collection patterns.
Accrual basis
Revenue when earned (December). Expenses when incurred, regardless of cash.
More complex but matches economic reality. Required by GAAP and IFRS.
Why accrual is the law for any real company

Cash basis can make a profitable company look broke (just collected slowly) or a money-losing company look great (collected fast on bad sales). Accrual matches revenue with the period it was actually earned, and matches expenses with the revenue they helped generate. That's the matching principle — one of the most important ideas in accounting.

Try it

Cash vs. accrual — see the gap

Adjust each transaction's earned period and cash period. See how the two methods can give very different "profit" numbers for the same business.

$10,000
$3,000
$2,000
December — Cash Basis
Revenue: $0
Expenses: $0
Profit: $0
December — Accrual Basis
Revenue: $0
Expenses: $0
Profit: $0

§F1.4Two rule books: US GAAP and IFRS

Standard-setters issue the rules of accounting. Two main rulebooks govern most of the world:

US GAAPIFRS
Set byFASB (Financial Accounting Standards Board)IASB (International Accounting Standards Board)
Used byU.S. public companies (required by SEC)EU, UK, Australia, Canada, Japan, ~140+ countries
StyleRules-based — specific guidance for many situationsPrinciples-based — general framework, more judgment
CodificationASC (Accounting Standards Codification)IAS / IFRS standards

The CMA exam asks about both. Throughout this guide, we'll flag GAAP/IFRS differences with a yellow callout.

§F1.5Underlying assumptions you'll see referenced everywhere

  • Going concern — we assume the business will continue operating into the foreseeable future. Without this, you couldn't depreciate anything (you'd just liquidate).
  • Periodicity — we slice the continuous life of a business into discrete periods (quarters, years) for reporting.
  • Monetary unit — we report in a stable currency (U.S. companies in $USD), and we ignore inflation in basic statements.
  • Economic entity — the business's books are separate from the owner's personal finances.
  • Historical cost (default) — most assets are recorded at what we paid, not what they're worth today (with many specific exceptions you'll meet).

Check your understanding

A consulting firm finishes a $50,000 project in December but doesn't get paid until February. Under accrual basis, when is the $50,000 revenue recognized?

  • February (when cash is received)
  • December (when the work is completed and earned)
  • Allocated half to each year
  • Whenever the company chooses
Why: Accrual recognizes revenue when it's earned — i.e., when the performance obligation is satisfied — not when cash arrives. The work was done in December, so revenue belongs there. The unpaid amount sits in Accounts Receivable until February.

§F1.4Users of financial statements — direct vs. indirect, internal vs. external

Different users have different needs. The CMA exam tests your ability to identify "which user cares about what":

UserInternal/ExternalDirect/IndirectPrimary need
Owners (shareholders)External (typically)DirectPerformance, dividends, value
ManagementInternalDirectOperating decisions, planning
EmployeesInternalDirectJob security, compensation, pension solvency
Lenders/creditorsExternalDirectLiquidity, ability to pay debt
SuppliersExternalIndirectCustomer's payment ability
CustomersExternalIndirectContinued operations of supplier
Government/tax authoritiesExternalIndirectTax collection, regulatory compliance
Financial analystsExternalIndirectRecommendations to investors
Public/general communityExternalIndirectSocial impact, sustainability

§F1.5Accrual vs. cash basis — full picture

Accrual basisCash basis
Revenue recognized whenEarned (performance obligation satisfied)Cash received
Expense recognized whenIncurred (matching to revenue)Cash paid
Required by GAAP/IFRSYesNo (modified cash basis allowed for some small entities)
Used forMost companies' financial reportingSome small businesses, individual taxpayers, very small entities
Matches revenue with related expense?Yes — matching principleNo

§F1.6Regulatory framework — who sets the rules?

Understanding the standard-setting bodies is heavily tested:

  • FASB (Financial Accounting Standards Board) — Sets US GAAP for public and private companies. Issues Accounting Standards Codification (ASC) and Updates (ASU).
  • IASB (International Accounting Standards Board) — Sets IFRS, used in 140+ countries.
  • SEC (Securities and Exchange Commission) — US federal agency overseeing public company disclosure. Has authority over US GAAP for SEC registrants but delegates standard-setting to FASB.
  • PCAOB (Public Company Accounting Oversight Board) — Sets auditing standards for audits of public companies (post-Sarbanes-Oxley).
  • AICPA (American Institute of CPAs) — Sets auditing standards for non-public companies; sets ethics rules; administers the CPA exam.
  • GASB (Governmental Accounting Standards Board) — Sets standards for state and local governments.
  • IFRS Foundation — Parent of the IASB; oversees IFRS development.

§F1.7Conceptual underpinnings — assumptions and principles

The conceptual framework rests on key assumptions and principles. Memorize these:

ConceptMeaningWhy it matters
Economic entityCompany is separate from its ownersDon't mix personal and business transactions
Going concernEntity will continue operating in foreseeable futureJustifies historical cost (vs. liquidation values)
Periodicity (time period)Long economic life is divided into reporting periodsEnables periodic reporting (annual, quarterly)
Monetary unitTransactions measured in currency; assume stable purchasing powerSets the unit of measurement; ignores inflation
Historical costRecord at original transaction priceVerifiable, objective; less reliable for long-held assets
Revenue recognition (5-step ASC 606)Recognize revenue when performance obligation satisfiedFoundation for income measurement
MatchingMatch expenses to the revenues they helped generateIncome measurement quality
Full disclosureInclude in FS or notes anything that could influence user decisionsNotes integral to FS
MaterialityItems immaterial in amount can be treated practically (expensed, etc.)Allows reasonable practical compromises
Conservatism (prudence)When uncertainty, choose the option that's less likely to overstate income/assetsReduces aggressive accounting

Check your understanding — Practice 2

Which body issues IFRS Standards used by 140+ countries?

  • FASB
  • SEC
  • IASB
  • PCAOB
Why: The IASB (International Accounting Standards Board), under the IFRS Foundation, issues IFRS Standards. FASB issues US GAAP. SEC oversees US public-company disclosure. PCAOB sets US public-company auditing standards. Don't confuse standard-setters with regulatory or oversight bodies.

Check your understanding — Practice 3

A company is being valued. The accountant decides to use the equipment's historical cost rather than its current market value. The supporting assumption is:

  • Going concern
  • Materiality
  • Periodicity
  • Monetary unit
Why: Going concern assumes the entity will continue operating. If we expected liquidation, we'd use liquidation values (what we'd get in a fire sale). Going concern justifies historical cost — we'll use the asset over its life, so its acquisition price is more relevant than current market value.
Foundation F2 · Start Here

Debits & Credits — The Bookkeeping Language

Once you accept that "debit" just means "left side" and "credit" means "right side," everything else falls into place.

§F2.1The double-entry rule

Every transaction touches at least two accounts. The amounts on the debit (left) side must equal the amounts on the credit (right) side. This is what keeps the accounting equation in balance.

Forget what "debit" and "credit" mean in everyday English

When your bank "credits" your account, that's their books — your deposit is a liability to them. In your accounting class, "credit" is just the right side of an entry. There's no inherent "good" or "bad" meaning. Debit = left, Credit = right. That's the whole story.

§F2.2The five account types and their normal balances

Every account in the entire chart of accounts falls into one of five types. Each type has a "normal balance" — the side it sits on most of the time.

TypeNormal balanceIncreased byDecreased by
AssetsDebitDebitCredit
ExpensesDebitDebitCredit
LiabilitiesCreditCreditDebit
EquityCreditCreditDebit
RevenueCreditCreditDebit
Mnemonic — DEAD CLIC

DEAD accounts are increased by Debits: Dividends, Expenses, Assets, Draws.
CLIC accounts are increased by Credits: Capital (Equity), Liabilities, Income (Revenue), Contributions.

§F2.3Why this works — the equation as a balance scale

Remember Assets = Liabilities + Equity. Now look at the chart above. Assets sit on the left side of that equation, and they have debit normal balances. Liabilities and Equity sit on the right side, and they have credit normal balances. Revenue increases equity, so it's a credit. Expenses decrease equity, so they're debits. Everything traces back to keeping the equation in balance.

§F2.4Reading a journal entry

The standard format puts debits first (left, no indent), credits next (indented). Always: total debits = total credits.

Example 1: Buy $5,000 of equipment with cash.

DR  Equipment                 5,000
    CR  Cash                           5,000

Equipment (asset) ↑ via debit. Cash (asset) ↓ via credit. Net effect on assets = $0. Equation still balances.

Example 2: Earn $1,000 of service revenue, customer pays cash.

DR  Cash                        1,000
    CR  Service Revenue       1,000

Cash (asset) ↑ via debit. Revenue (which increases Equity) ↑ via credit. Equation: Assets ↑ $1,000 = Equity ↑ $1,000. Balances.

Sort it

Which side is the normal balance?

Drag each account into the bucket for its normal balance.

Debit normal
0
Credit normal
0

§F2.5Contra accounts — the exceptions that prove the rule

Some accounts intentionally sit on the "wrong" side of their type — these are contra accounts. They reduce another account in the same category.

Contra accountReducesNormal balance
Accumulated DepreciationPP&E (asset)Credit (opposite of asset)
Allowance for Doubtful AccountsAccounts Receivable (asset)Credit
Treasury StockEquityDebit (opposite of equity)
Sales Returns & AllowancesSales RevenueDebit (opposite of revenue)

Check your understanding

A company pays $1,200 cash for one year of insurance coverage starting next month. What's the journal entry today?

  • DR Insurance Expense $1,200 / CR Cash $1,200
  • DR Cash $1,200 / CR Insurance Expense $1,200
  • DR Prepaid Insurance $1,200 / CR Cash $1,200
  • No entry — wait until coverage starts
Why: The coverage hasn't been used yet, so it's not an expense — it's a future benefit you've paid for. That's an asset called Prepaid Insurance. Debit Prepaid Insurance (asset ↑), credit Cash (asset ↓). Each month the coverage runs, you'll record DR Insurance Expense / CR Prepaid Insurance to "use up" $100 of it.

§F2.4The DEAD-CLIC framework

Every account has a "normal balance" (debit or credit) and rules for what increases vs. decreases it:

CategoryMnemonicNormal balanceIncreased byDecreased by
Dividends/DrawingsDEADDebitDebitCredit
ExpensesDEADDebitDebitCredit
AssetsDEADDebitDebitCredit
Cost of Goods (Drawings = sole prop)DEADDebitDebitCredit
Common Stock / CapitalCLICCreditCreditDebit
LiabilitiesCLICCreditCreditDebit
Income/RevenueCLICCreditCreditDebit
Capital (paid-in, retained)CLICCreditCreditDebit

§F2.5Contra accounts — flipping normal balances

Contra accounts have a normal balance opposite to the parent account they offset:

Contra accountOffsetsNormal balancePurpose
Allowance for Doubtful AccountsAccounts Receivable (asset)CreditEstimated uncollectible portion
Accumulated DepreciationPP&E (asset)CreditCumulative depreciation
Sales Returns and AllowancesSales (revenue)DebitReductions of revenue
Sales DiscountsSales (revenue)DebitCash discounts taken by customers
Treasury StockEquityDebitStock repurchased by the company
Discount on Bonds PayableBonds Payable (liability)DebitBond sold below face value

§F2.6T-accounts — visualizing the balances

T-accounts show debits on the left, credits on the right. Working through journal entries this way builds intuition:

Worked example — five transactions, T-accounts

Transactions: (1) Issue stock for $50,000 cash. (2) Buy equipment $30,000 on account. (3) Provide services for $20,000 cash. (4) Pay $5,000 of salaries. (5) Pay $10,000 toward the equipment payable.

Cash: DR $50K, $20K = $70K total; CR $5K, $10K = $15K total → ending balance DR $55K (asset, normal debit)
Common Stock: CR $50K → balance CR $50K (equity, normal credit)
Equipment: DR $30K → balance DR $30K (asset)
Accounts Payable: CR $30K, DR $10K → balance CR $20K (liability)
Service Revenue: CR $20K → balance CR $20K (revenue)
Salaries Expense: DR $5K → balance DR $5K (expense)
Verification: Assets ($55K Cash + $30K Equip = $85K) = Liabilities ($20K AP) + Equity ($50K CS + Net Income $15K) = $85K ✓

§F2.7Why the system works — five centuries of double-entry

Luca Pacioli published the first description of double-entry bookkeeping in 1494. The math hasn't changed: every transaction has equal and opposite effects. This forces:

  • The accounting equation always balances (Assets = Liabilities + Equity) at every moment.
  • Total debits always equal total credits in the system.
  • Self-checking — if your trial balance doesn't balance, you have an error somewhere (though balanced totals don't prove correctness — see Trial Balance discussion).

Check your understanding — Practice 2

Which transaction would increase total assets AND increase total equity?

  • Buying equipment with cash
  • Borrowing from a bank
  • Paying off an account payable
  • Providing services on account (creating AR)
Why: Service on account: DR AR (asset ↑), CR Service Revenue (revenue ↑ → equity ↑ via NI). Both sides increase. Buying equipment is asset-for-asset (no equity change). Borrowing is asset ↑ + liability ↑. Paying AP is asset ↓ + liability ↓.

Check your understanding — Practice 3

Which of the following has a credit normal balance?

  • Treasury Stock
  • Sales Discounts
  • Accumulated Depreciation
  • Prepaid Rent
Why: Accumulated Depreciation is a contra-asset — sits on the asset side of the B/S but reduces it. As a contra to debit-normal Asset, it's credit-normal. Treasury stock is contra-equity (debit-normal). Sales discounts are contra-revenue (debit-normal). Prepaid rent is an asset (debit-normal).
Foundation F3 · Start Here

The Accounting Cycle

The full path from a real-world transaction to a published financial statement — and the adjustments that get done along the way.

§F3.1The eight steps

Every period (typically a quarter or year), accounting systems run through the same sequence:

§F3.2The trial balance — the first sanity check

A trial balance simply lists every account and its current balance. Total debits should equal total credits. If they don't, you have a posting error somewhere. But equal debits and credits don't prove the entries were correct — you could have posted to the wrong accounts and still have a balanced trial balance. It just proves the arithmetic is internally consistent.

§F3.3Adjusting entries — accruals and deferrals

This is the step where accrual accounting actually happens. Cash hits the books when it moves; recognition happens via adjusting entries at period-end.

The other big adjusting entry: depreciation. At year-end, you recognize that long-lived assets have been "used up" a bit:

DR  Depreciation Expense      XXX
    CR  Accumulated Depreciation    XXX

§F3.4Closing entries — temporary vs. permanent accounts

At year-end, after the financial statements are prepared, we close the temporary accounts — bringing their balances to zero so they can start fresh next period.

TypeAccountsGet closed at year-end?
TemporaryRevenues, Expenses, Dividends, Gains, LossesYes — closed to Retained Earnings
Permanent (real)Assets, Liabilities, Equity (incl. Retained Earnings)No — balances roll forward

This is why your balance sheet shows accumulated values (assets you still own, debts you still owe) while your income statement shows just this period's activity (revenues earned, expenses incurred during this year).

Sort it

Temporary or Permanent?

Drag each account into the right bucket. Temporary accounts get reset to zero at year-end via closing entries.

Temporary (closes)
0
Permanent (rolls forward)
0

Check your understanding

In December, a tenant pays you $6,000 cash for January–June rent (6 months). You're a calendar-year company. What's the December 31 adjusting entry?

  • DR Cash $6,000 / CR Rent Revenue $6,000
  • DR Rent Revenue $1,000 / CR Unearned Revenue $1,000
  • No adjusting entry needed — none of the rent has been earned yet
  • DR Unearned Revenue $6,000 / CR Rent Revenue $6,000
Why: When the cash arrived in December, you booked DR Cash $6,000 / CR Unearned Revenue $6,000 (a liability — you owe them tenancy). At Dec 31, none of that rent has been earned yet (the tenant hasn't lived there for any of the period). So no adjusting entry is needed at Dec 31. You'll recognize $1,000/month of revenue starting in January as the months pass. The trap answer is option B — that would be the entry at Jan 31, not Dec 31.

§F3.4The full accounting cycle — 10 steps

  1. Identify and analyze transactions. Source documents (invoices, receipts, bank statements).
  2. Journalize. Record in chronological order in a journal (DR/CR with explanation).
  3. Post to general ledger. Transfer journal entries to individual T-accounts.
  4. Prepare unadjusted trial balance. List all account balances; verify total debits = total credits.
  5. Make adjusting entries. Recognize accruals, deferrals, depreciation, etc.
  6. Prepare adjusted trial balance. Updated account balances after adjustments.
  7. Prepare financial statements. Income statement first (NI flows into RE on B/S), then statement of equity, then B/S, then SCF.
  8. Make closing entries. Zero out temporary accounts (revenues, expenses, dividends) into RE.
  9. Prepare post-closing trial balance. Only permanent accounts; verify books are clean for next period.
  10. Reverse certain adjusting entries (optional). For some accruals, reversing simplifies bookkeeping in the next period.

§F3.5Four types of adjusting entries

TypeWhen appliedCash flow timingExample
Accrued revenueRevenue earned, no cash received yetCash will come laterServices performed but not billed: DR AR / CR Revenue
Accrued expenseExpense incurred, no cash paid yetCash will be paid laterWages owed at year-end: DR Wage Exp / CR Wages Payable
Deferred (unearned) revenueCash received, revenue not yet earnedCash already receivedMagazine subscription: DR Cash / CR Unearned Rev (initially); DR Unearned / CR Rev (as earned)
Deferred (prepaid) expenseCash paid, expense not yet incurredCash already paidInsurance prepaid: DR Prepaid / CR Cash; DR Exp / CR Prepaid (as used)

Plus: Depreciation/amortization (allocate fixed asset cost over time): DR Depreciation Expense / CR Accumulated Depreciation. Estimated bad debts: DR Bad Debt Expense / CR Allowance for Doubtful Accounts.

§F3.6Closing entries — clearing temporary accounts

At year-end, all temporary accounts are closed out into RE. Sequence:

  1. Close revenue accounts to Income Summary: DR Revenues / CR Income Summary
  2. Close expense accounts to Income Summary: DR Income Summary / CR Expenses
  3. Close Income Summary to RE: DR Income Summary (if profit) / CR RE; or reverse for loss
  4. Close Dividends to RE: DR RE / CR Dividends Declared

After closing, all temporary accounts have zero balances, and RE has been updated for the period's NI and dividends. The post-closing trial balance contains only permanent accounts (assets, liabilities, equity).

§F3.7Reversing entries — when and why

Reversing entries (made on the first day of the next period) reverse certain adjusting entries to simplify bookkeeping. Typically reverse:

  • Accrued revenues (DR Revenue / CR AR — reverses the original accrual)
  • Accrued expenses (DR Wages Payable / CR Wage Expense)
  • Sometimes: deferred items if originally recorded in income statement accounts

Reversing entries are optional — they don't change the financial statements. Their purpose is to simplify subsequent transactions: when the cash payment arrives, the bookkeeper just records DR Wages Expense / CR Cash without remembering whether part was previously accrued.

§F3.8Common errors caught at trial balance

  • Slide error: A digit shift (e.g., $1,000 recorded as $100). Difference between debits and credits will be divisible by 9.
  • Transposition error: Two digits flipped (e.g., $54 vs. $45). Also divisible by 9.
  • Posted to wrong side: Difference between DR and CR will be 2× the error amount.
  • Missing entry: Total balances both sides; trial balance won't catch a completely missed transaction.
  • Wrong amount on both sides: Trial balance will balance even though entry is wrong.

Check your understanding — Practice 2

A company has accrued $20,000 of wages at Dec 31 (wages payable). On Jan 5, it pays a $30,000 bi-weekly payroll. WITHOUT reversing entries, the Jan 5 entry is:

  • DR Wages Payable $20,000 / DR Wages Expense $10,000 / CR Cash $30,000
  • DR Wages Expense $30,000 / CR Cash $30,000
  • DR Wages Payable $30,000 / CR Cash $30,000
  • No entry — already accrued
Why: Of the $30,000 payment, $20,000 settles the prior-period accrual (DR Wages Payable). The other $10,000 is current-period expense (DR Wages Expense). With reversing entries, the entire $30K could be debited to Wages Expense for simplicity (the reversal already cleared $20K of the next-period expense).

Check your understanding — Practice 3

Closing entries are made for which purpose?

  • To prepare adjusting entries
  • To balance the trial balance
  • To zero out temporary accounts and update RE
  • To prepare reversing entries
Why: Closing entries clear all temporary accounts (revenues, expenses, dividends) so they begin the next period at zero. The net effect updates Retained Earnings for the period's net income (less dividends). Permanent accounts (assets, liabilities, equity) are not closed — they carry forward.
Foundation F4 · Start Here

Conceptual Framework — Elements & Recognition

The "why" behind the rules — what makes information useful, how items qualify for the financial statements, and how they're measured.

§F4.1Qualitative characteristics — what makes accounting info useful

The FASB and IASB agree on what makes financial information useful. The framework splits these into two tiers.

TierCharacteristicMeans
FundamentalRelevanceCapable of making a difference in decisions (predictive value, confirmatory value, materiality)
Faithful representationComplete, neutral, free from error
EnhancingComparabilityCan compare across companies and across time
VerifiabilityIndependent observers would reach similar conclusions
TimelinessAvailable before it loses relevance
UnderstandabilityReasonably informed user can grasp it

Pervasive constraint: cost-benefit. The benefit of providing information must exceed the cost of producing it. Materiality is a relevance threshold: if leaving it out wouldn't change a user's decision, you don't have to bother.

§F4.2The 10 elements of financial statements (FASB CON 8)

Every line on a financial statement is one of these 10 things. If a number doesn't fit any of these definitions, it shouldn't be there.

ElementQuick definition
AssetsProbable future economic benefits, controlled by the entity
LiabilitiesProbable future sacrifices owed to others
EquityResidual interest = Assets − Liabilities
Investments by ownersIncreases in equity from contributions of capital
Distributions to ownersDecreases in equity from dividends/withdrawals
Comprehensive incomeAll non-owner changes in equity for the period
RevenuesInflows from delivering goods/services (main business)
ExpensesOutflows in earning revenues (main business)
GainsIncreases in equity from peripheral/incidental transactions
LossesDecreases in equity from peripheral/incidental transactions
Revenue vs. gain — what's the difference?

Both increase equity, but revenue comes from the company's main activities (a furniture company selling furniture), while a gain comes from incidental activities (a furniture company selling its old delivery truck). The income statement separates these to give users a clearer picture of "the business."

§F4.3Recognition criteria

Just because something fits an element definition doesn't mean it appears on the books. To be recognized (recorded), an item must:

  1. Meet the definition of an element (asset, liability, etc.)
  2. Be measurable with reasonable reliability — you can put a number on it
  3. Be relevant to users
  4. Be a faithful representation of what it claims to be

This is why a company can't put its own internally generated brand value on the balance sheet — even though it's clearly an "asset" in some sense, it fails the measurability test (no reliable way to value it).

§F4.4Measurement attributes — five ways to put a number on it

AttributeWhat it meansUsed for...
Historical costWhat we paid (original transaction)Most assets — PP&E, inventory by default
Current cost (replacement)What it would cost to replace todayLIFO inventory market valuation
Current market value (Fair value)Exit price in an orderly transaction todayTrading securities, AFS, derivatives
Net realizable valueExpected sale price minus disposal costsInventory at lower-of-cost-or-NRV, AR
Present valueDiscounted future cash flowsLong-term receivables, bonds, lease liabilities, pensions

§F4.5Where the rules come from

You'll see these citations all over CMA materials. A quick map:

  • FASB ASC — Accounting Standards Codification, the single source of US GAAP for non-governmental entities. Organized by topic number (e.g., ASC 606 = revenue, ASC 842 = leases, ASC 326 = credit losses).
  • FASB Concepts Statements (CON) — the conceptual framework underlying GAAP. CON 8 supersedes the older CON statements covering objectives, qualitative characteristics, and elements.
  • IFRS / IAS — IFRS Standards (newer, after 2001) and IAS Standards (older, before 2001). Both currently in force. E.g., IAS 1 = presentation of FS, IFRS 15 = revenue.
  • SEC — for U.S. publicly traded companies, additional disclosure rules under Regulation S-X and Regulation S-K, plus Forms 10-K (annual) and 10-Q (quarterly).

Check your understanding

A company is renowned for its strong brand, but the brand is internally generated (built up over decades). Why isn't this brand on the company's balance sheet?

  • It can't be reliably measured — fails the recognition criteria
  • Brands are not assets under any framework
  • It would create double-counting with goodwill
  • The company chose not to recognize it
Why: An internally built brand probably qualifies as an "asset" by definition (probable future economic benefit, controlled by the entity), but recognition also requires reliable measurement. There's no transaction price for self-built brand value, so no objective way to put a number on it. The same brand purchased in an acquisition would be measured by what was paid — and recognized.

§F4.4The 10 elements of financial statements (FASB CON 6)

ElementDefinition (simplified)
AssetsProbable future economic benefits obtained or controlled by an entity from past transactions
LiabilitiesProbable future sacrifices of economic benefits arising from present obligations from past transactions
EquityResidual interest in assets after deducting liabilities
Investments by ownersIncreases in equity from owner contributions (cash, services, conversion of debt)
Distributions to ownersDecreases in equity from transferring assets/services to owners (dividends)
Comprehensive incomeAll non-owner changes in equity for a period
RevenuesInflows from delivering goods/services constituting central operations
ExpensesOutflows from delivering goods/services constituting central operations
GainsIncreases in equity from peripheral transactions, other than revenues and owner contributions
LossesDecreases in equity from peripheral transactions, other than expenses and owner distributions

§F4.5Recognition criteria — when do we put it on the books?

An item is recognized in the financial statements when it meets ALL of the following:

  1. Definition: meets the definition of one of the 10 elements (asset, liability, etc.).
  2. Measurability: has a relevant attribute measurable with sufficient reliability.
  3. Relevance: the information is capable of making a difference in user decisions.
  4. Reliability: the information is representationally faithful, verifiable, and neutral.

§F4.6Measurement attributes — five ways to value items

AttributeDefinitionUsed for
Historical costAmount paid (or fair value of consideration given) at acquisitionMost PP&E, inventory (with LCNRV cap), HTM debt securities
Current costWhat it would cost to replace the asset todayRare; some inventory under LCM
Net realizable value (NRV)Estimated selling price − costs to complete and sellInventory ceiling under LCNRV; receivables (less allowance)
Fair valuePrice to sell asset (or transfer liability) in an orderly transaction at measurement dateTrading securities, AFS, derivatives, certain investments, business combinations
Present valueDiscounted future cash flowsLong-term receivables, lease liabilities, bonds, pension obligations

§F4.7Qualitative characteristics — what makes information useful

TierCharacteristicWhat it means
FundamentalRelevanceCapable of making a difference (predictive value, confirmatory value, materiality)
Faithful representationComplete, neutral, free from error
EnhancingComparabilityInformation is comparable across companies and over time
VerifiabilityIndependent observers would reach similar conclusions
TimelinessAvailable in time to influence decisions
UnderstandabilityClassified, characterized, and presented clearly
ConstraintCost-benefitBenefits of providing information should exceed costs of providing it

Memory aid: "Fundamental = RF (Relevance, Faithful)." "Enhancing = CVTU (Comparability, Verifiability, Timeliness, Understandability)."

§F4.8Fair value hierarchy (ASC 820)

For items measured at fair value, ASC 820 prescribes a three-level hierarchy:

LevelInputsExamples
Level 1Quoted prices in active markets for IDENTICAL assetsPublic stock, exchange-traded futures
Level 2Observable inputs other than quoted prices in active markets (similar assets, observable rates)OTC swaps, less actively traded bonds, recent market transactions for similar assets
Level 3Unobservable inputs (require management estimation)Private equity investments, complex derivatives, non-traded instruments

Disclosure: companies disclose which level each FV measurement falls into. Level 3 measurements require additional disclosure of valuation techniques and significant unobservable inputs.

§F4.9Underlying assumptions and constraints

ConceptWhat it means
Going concernEntity will continue operating; justifies historical cost
Economic entityBusiness is separate from owners and other entities
PeriodicityLong economic life divided into reporting periods
Monetary unitTransactions measured in stable monetary unit (ignore inflation)
Materiality (constraint)Items immaterial in amount can be treated practically
Conservatism (constraint)Under uncertainty, choose less optimistic option
Cost-benefit (constraint)Benefit of information must exceed cost of providing it

Check your understanding — Practice 2

A company has level 3 fair value inputs. This means:

  • Quoted prices in active markets are available
  • Observable inputs from similar transactions are used
  • Unobservable inputs requiring management estimation are used
  • The asset cannot be measured at fair value
Why: Level 3 = unobservable inputs (no quoted prices, no observable similar transactions). Management uses internal models, historical data, etc. to estimate FV. Level 1 has direct market quotes; Level 2 has observable but indirect inputs. Level 3 measurements carry the highest uncertainty and require the most disclosure.

Check your understanding — Practice 3

Per the FASB Conceptual Framework, the two FUNDAMENTAL qualitative characteristics of useful financial information are:

  • Relevance and faithful representation
  • Comparability and verifiability
  • Reliability and timeliness
  • Materiality and conservatism
Why: Memorize: Fundamental = RELEVANCE + FAITHFUL REPRESENTATION. Enhancing = Comparability, Verifiability, Timeliness, Understandability. The pre-2010 framework used "reliability" but it was replaced by "faithful representation." Materiality is now treated as a component of relevance, and conservatism was demoted to a constraint.
Unit 21 · A.2 · Recognition & Measurement

Leases

In 2019, ASC 842 forced almost every lease onto the balance sheet — fundamentally changing how leverage and asset bases look.

§21.1Why this changed

Pre-2019, "operating leases" were off-balance-sheet. A company could lease $5B of equipment under operating leases and show essentially nothing on its books — just rent expense over time. Investors couldn't see the real obligation.

The FASB fixed this with ASC 842 (and IFRS did the same with IFRS 16). Now almost all leases over 12 months put both an asset and a liability on the lessee's balance sheet.

§21.2Identifying a lease

A contract is (or contains) a lease if it conveys the right to control the use of an identified asset for a period of time in exchange for consideration. "Control" means the lessee gets substantially all the economic benefits AND directs how the asset is used.

§21.3The lessee's two classifications (US GAAP)

If any one of the five criteria below is met, the lease is a finance lease. Otherwise, it's an operating lease.

The five finance-lease tests (ASC 842) — pass any ONE → finance lease
  1. Title transfer — ownership passes to lessee at end of lease
  2. Bargain purchase option — lessee is reasonably certain to exercise
  3. Lease term ≥ 75% of asset's remaining economic life
  4. PV of payments ≥ 90% of asset's fair value
  5. Specialized asset — no alternative use to lessor at end

The 75% / 90% thresholds are guidelines from ASC 842, not bright-line rules — but companies generally treat them as such.

§21.4The big change: BOTH lease types are on B/S now

Whether finance or operating, the lessee records:

  • Right-of-use (ROU) asset — the right to use the leased item
  • Lease liability — the present value of future lease payments

The difference is on the income statement:

Finance leaseOperating lease
Balance sheetROU asset + Lease liabilityROU asset + Lease liability
Income statementTwo pieces: Interest expense + Amortization expenseOne piece: Lease expense (straight-line)
Total expense patternFront-loaded (higher expense in early years)Even (same each year)
Cash flow classificationInterest in CFO; principal in CFFEntire payment in CFO
IFRS is different — single model

Under IFRS 16, the lessee uses one model for all leases (essentially the finance-lease pattern: separate interest + amortization). IFRS abandons the operating-lease distinction entirely for lessees. So a U.S. company applying IFRS will report different income statement patterns than under GAAP.

Try it

ROU asset and liability — present-value calculator

Enter the lease parameters. We compute the lease liability (PV of payments) and the initial ROU asset.

Lease liability (PV)
