§21.5Short-term lease exemption

For leases with a term of 12 months or less (and no purchase option), the lessee can elect to skip the ROU/liability machinery entirely and just expense rent on a straight-line basis. This avoids burying small leases in B/S complexity.

Check your understanding

A company signs a 6-year lease on equipment with a 7-year economic life. PV of payments is $450,000; the equipment's FV is $500,000. There's no purchase option or title transfer. How is this classified under US GAAP?

  • Operating lease — none of the criteria are clearly met
  • Short-term lease — exempt
  • Finance lease — term is 86% of life (≥75%) and PV is 90% of FV
  • Finance lease only because of the PV test
Why: Test 3 (term ≥ 75% of life): 6/7 = 85.7% ✓. Test 4 (PV ≥ 90% of FV): $450,000/$500,000 = 90% exactly ✓. Either one alone would force finance-lease classification — and both are met. Tests 1, 2, and 5 are not met, but only one needs to pass.

§21.6The five classification tests — full text

If any one of these is met, the lease is a Finance Lease (lessee) / Sales-Type Lease (lessor). Otherwise, Operating (lessee) / Operating (lessor):

  1. Title transfers at the end of the lease term.
  2. Bargain purchase option — lessee has option to buy at price significantly below FV.
  3. Lease term ≥ 75% of asset's economic life (don't apply if lease starts in last 25% of life).
  4. PV of lease payments ≥ 90% of asset FV.
  5. Specialized asset with no alternative use to the lessor at end of term.

§21.7Initial measurement — ROU asset and lease liability

At lease commencement, the lessee records (regardless of finance/operating classification):

Lease Liability = PV of remaining lease payments, discounted at the rate implicit in the lease (or lessee's incremental borrowing rate if implicit not determinable)
ROU Asset = Lease Liability + Prepayments − Lease Incentives Received + Initial Direct Costs

§21.8Subsequent accounting — finance vs. operating P&L pattern

Finance LeaseOperating Lease
Lease liability accountingEffective interest method (interest expense + principal reduction)Effective interest method (same — but recharacterized)
ROU asset amortizationStraight-line over lease term (or asset life)Plug — set so total expense (interest + amort) is straight-line
Income statement presentationTwo lines: Interest Expense + AmortizationSingle line: Lease Expense (straight-line)
Total lifetime expenseSame total (cash payments)Same total (cash payments)
PatternFront-loaded — interest highest earlyStraight-line — same expense each year

§21.9Worked example — finance lease

Setup

5-year lease. $50,000 annual payments at year-end. Discount rate = 8%. PV of ordinary annuity factor (5 years, 8%) = 3.99271. Asset transfers at end (test 1 met → finance lease).

Lease Liability at commencement = $50,000 × 3.99271 = $199,635. ROU Asset same = $199,635.

YearBeg liabInterest (8%)PaymentPrincipalEnd liabAmortTotal expense
1$199,635$15,971$50,000$34,029$165,606$39,927$55,898
2$165,606$13,248$50,000$36,752$128,854$39,927$53,175
3$128,854$10,308$50,000$39,692$89,162$39,927$50,235
4$89,162$7,133$50,000$42,867$46,295$39,927$47,060
5$46,295$3,705$50,000$46,295$0$39,927$43,632
Total$50,365$250,000$199,635$199,635$249,000

Note the front-loading: Year 1 expense = $55,898; Year 5 expense = $43,632. The amortization is straight-line ($199,635 / 5 = $39,927) but the interest expense decreases as the liability shrinks.

§21.10Operating lease — same B/S, different P&L

Same lease as a finance lease setup, but classified operating: same ROU asset and lease liability at commencement. The difference: total expense is recognized straight-line.

Total lifetime expense = $250,000 cash. Straight-line = $50,000/year. The "amortization" of the ROU is computed as a plug: Year 1 amortization = $50,000 (lease expense) − $15,971 (interest) = $34,029.

§21.11Lessor accounting — three classifications

The lessor's perspective has three categories under ASC 842:

Lessor classificationWhenTreatment
Sales-type leaseAny of the 5 finance-lease tests is metRecognize sales revenue and COGS at commencement; receivable for PV of payments
Direct financingLessee tests fail BUT (a) PV of payments + 3rd-party guaranteed residual ≥ 90% of FV AND (b) collection probableRecognize receivable; no immediate gross profit; recognize interest income over lease term
Operating leaseDefault — neither sales-type nor direct financingKeep asset on books; recognize lease income straight-line

§21.12IFRS 16 — single model for lessees

IFRS 16 (effective 2019) abandoned the dual-model approach for lessees. Under IFRS:

  • All leases >12 months are accounted for like US GAAP finance leases — separate interest expense and amortization, front-loaded total expense.
  • No "operating lease" category for lessees.
  • Lessor accounting still has finance vs. operating distinction (similar to US GAAP).
  • Two exemptions: short-term leases (≤12 months) and low-value asset leases (e.g., laptops, office furniture <~$5,000).

Practical effect: a US GAAP company reporting an operating lease would have a different income statement under IFRS — front-loaded expense profile. ROU asset and liability values are similar; only the expense pattern differs.

Check your understanding — Practice 2

A 4-year lease has annual payments of $20,000 at year-end. Discount rate is 6%. PV of ordinary annuity factor (4 yrs, 6%) = 3.4651. The initial ROU asset is:

  • $80,000
  • $69,302
  • $73,302
  • $20,000
Why: Lease Liability = $20,000 × 3.4651 = $69,302. ROU = Lease Liability (same, assuming no prepayments or initial direct costs). Both finance and operating leases use this same initial measurement under ASC 842.

Check your understanding — Practice 3

A US GAAP lessee has a 5-year operating lease with total cash payments of $300,000 over 5 years. ROU asset = $250,000 at commencement; discount rate 5%. Year 1 lease expense (income statement) is approximately:

  • $60,000 (straight-line cash)
  • $50,000 (ROU divided by 5)
  • $62,500 (interest plus amortization)
  • $30,000
Why: Operating lease under US GAAP: total expense is straight-line over the lease term = $300,000 / 5 = $60,000/year. The internal split between interest ($250K × 5% = $12,500) and amortization ($60K − $12,500 = $47,500) is a B/S detail; the income statement shows just one line of $60,000.
Unit 22 · A.2 · Recognition & Measurement

Owners' Equity & Retained Earnings

The components of stockholders' equity, why retained earnings is the bridge between income and the balance sheet, and how prior-period adjustments work.

§22.1The components of stockholders' equity

Open any 10-K and the equity section will look like this:

ComponentWhat it is
Common stock (at par)# shares issued × par value (mostly historical/legal)
Preferred stockSenior class of stock with fixed dividend rights
Additional paid-in capital (APIC)Issue price above par — what investors actually paid in excess of legal capital
Retained earningsCumulative net income (less dividends paid) since inception
Accumulated OCI (AOCI)Cumulative other comprehensive income — FX, AFS gains, pension adjustments
Treasury stock (contra)Shares repurchased, sitting in treasury — reduces equity
Non-controlling interestEquity portion held by minority shareholders of a consolidated subsidiary

§22.2The retained earnings roll-forward

RE is the most-asked-about equity line. Its movement each year follows a simple equation:

Beginning RE  +  Net Income  −  Dividends Declared  ±  Adjustments  =  Ending RE

Two important nuances:

  • Dividends declared, not paid, reduce RE. A dividend declaration creates a liability (Dividends Payable) and reduces RE on the declaration date. Cash flows when paid, but RE moved on declaration.
  • Stock dividends reduce RE and increase paid-in capital — they're an internal reshuffle, not a true distribution. Cash dividends and stock dividends both reduce RE; only cash dividends use cash.

§22.3Prior-period adjustments — when you correct an error

If you discover an error in a prior period's financial statements, you don't run it through this year's income statement. Instead, you restate the affected prior periods and adjust the beginning retained earnings of the earliest period presented. Net of tax. The error is "as if it never happened" in the income statement — it just changes the starting RE balance.

Three things that adjust RE without going through this year's NI

1. Prior-period error corrections (net of tax)
2. Some changes in accounting principle (retrospective application) — adjust beginning RE for the cumulative effect
3. Treasury stock losses on resale (when the contributed-capital pool is exhausted)

§22.4Appropriated retained earnings

A board can appropriate a portion of RE — earmark it for a specific purpose (paying off debt, expansion, lawsuits). This doesn't reduce total RE; it just labels a chunk as "set aside." Disclosed in the footnotes. Doesn't actually restrict cash — purely a presentation/governance concept.

Try it

Retained Earnings roll-forward

Walk through a realistic year. Note how dividends declared (not paid) and stock dividends reduce RE.

$5,000,000
$2,000,000
$500,000
$0
$0
Beginning RE (as previously reported)$0
± Prior-period error correction$0
Beginning RE (as restated)$0
+ Net income$0
− Cash dividends declared$0
− Stock dividends$0
Ending RE (12/31)$0

Check your understanding

In 2024, a company discovers it accidentally expensed $500,000 in 2023 that should have been capitalized as inventory. The 2023 books are closed. Tax rate is 25%. What's the correct treatment in 2024?

  • Recognize $500,000 of additional revenue in 2024
  • Add $500,000 to 2024 income
  • Restate 2023; increase beginning 2024 RE by $375,000 (net of tax)
  • Increase beginning 2024 RE by $500,000
Why: Errors don't run through current-year income. The proper treatment is to restate the prior period (2023) and adjust beginning RE for the net-of-tax effect: $500,000 × (1 − 25%) = $375,000 increase to beginning RE. The footnotes will disclose the error and its correction.

§22.4Statement of Retained Earnings — full rollforward

Beginning RE  ±  Prior period adjustments  +  Net Income  −  Cash dividends declared  −  Stock dividends  −  Appropriations  =  Ending RE
  • Beginning RE: from prior year's ending RE, adjusted for any restatements due to error correction or accounting principle change.
  • Prior period adjustments: error corrections and certain mandated changes, net of tax. Adjusts beginning RE; doesn't run through current NI.
  • Net Income: current period's NI from income statement.
  • Cash dividends DECLARED: reduce RE on declaration date, not payment date.
  • Stock dividends: reduce RE (small at FV; large at par).
  • Appropriations: reclassify within RE — doesn't reduce total RE.

§22.5Three categories of accounting changes

TypeTreatmentExample
Change in accounting principleRetrospective application — restate prior periods; adjust beginning RE of earliest period presentedFIFO to weighted-avg inventory; voluntary change to LIFO from FIFO (rare)
Change in accounting estimateProspective only — apply to current and future periods onlyUseful life of equipment; allowance for doubtful accounts; warranty rate
Error correctionRetrospective restatement — adjust prior periods presented; adjust beginning REForgot to record an expense; mathematical error

Memory aid: "PRINCIPLE → restate; ESTIMATE → prospective; ERROR → restate." When an estimate change is "inseparable" from a principle change (e.g., switching depreciation methods), treat as estimate change (prospective).

How to apply a change in estimate — the procedure

"Prospective" sounds simple but the math has a specific shape. The four-step procedure:

  1. Compute the carrying value at the start of the period of change — using the OLD estimate. Don't restate.
  2. Determine the new remaining useful life from that point — total revised life MINUS years already elapsed.
  3. Subtract the (revised) salvage value from the start-of-period carrying value. (If salvage is also revised, use the new estimate.)
  4. Divide by the new remaining life to get the new annual depreciation. Apply it from the period of change forward — never restate prior years.
Formula
New annual depreciation = (CV at start of revision year − Revised salvage) ÷ Revised remaining life
Worked example — useful life shortened

Equipment purchased Jan 2021 for $600,000. Original life 10 years; salvage $0; straight-line. In 2024 (after 3 years of depreciation), management revises the useful life to 6 years total (3 years already elapsed + 3 more).

  • Step 1 — CV at start of 2024: original annual depreciation = $600K ÷ 10 = $60K/year. Three years used = $180K. CV at start of 2024 = $600K − $180K = $420,000.
  • Step 2 — New remaining life: revised total 6 years − 3 elapsed = 3 years.
  • Step 3 — Salvage: $0 (unchanged in this example).
  • Step 4 — New annual depreciation: ($420,000 − $0) ÷ 3 = $140,000/year for 2024, 2025, 2026.

No restatement of 2021–2023; no journal entries to fix prior years. Just apply the new $140K starting in the period the estimate changes. Same procedure handles a revised salvage value (use the new salvage in Step 3) or a revised method (e.g., switching SL → DDB is treated as an estimate change too).

§22.6Appropriations of retained earnings

An appropriation reclassifies a portion of unrestricted RE into "Appropriated RE" — signaling that the board has earmarked it for a specific purpose (planned expansion, contingency reserve, debt covenant compliance). Mechanics:

  • DR Retained Earnings (Unrestricted) / CR Retained Earnings — Appropriated for [purpose]
  • No change to total RE; no change to total equity
  • Reverse the entry when the purpose is achieved or no longer relevant
  • Required by some bond indentures or state statutes (e.g., for sinking fund maintenance)

§22.7Liquidating dividends — when paid out of capital

If a company declares dividends exceeding its retained earnings balance, the excess is treated as a liquidating dividend — a return of capital, not a return on capital. Reduces APIC (paid-in capital), not RE.

Required disclosure: Inform shareholders that part of the dividend represents a return of their original investment (potentially affecting their tax basis). Common in industries with declining operations or wasting assets (mining, oil & gas).

Check your understanding — Practice 2

In 2024, a company changes its inventory method from weighted average to FIFO. Cumulative effect of the change on prior period inventory: increase of $200,000 (pre-tax). Tax rate 25%. The change is reflected by:

  • Recognize $200,000 in 2024 income
  • Recognize $150,000 (after tax) in 2024 income
  • Restate prior periods; adjust beginning 2024 RE by +$150,000 net of tax
  • Disclose only — no adjustment to financial statements
Why: Change in accounting principle = retrospective application. Restate the comparative prior periods presented and adjust beginning RE of the earliest period by the net-of-tax cumulative effect: $200,000 × (1 − 25%) = $150,000 increase to beginning 2024 RE.

Check your understanding — Practice 3

In 2024, a company revises the useful life of its equipment from 10 years to 6 years. The equipment was purchased 3 years ago for $600,000 with no salvage. Treatment is:

  • Retrospective — restate prior years
  • Prospective — recompute depreciation over the new remaining life (6 − 3 = 3 years), divide remaining book value
  • Adjust beginning RE
  • Recognize a one-time charge for the prior periods' under-depreciation
Why: Change in estimate = prospective only. Carrying value at start of 2024 = $600K − ($60K × 3) = $420K. New remaining life = 3 years. New annual depreciation = $420K / 3 = $140K/year going forward. Don't restate prior years.
Unit 23 · A.2 · Recognition & Measurement

Common Stock

The basic unit of corporate ownership — and the surprising amount of legal complexity hiding behind a $0.01 par value.

§22.1Three share counts you must distinguish

TermDefinition
AuthorizedThe maximum number of shares the corporate charter allows the company to issue
IssuedShares actually sold to investors at some point
OutstandingIssued shares currently held by investors (= Issued − Treasury)

Outstanding is what matters for EPS, dividends, and voting. Treasury shares don't vote, don't receive dividends, and aren't counted in EPS denominators.

§22.2Par value vs. no-par stock

Par value is a legal/historical concept. It's a nominal floor below which the company is generally prohibited from issuing shares. Most modern stock has a par of $0.01 or even $0.0001 — purely symbolic. The amount above par paid by investors goes to Additional Paid-in Capital (APIC).

Example: Issue 1,000 shares of $1 par stock for $25 each.

DR  Cash                           25,000
    CR  Common Stock (at par)     1,000   [1,000 sh × $1 par]
    CR  Additional Paid-in Capital   24,000   [the rest]

§22.3Stock issued for non-cash consideration

If a company issues stock in exchange for services, equipment, or another asset, record the transaction at fair value — using whichever is more reliable: the FV of the stock or the FV of what's received.

§22.4Stock splits vs. stock dividends

These are easy to confuse. Both increase share count without raising new cash. The accounting differs.

Stock splitSmall stock dividend (<25%)Large stock dividend (≥25%)
What happensEach share is divided (e.g., 2-for-1 doubles share count, halves price)Each shareholder gets X% more sharesEach shareholder gets X% more shares
Par valueAdjusts proportionally (halves on a 2:1 split)Stays the sameStays the same
RE impactNone — just a memo entryReduce by FV of new sharesReduce by par of new shares
APIC impactNoneIncreased by FV − parNone
Why does the size matter for stock dividends?

A small stock dividend (under 25%) is treated as a notional distribution to shareholders — record at fair value as if you were "paying" them. A large stock dividend behaves more like a stock split — record at par because the share price will adjust to reflect dilution. This is a US-GAAP-specific convention.

Try it

Stock split / dividend impact

Set the action and watch the math: same total value, just sliced differently.

1,000,000
$100
—
New shares outstanding
0
New price per share
$0
Total market cap
$0

Check your understanding

A company has 1,000,000 shares of $1 par common stock outstanding, currently trading at $50. It declares a 10% stock dividend. What's the journal entry?

  • DR Retained Earnings $100,000 / CR Common Stock $100,000
  • DR Cash $5,000,000 / CR Common Stock $5,000,000
  • DR RE $5,000,000 / CR Common Stock $100,000 / CR APIC $4,900,000
  • No entry — stock dividends are memo only
Why: 10% × 1M shares = 100,000 new shares. Small stock dividend (<25%), so record at fair value: 100,000 × $50 = $5,000,000 reduction to RE. The legal capital portion (par) is 100,000 × $1 = $100,000. The remainder ($4.9M) goes to APIC. No cash moves — this is just an internal equity reshuffle.

§23.4Three flavors of common stock

TypeDescriptionIssuance entry
Par-value stockHas a stated par (e.g., $1 par) — minimum legal capital per shareDR Cash; CR Common Stock at par; CR APIC for excess
No-par stockNo par; entire issue price goes to Common StockDR Cash; CR Common Stock at full issue price
Stated-value stockNo-par but board sets a "stated value" — functions like parDR Cash; CR Common Stock at stated value; CR APIC for excess

§23.5Stock issued for non-cash consideration

When stock is issued for property, services, or settlement of debt, record at the more reliably measurable fair value:

  • If the stock is publicly traded → use stock's market price (typically more reliable).
  • If the stock is privately held but the consideration has a known FV → use that.
  • Recognize gain/loss only if the asset/service exchanged has a different FV than the carrying amount.
Worked example — stock for legal services

A startup issues 5,000 shares of $1 par common stock to its lawyer for legal services. The stock is privately held; the lawyer typically charges $200/hour and provided 250 hours of work (FV = $50,000). Journal: DR Legal Services Expense $50,000; CR Common Stock $5,000 (par); CR APIC $45,000.

§23.6Stock subscriptions

Sometimes stock is sold on installment — the buyer signs a subscription agreement, paying part now and the rest over time. Until fully paid, the shares are not issued.

DR Cash (down payment)
DR Subscriptions Receivable (remaining balance)
CR Common Stock Subscribed (par × shares subscribed)
CR APIC (excess)

When fully paid: DR Cash; CR Subscriptions Receivable. Then: DR Common Stock Subscribed; CR Common Stock (move from "subscribed" to "issued"). Subscriptions Receivable is contra-equity (deducted from total equity), not an asset.

§23.7Stock dividends — small vs. large mechanics

Small (< 25%)Large (≥ 25%)
Reduction of RE at:Fair value of sharesPar value only
CR Common Stock at:Par valuePar value
CR APIC for:Excess of FV over par$0 — no APIC effect
RationalePer-share market price largely unaffected by small distributionPer-share price falls in proportion to share increase — like a stock split

§23.8Stock splits — accounting non-event

A stock split (e.g., 2-for-1) increases the share count and proportionally reduces par. Total Common Stock account value is unchanged. Memo entry only — no journal entry. Ending equity values are identical to the pre-split values.

Reverse stock split: opposite — fewer shares, higher par. Same accounting non-event. Companies use reverse splits to lift a low share price (e.g., to maintain stock exchange listing minimums).

Check your understanding — Practice 2

A company has 200,000 shares of $5 par common stock outstanding. It executes a 5-for-1 stock split. After the split, the company has:

  • 1,000,000 shares of $5 par (Common Stock account ↑)
  • 40,000 shares of $25 par
  • 1,000,000 shares of $1 par (Common Stock account unchanged at $1M total)
  • 200,000 shares of $1 par (Common Stock account ↓)
Why: 5-for-1 split: shares × 5; par ÷ 5. New: 200K × 5 = 1,000,000 shares; new par = $5/5 = $1. Total Common Stock value: 1,000,000 × $1 = $1,000,000. Same as before (200,000 × $5 = $1,000,000). Memo entry only — no equity change in dollars.

Check your understanding — Practice 3

Investor subscribes to 10,000 shares of $1 par common stock at $20/share. Pays 30% down. The journal entry on subscription is:

  • DR Cash $60,000 / DR Subscriptions Receivable $140,000 / CR Common Stock Subscribed $10,000 / CR APIC $190,000
  • DR Cash $200,000 / CR Common Stock $10,000 / CR APIC $190,000
  • DR Cash $60,000 / CR Common Stock $60,000
  • No entry until fully paid
Why: 10,000 × $20 = $200K total subscription. 30% down = $60K cash; $140K still receivable. CR Common Stock Subscribed at par × shares = $10,000. APIC = $190,000 (excess). Subscriptions Receivable is a contra-equity account, not a regular receivable.
Unit 24 · A.2 · Recognition & Measurement

Preferred Stock

A senior class of stock with priority over common in dividends and liquidation — and dozens of features that change its economics.

§24.1What "preferred" actually means

Preferred shareholders sit between bondholders and common shareholders. They get paid before common shareholders, but only after creditors. Two main preferences:

  • Dividend preference — preferred dividends must be paid before any common dividend can be paid
  • Liquidation preference — if the company is dissolved, preferred shareholders get their stated liquidation value before common shareholders get anything

§24.2The four big features that modify preferred stock

FeatureWhat it doesFor whom?
CumulativeIf a year's dividend is missed, it accumulates ("dividends in arrears") and must be paid before common can receive a future dividendHelps preferred holders
ParticipatingAfter preferred gets its base rate and common gets a "matching" rate, any extra dividend is shared between both classes pro rataHelps preferred holders
ConvertibleHolder can convert preferred into a fixed # of common sharesHelps preferred holders
Callable (redeemable)Issuer can buy the stock back at a stated call priceHelps the issuer
Dividends in arrears are not a liability

Until the board declares a dividend, no liability exists — even on cumulative preferred. Dividends in arrears must be disclosed in the footnotes but not booked as a liability. Once declared, they become Dividends Payable.

§24.3The dividend allocation puzzle (a CMA classic)

When a company declares a dividend, you may need to allocate it between preferred and common — especially when preferred is cumulative or participating with arrears. The standard approach:

  1. Pay preferred its arrears first (if cumulative)
  2. Pay preferred its current-year dividend
  3. Pay common a matching dividend (same rate as preferred) — only if preferred is participating
  4. Distribute any remainder between common and participating preferred pro rata based on par values
Try it

Allocating a dividend between preferred and common

Toggle the features and watch the allocation change.

$500,000
$2,000,000
6.0%
$5,000,000
1 year
—
—
Step 1 — Preferred arrears$0
Step 2 — Preferred current year$0
Step 3 — Common matching dividend$0
Step 4 — Pro-rata remainder (preferred)$0
Step 4 — Pro-rata remainder (common)$0
Total to preferred$0
Total to common$0

Check your understanding

A company has $1,000,000 of 6% cumulative preferred stock and $5,000,000 of common stock. It missed dividends in 2022 and 2023. In 2024, it declares a $200,000 dividend. How much goes to preferred?

  • $60,000 (current year only)
  • $180,000 (2 arrears + current year = 3 × $60,000)
  • $120,000 (2 years of arrears)
  • $200,000 (everything)
Why: Preferred dividend per year = 6% × $1M = $60,000. Cumulative preferred must be paid all arrears + current year before common can get anything. So preferred gets 3 × $60,000 = $180,000. Common gets the remaining $20,000. (If preferred had been non-cumulative, the missed years would simply be lost — and preferred would only get $60,000 this year.)

§24.4Preferred stock features — the four dimensions

Each preferred stock issue has a unique combination of these features, each affecting dividend rights and accounting:

FeatureVariantEffect
Dividend cumulationCumulativeMissed dividends accumulate as "arrears" — must be paid before common dividends
Non-cumulativeMissed dividends are lost forever
Dividend participationNon-participating (typical)Receives only the stated rate
Fully participatingReceives stated rate, then shares pro-rata with common in additional dividends
Partially participatingStated rate plus additional up to a cap
ConvertibilityConvertibleCan be converted to common stock at a fixed ratio
Non-convertibleCannot be converted
CallabilityCallableIssuer can redeem at a stated price
RedeemabilityMandatorily redeemableMust be redeemed at a fixed date — classified as liability, not equity, under ASC 480

§24.5Worked example — fully participating preferred

Setup

$1,000,000 of 6% cumulative, fully participating preferred stock outstanding ($100 par, 10,000 shares).
$2,000,000 of common stock outstanding ($10 par, 200,000 shares).
Total dividends declared: $300,000. No arrears.

  1. Preferred basic dividend: 6% × $1M = $60,000 → Preferred
  2. Common matching basic dividend (to keep proportional): 6% × $2M = $120,000 → Common
  3. Remaining $300K − $60K − $120K = $120,000 to share pro-rata.
  4. Pro-rata allocation: Preferred share = $1M / ($1M + $2M) = 33.3% × $120K = $40,000. Common share = $2M / $3M = 66.7% × $120K = $80,000

Total: Preferred = $60,000 + $40,000 = $100,000. Common = $120,000 + $80,000 = $200,000.

§24.6Convertible preferred — accounting at conversion

When convertible preferred is converted to common stock, use the book-value method (most common): no gain or loss recognized, book values transfer.

DR Preferred Stock (par)
DR APIC—Preferred
CR Common Stock (par × new shares)
CR APIC—Common (plug to balance)

If the carrying value of preferred is less than the par value of common shares issued, the difference reduces RE. Use of the market value method (recognize gain/loss based on FV) is rarely permitted.

§24.7Mandatorily redeemable preferred — liability classification

If preferred stock has a fixed redemption date and the issuer must redeem at a specified amount, ASC 480 requires classifying it as a liability, not equity. This treatment:

  • Dividends are reported as interest expense on the income statement, not as a deduction from RE.
  • Affects debt-to-equity ratios meaningfully.
  • Optional redemption (callable) does not trigger liability classification — only mandatory.

§24.8EPS impact of preferred dividends

For Basic EPS, subtract preferred dividends declared (or accrued for cumulative preferred even if not declared) from NI:

Basic EPS = (Net Income − Preferred Dividends) ÷ Weighted-avg common shares

For cumulative preferred, subtract the annual dividend whether declared or not. For non-cumulative, subtract only declared dividends. Convertible preferred is included in diluted EPS using the if-converted method when dilutive.

Check your understanding — Practice 2

A company has $500,000 of 5% non-cumulative preferred and $1,500,000 common stock. It missed dividends in Year 1 and 2. In Year 3, declares $50,000 dividends. How much goes to preferred?

  • $75,000 (3 years' worth)
  • $25,000 (current year only)
  • $50,000 (entire dividend)
  • $0 (preferred forfeits when missed)
Why: Non-cumulative — missed dividends are lost. Year 3 preferred = 5% × $500K = $25,000. Common gets remaining $25,000. If preferred had been cumulative, all 3 years × $25,000 = $75,000 would be required first.

Check your understanding — Practice 3

A company has $200,000 of 8% mandatorily redeemable preferred stock. Annual dividends are paid. On the income statement, the dividends should be presented as:

  • Dividend distribution to preferred shareholders (below NI line)
  • Other income/expense in NI
  • Interest expense (above NI line)
  • Reduction to APIC
Why: ASC 480 reclassifies mandatorily redeemable preferred as a liability. Consequently, the "dividends" are economically interest payments and are reported as interest expense above the NI line. This affects pretax income and ETR. Many candidates miss this nuance — for ALL non-mandatorily-redeemable preferred, dividends remain a deduction from RE.
Unit 25 · A.2 · Recognition & Measurement

Treasury Stock & Share Classes

When a company buys back its own shares, where does that money go on the balance sheet — and what happens when it resells them?

§25.1Why companies buy their own stock

  • Boost EPS — fewer shares outstanding = higher EPS at the same NI
  • Signal undervaluation — "we think our stock is cheap"
  • Fund employee stock plans — stock grants, ESOPs, exercises of options
  • Return capital tax-efficiently — buybacks vs. dividends (different tax treatment for shareholders)
  • Defend against takeovers — concentrate voting in friendly hands

Treasury stock is recorded as a contra-equity account — a debit balance that reduces total stockholders' equity.

§25.2Two methods — Cost vs. Par Value

Cost method (most common)Par value method
Treasury stock recorded at...The price paid to repurchasePar value of repurchased shares
Excess over par...Stays in Treasury Stock account (single line)Reduces APIC (and possibly RE if APIC depleted)
On reissue, gain/loss...Goes to APIC — never to NIRecorded as if new issuance
Treasury stock transactions never affect net income

A company can never recognize gain or loss on transactions in its own stock. If treasury shares are reissued above their cost, the difference goes to APIC. If reissued below cost, the deficit comes out of APIC first, then RE if APIC is exhausted. The income statement is untouched either way.

§25.3Cost method walkthrough — three transactions

1. Repurchase 1,000 shares at $30 each:

DR  Treasury Stock     30,000
    CR  Cash                30,000

2. Reissue 500 of those shares at $40 each ($10 above cost):

DR  Cash                    20,000
    CR  Treasury Stock          15,000   [at cost]
    CR  APIC — Treasury     5,000   [the gain]

3. Reissue remaining 500 at $25 each ($5 below cost):

DR  Cash                    12,500
DR  APIC — Treasury      2,500   [the loss — wipes out earlier gain pool]
    CR  Treasury Stock          15,000

§25.4Constructive retirement

If a company buys back shares with no intention of reselling them, it can retire them — removing them from issued share count entirely. This is recorded by reducing common stock, APIC, and possibly RE — there is no treasury stock account, the shares are simply gone.

§25.5Multiple share classes

Many companies (especially tech) issue multiple classes of common stock with different voting rights — a way for founders to keep control even after taking outside investment.

  • Class A — typically the public class with 1 vote per share
  • Class B — typically founder/insider class with 10 votes per share (or more)
  • Class C — sometimes a non-voting class

All classes have equal economic rights to dividends and liquidation proceeds (usually) — the difference is purely in voting power. EPS calculations treat all common shares as a single pool unless specified otherwise.

Check your understanding

A company buys back 1,000 shares of its $1 par stock for $50 each (cost method), then later resells all 1,000 at $35 each. APIC—Treasury balance was $0 before. What's the entry for the reissue?

  • DR Cash $35,000 / CR Treasury $50,000 / CR Loss $15,000
  • DR Cash $35,000 / DR Loss on Treasury $15,000 / CR Treasury $50,000
  • DR Cash $35,000 / DR Retained Earnings $15,000 / CR Treasury $50,000
  • DR Cash $35,000 / CR Treasury $35,000
Why: Treasury stock can never produce a loss on the income statement. The $15,000 deficit on the resale must be absorbed somewhere in equity. APIC—Treasury Stock is empty (no prior gains to absorb the loss), so the deficit goes to Retained Earnings instead. If there had been a prior $15,000 in APIC—Treasury, that would absorb it first.

§25.4Cost method vs. par value method — full mechanics

StepCost MethodPar Value Method
Repurchase sharesDR Treasury Stock at cost; CR CashDR Treasury Stock at par; DR APIC (original); plug to RE if deficit; CR Cash
Reissue above costDR Cash; CR Treasury at cost; CR APIC—TS for excessTreat like a new issuance — DR Cash; CR Treasury at par; CR APIC for excess
Reissue below costDR Cash; CR Treasury at cost; DR APIC—TS first, then RE for deficitTreat like a new issuance below par (rare)
B/S presentationSingle contra-equity line at costCommon Stock and APIC each reduced

Cost method is more common — simpler bookkeeping, especially when the company plans to reissue. Par method is more theoretically pure (treats buyback as retirement) and is used by some firms for permanent retirements.

§25.5Worked example — cost method full lifecycle

Three transactions on $1 par stock

Step 1: Buy back 1,000 shares at $40 each.
DR Treasury Stock $40,000 / CR Cash $40,000

Step 2: Reissue 400 shares at $50 each (above cost).
DR Cash $20,000 / CR Treasury Stock $16,000 (400 × $40 cost) / CR APIC—Treasury Stock $4,000

Step 3: Reissue 600 shares at $30 each (below cost). APIC—TS balance from step 2 = $4,000.
Cost = 600 × $40 = $24,000. Cash received = 600 × $30 = $18,000. Deficit = $6,000.
DR Cash $18,000; DR APIC—TS $4,000 (exhaust the balance); DR RE $2,000 (remaining deficit); CR Treasury Stock $24,000.

§25.6Treasury stock retirement (formal cancellation)

If treasury stock is formally retired (canceled), it can never be reissued. The accounting depends on which method was used:

  • Cost method, retire treasury: DR Common Stock at par; DR APIC—Common (original allocation); CR Treasury at cost; plug deficit to RE.
  • Par method, retire treasury: Treasury was already at par. Just close it: DR Common Stock at par; CR Treasury at par.

§25.7Why companies buy back stock

  • Boost EPS — fewer shares outstanding, same NI → higher EPS. Often perceived as creating value.
  • Return cash to shareholders — alternative to dividends; tax-efficient (only paid by selling shareholders).
  • Anti-takeover defense — increases insider/founder ownership %.
  • Provide shares for employee stock plans without diluting via new issuance.
  • Signaling — management buying back signals belief that stock is undervalued.

§25.8Multiple share classes (dual-class structures)

Some companies (Google, Meta, Snap, NYT) issue multiple share classes with different voting rights, often to preserve founder control:

  • Class A: publicly traded; typically 1 vote per share.
  • Class B: typically held by founders; multiple votes per share (e.g., 10 votes).
  • Class C: often non-voting; may be issued to maintain economic control without voting rights.

Each class is recorded separately in equity. Dividend rights are usually equal economically; voting rights differ.

Check your understanding — Practice 2

A company purchases 5,000 shares of treasury stock at $50 each. Later it retires all 5,000 shares. The original par was $1 with original APIC of $9 per share. Using cost method, retirement entry includes:

  • DR Common Stock $250,000 / CR Treasury $250,000
  • DR Common Stock $5,000 / CR Treasury $5,000
  • DR Treasury $250,000 / CR Common Stock $5,000 / CR APIC $245,000
  • DR Common Stock $5,000 / DR APIC $45,000 / DR RE $200,000 / CR Treasury $250,000
Why: Cost method retirement: remove the share's original equity allocation (par $5K + APIC $45K = $50K) and credit Treasury at cost ($250K). Deficit = $250K − $50K = $200K → debit RE. Cost > original issuance creates a deficit because you bought back at higher prices than you sold — the difference comes out of RE.

Check your understanding — Practice 3

A company has 1M shares of Class A (1 vote) and 200K shares of Class B (10 votes per share). Founder owns 100% of Class B. Founder's voting power is:

  • 67% (2M votes / 3M total)
  • 17% (200K / 1.2M)
  • 20% (200K / 1M)
  • 50% — equal class status
Why: Class B voting power = 200,000 × 10 = 2,000,000 votes. Class A = 1,000,000 × 1 = 1,000,000 votes. Total = 3,000,000 votes. Founder holds 2M of 3M = 66.7% voting control while owning only 200K / 1.2M = 17% of shares economically. This is why dual-class structures are controversial — minority economic ownership wields majority voting power.
Try it

Treasury stock under the cost method — see the equity moves

Adjust each variable to see how a buyback and reissue flow through Treasury Stock, APIC—Treasury, and Retained Earnings. The income statement is never touched.

1,000
$40
500
$50
$0
Treasury Stock (DR)$0
Cash from reissue$0
APIC—Treasury Δ$0
Retained Earnings Δ$0
Net Income impact$0
Reissue exceeds shares held

You can't reissue more shares than you hold in treasury. Reduce the reissue count or buy back more.

Rule: Reissue gain → APIC—Treasury. Reissue loss → drain APIC—Treasury first, then RE for the residual. Net income is never affected.

Unit 26 · A.2 · Recognition & Measurement

Revenue Recognition — The 5-Step Model

Maybe the most important standard you'll learn — ASC 606 / IFRS 15 unified revenue accounting under one global, principle-based framework.

§26.1Why this matters

Before 2018, US GAAP had hundreds of industry-specific revenue rules — software, real estate, telecom, construction, each with its own quirks. Then ASC 606 / IFRS 15 replaced all of them with a single, principle-based, 5-step model. The same logic now applies whether you're a software company, a hospital, an airline, or a bakery.

The core principle

Recognize revenue to depict the transfer of promised goods or services to a customer in an amount that reflects the consideration the entity expects to be entitled to in exchange.

§26.2The 5 steps

§26.3Step-by-step deep dive

Step 1 — Identify the contract

A contract (written, oral, or implied) must meet all five criteria: approved by both parties, identifies rights, identifies payment terms, has commercial substance, and collection is probable. If "probable" can't be established, no contract exists for accounting purposes — wait until cash arrives.

Step 2 — Identify performance obligations

A performance obligation is a distinct good or service. "Distinct" means: (a) the customer can benefit from it on its own or with readily available resources, AND (b) it's separately identifiable in the contract.

Example: a phone company sells you a phone + 24-month service for one bundled price. Phone and service are each distinct (you could buy each separately); the company has TWO performance obligations.

Step 3 — Determine the transaction price

The amount the entity expects to receive. Adjust for:

  • Variable consideration (discounts, rebates, refunds, performance bonuses) — estimate using expected value or most likely amount, whichever better predicts
  • Constraint on variable consideration — only include the amount that's "highly probable" to not reverse
  • Significant financing component — if payment is more than ~1 year deferred, separate out an interest element and recognize it as financing income
  • Non-cash consideration — measure at fair value
  • Consideration payable to customer — generally a reduction of the transaction price

Step 4 — Allocate the price

Allocate based on the relative standalone selling price of each performance obligation. If a SSP isn't directly observable, estimate it (adjusted market assessment, expected cost plus margin, residual approach).

Step 5 — Recognize revenue

Either at a point in time (when control transfers to the customer) or over time. Over-time recognition requires meeting any one of three criteria — covered in detail in Unit 28 (Long-Term Contracts).

Try it

Allocate transaction price by relative SSP

A bundled deal sells for $1,000. Adjust each element's standalone selling price. The transaction price is allocated proportionally.

$1,000
$800
$400
$100
Performance obligationSSPAllocationRecognition timing
Phone$0$0Point in time (delivery)
Service$0$0Over time (24 months)
Accessories$0$0Point in time (delivery)
Total$0$0

§26.4Principal vs. agent — who recognizes what?

If a company arranges for another party to provide goods/services to a customer, is the company a principal (recognize gross revenue) or an agent (recognize only the commission/fee)? The deciding question: does the company control the good/service before transfer to the customer? If yes → principal. If it just facilitates → agent.

This is huge for marketplaces (Uber, Airbnb, eBay) — they generally recognize only their fees, not the full transaction value, because they don't control the underlying service.

§26.5Contract assets and contract liabilities

TermWhat it is
Contract assetYou've satisfied a performance obligation but haven't billed yet (more than just receivable — conditional right)
Accounts receivableUnconditional right to consideration — invoice has been issued
Contract liabilityYou've collected cash (or been billed) before satisfying the obligation. Same idea as "deferred revenue."

Check your understanding

A software company sells a 1-year license + 1 year of customer support for $1,200 total. Standalone, the license sells for $900 and support for $600. How is revenue allocated?

  • $1,200 to license at delivery; nothing to support
  • $720 to license at delivery; $480 to support over 12 months
  • $900 to license; $300 to support
  • $1,200 split evenly
Why: Total SSP = $900 + $600 = $1,500. License share = 900/1500 = 60%, so 60% × $1,200 = $720 (recognized at point-in-time delivery). Support share = 600/1500 = 40%, so 40% × $1,200 = $480 (recognized over 12 months — about $40/month). The bundled price of $1,200 (less than $1,500 standalone) implies a $300 implicit discount, which is allocated proportionally.

§26.4Step 1 deep dive — Identify the contract

A contract under ASC 606 must meet ALL FIVE criteria:

  1. Approved by all parties (in writing, oral, or implied by customary practice).
  2. Each party's rights regarding the goods/services are identifiable.
  3. Payment terms are identifiable.
  4. Has commercial substance (will change risk, timing, or amount of future cash flows).
  5. Collection is probable (consider customer's intent and ability to pay; "probable" = likely under GAAP, ~75–80%).

If criteria not all met, recognize cash received as a liability until criteria become met or the company has no further obligation.

§26.5Step 2 deep dive — Identify performance obligations (POs)

A PO is a promise to transfer a distinct good or service. "Distinct" requires both:

  • Capable of being distinct: Customer can benefit from it on its own or with readily available resources.
  • Distinct in context: The promise is separable from other promises in the contract.

Practical examples:

  • Software license + 1 year of post-contract support → 2 POs (each can be benefited from separately).
  • Bricks + bundled labor for construction → 1 PO (the bricks alone don't give the customer the building; bundled with labor produces the integrated output).
  • Hotel room + breakfast + parking → potentially 3 POs if each is distinct in context. Could be 1 if marketed as "all-inclusive" package.

§26.6Step 3 deep dive — Determine transaction price

Transaction price = expected consideration. Five components to consider:

  1. Variable consideration (rebates, refunds, performance bonuses, royalties) — estimate using either expected value (probability-weighted) or most likely amount. Constrain to amount highly probable to not reverse.
  2. Significant financing component — if payment timing differs from transfer by more than 12 months, separate the financing element (interest income/expense over the period).
  3. Non-cash consideration — measure at FV at contract inception.
  4. Consideration paid TO the customer (slotting fees, marketing payments) — generally treat as a reduction of revenue, not as expense.
  5. Sales tax collected — exclude (it's a pass-through to government).

§26.7Step 4 deep dive — Allocate transaction price

Allocate based on relative standalone selling prices (SSP):

  • Use observable SSP if available (the price charged when sold separately to similar customers).
  • If not observable, estimate using:
    1. Adjusted market assessment — what would the market charge?
    2. Expected cost plus a margin — your costs plus a typical markup.
    3. Residual approach — total transaction price minus observable SSPs of other components (only when SSP is highly variable or uncertain for one component).

§26.8Step 5 deep dive — Recognize revenue (point in time vs. over time)

Test (any one suffices for over-time recognition)Example
Customer simultaneously receives and consumes the benefitsCleaning services, IT support, payroll processing
Performance creates/enhances an asset the customer controls during creationBuilding a custom factory on customer's land
No alternative use AND enforceable right to payment for performance to dateHighly customized product (e.g., custom software for one client only)

If none of the over-time tests is met, recognize at a point in time — when control transfers (typically with possession, legal title, customer acceptance).

§26.9Principal vs. agent

Critical for marketplaces, resellers, and platform businesses. Determines whether to recognize revenue gross (full price) or net (commission only):

You're the Principal (gross)You're the Agent (net)
Control the good/service before transfer to customerDon't control; just facilitate
Inventory risk before transferNo inventory risk
Discretion over pricingPricing set by another party
Primary responsibility for fulfillmentOther party fulfills
Examples: traditional retailer, manufacturerExamples: online marketplace (eBay), travel booking site

§26.10Contract modifications

When a contract is modified mid-stream, three accounting treatments are possible:

  1. Treat as a separate contract if (a) added goods/services are distinct AND (b) priced at SSP. Existing contract continues; new contract starts.
  2. Termination + new contract if added goods/services are distinct but NOT priced at SSP. Stop recognizing original; combine remaining + new at adjusted prices.
  3. Cumulative catch-up adjustment if added goods/services are NOT distinct from existing. Treat as part of original contract; recognize cumulative adjustment to revenue based on new total.

§26.11Contract assets vs. contract liabilities

  • Contract asset = company has performed; consideration not yet billed/due. (NOT receivable yet — receivable requires billing.)
  • Receivable = unconditional right to payment (billed; just waiting for cash).
  • Contract liability = company received cash before performance (deferred revenue).

Check your understanding — Practice 2

An online platform takes orders for restaurant meals. Customer pays $50; restaurant receives $42; platform keeps $8 commission. The platform doesn't take inventory risk and doesn't set prices. Revenue to the platform is:

  • $50 — gross revenue
  • $42 — net of commission
  • $8 — agent commission only (net basis)
  • $0 — until customer receives meal
Why: The platform is an agent — it doesn't control the meal before transfer. Revenue = $8 commission only (net basis). Most marketplaces (Uber, Airbnb, eBay, GrubHub) recognize commissions, not the underlying transaction values. Failing this test means substantially overstating revenue.

Check your understanding — Practice 3

A company sells a 3-year service contract for $36,000 paid upfront. Service is provided continuously. At year 1 end, what's the contract liability balance?

  • $24,000
  • $12,000
  • $36,000
  • $0 — fully earned at sale
Why: Recognize $12K revenue per year (straight-line). At end of Year 1: revenue recognized $12K, deferred (contract liability) = $36K − $12K = $24K. By end of Year 2: $12K liability remains. By end of Year 3: $0. The liability gradually moves to revenue as service is provided.
Unit 27 · A.2 · Recognition & Measurement

Right of Return & Consigned Goods

Two situations where revenue recognition gets tricky: when the customer can give goods back, and when goods sit on someone else's shelves but you still own them.

§27.1Sales with a right of return

Almost every retailer offers some kind of return policy. Under ASC 606, the return right is a form of variable consideration — you don't actually expect to keep all the cash, because some will be refunded.

The recognition pattern at sale:

  1. Recognize revenue only for the amount the company expects to be entitled to (i.e., excluding the estimated returns)
  2. Record a refund liability for the consideration expected to be refunded
  3. Record a return asset (with corresponding adjustment to COGS) for the inventory expected to come back

Worked example

A retailer sells 1,000 widgets at $50 each (cost = $30 each). Past experience says 5% will be returned within 30 days.

At point of sale:
DR  Cash                        50,000   [1,000 × $50]
    CR  Sales Revenue         47,500   [950 expected to keep × $50]
    CR  Refund Liability      2,500   [50 expected returns × $50]

DR  COGS                     28,500   [950 × $30]
DR  Return Asset         1,500   [50 × $30]
    CR  Inventory              30,000
Re-estimate at every reporting date

If actual returns differ from estimate, adjust the refund liability and return asset (and revenue/COGS) at the next reporting date. The estimate is updated to reflect current expectations — both for items already sold and for new sales.

§27.2Consigned goods

Consignment is when a consignor (the owner) ships goods to a consignee (typically a retailer or distributor) who agrees to sell them on behalf of the consignor for a commission. Critically, title doesn't pass until the consignee sells to the end customer.

Consignor's booksConsignee's books
When goods shipReclassify Inventory → "Inventory on consignment"No entry — does not own the goods
When sold to customerDR Cash (or AR) / CR Sales / DR COGS / CR Inventory on consignmentRecognize commission revenue only (agent — net basis)
Year-end inventoryIncludes consigned goods sitting at consigneeExcludes consignor's goods from inventory count

§27.3Bill-and-hold arrangements

The customer is "billed" but the seller continues to hold the inventory. To recognize revenue before shipment, ALL of the following must be true (ASC 606):

  1. The arrangement has a substantive reason (e.g., customer asked for it)
  2. The product is separately identified as belonging to the customer
  3. The product is ready for physical transfer
  4. The seller cannot use the product or direct it to another customer

Failing any one means the seller hasn't transferred control — defer the revenue.

§27.4Channel stuffing — a red flag

"Channel stuffing" is when a seller pushes excess inventory into distributors at quarter-end to inflate revenue, knowing much will likely be returned. This is a classic earnings-management tactic that ASC 606's right-of-return mechanics partly counter — proper estimation of returns prevents the channel-stuffer from booking the full revenue.

Try it

Right of return — see the entries flex with the return rate

Adjust the variables and watch the four pieces (revenue, refund liability, COGS, return asset) move together. Higher expected returns shrink revenue today and inflate the refund liability.

1,000
$50
$30
5%
Revenue (today)$0
Refund liability$0
COGS (today)$0
Return asset$0
Gross profit (net of returns)$0
"Naïve" full revenue (wrong)$0

Why the line-through? Recognising the full $50,000 ignores the variable-consideration constraint and overstates revenue by the expected return amount.

Check your understanding

A clothing retailer sells $100,000 of merchandise (cost: $60,000) on the last day of Q4, with a generous 60-day return policy. Historical returns are 15%. What revenue should be recognized?

  • $0 — wait until the return window closes
  • $100,000 (recognize full amount, book returns when they happen)
  • $85,000 — net of estimated returns; book a $15,000 refund liability + $9,000 return asset
  • $15,000 — only the portion likely to be returned
Why: ASC 606 requires recognizing only the consideration the company expects to keep. With 15% expected returns, recognize $85,000 revenue and $51,000 COGS (85% × $60K). Book a $15,000 refund liability and $9,000 return asset for the expected returns. If actual returns differ, adjust at the next reporting date.

§27.4Right of return — full mechanics under ASC 606

When customers have a right of return, the seller recognizes revenue net of expected returns:

At sale (estimated 10% return rate; sales = $1M, cost = $600K):
DR Cash (or AR) $1,000,000CR Sales Revenue $900,000 (90%)
CR Refund Liability $100,000 (expected refund)
DR Cost of Goods Sold $540,000 (90%)CR Inventory $600,000
DR Return Asset $60,000 (expected returned inventory)
When actual returns occur (e.g., 8% returned):
DR Refund Liability $80,000CR Cash $80,000
DR Inventory $48,000 (returned at original cost)CR Return Asset $48,000
True-up at period end (returns less than expected — 8% vs estimated 10%):
DR Refund Liability $20,000CR Sales Revenue $20,000 (revenue not refunded)
DR Cost of Goods Sold $12,000CR Return Asset $12,000

§27.5Bill-and-hold — five strict criteria

To recognize revenue when goods remain at the seller's location ("billed but held"), ALL FIVE criteria must be met:

  1. The reason for the arrangement must be substantive — typically requested by the customer (not seller-driven to accelerate revenue).
  2. The product must be identified separately as belonging to the customer.
  3. The product must be currently ready for physical transfer.
  4. The seller must NOT have ability to use the product or direct it to another customer.
  5. Customer has accepted/will accept billing terms.
Bill-and-hold red flags

Aggressive bill-and-hold can be revenue manipulation — recording sales that haven't truly transferred control. The SEC has prosecuted multiple companies for premature bill-and-hold recognition. The substantive reason test is the key safeguard.

§27.6Consignment arrangements — full picture

In consignment, the consignor (manufacturer) ships goods to the consignee (retailer) for sale, but title and risk remain with the consignor.

EventConsignor (manufacturer)Consignee (retailer)
ShipmentInventory remains on consignor's books at cost; reclassify as "Inventory on consignment"No entry; track in memorandum records
Sale by consignee to end customerRecognize sales revenue (full price, less commission)Receive cash; remit consignor's share; recognize commission revenue
Year-end (unsold consigned goods)Remain in consignor's inventoryNot in consignee's inventory

§27.7Repurchase agreements — three scenarios

If a sale includes a repurchase obligation/right, the substance is determined by who has the power and at what price:

  1. Forward (seller's obligation to repurchase): Seller is committed to buy back. Treat as a financing arrangement (no revenue recognition).
  2. Call option (seller's right to repurchase): Seller can choose to buy back. If repurchase price ≥ original sale price → financing. If < original → lease.
  3. Put option (customer's right to require repurchase): If customer has economic incentive to exercise → financing. If not → may treat as sale with right of return.

§27.8Channel stuffing — what to watch for

"Channel stuffing" occurs when companies push excess inventory into distribution channels at quarter-end to inflate revenue, often combined with implicit return rights. ASC 606 requires recognizing only consideration the company expects to keep, so:

  • If returns are expected to be high, revenue is constrained.
  • Cash received doesn't equal revenue earned.
  • Build refund liability for expected returns.

Check your understanding — Practice 2

A pharma company ships $500,000 of drugs to a distributor in December. The distributor has unlimited right of return for 90 days. Historical returns are 35%. The pharma company should recognize:

  • $325,000 revenue (after estimating returns); $175,000 refund liability
  • $500,000 revenue; recognize returns when they occur
  • $0 revenue until 90 days pass
  • $500,000 revenue minus a $175,000 expense
Why: ASC 606: recognize revenue only for consideration expected to keep. With 35% expected returns, recognize 65% × $500K = $325K. Build refund liability of $175K and return asset for the expected returned inventory at cost. If actual returns differ, true up at period end.

Check your understanding — Practice 3

A company sells equipment for $80,000 with a contractual obligation to repurchase it 18 months later for $90,000. This transaction is properly accounted for as:

  • Sale; recognize $80,000 revenue
  • Sale with right of return
  • Lease (operating)
  • Financing arrangement (no revenue; recognize $10,000 as interest expense over 18 months)
Why: Forward repurchase obligation at higher price = financing. The transaction is in substance a $80K loan repaid with $90K. The "buyer" is really the lender; the $10K difference is interest. No revenue is recognized.
Unit 28 · A.2 · Recognition & Measurement

Long-Term Contracts

When a project spans multiple years, when do you book the revenue? Welcome to over-time recognition and the percentage-of-completion method.

§28.1The big choice: over time or point in time

Under ASC 606, every performance obligation is recognized either at a point in time or over time. To qualify for over-time recognition, the contract must meet any one of three criteria:

Three criteria for over-time recognition (any ONE qualifies)
  1. The customer simultaneously receives and consumes the benefits as the entity performs (e.g., janitorial services, internet service)
  2. The entity's performance creates or enhances an asset that the customer controls (e.g., construction on the customer's land)
  3. The asset has no alternative use to the entity AND the entity has an enforceable right to payment for performance to date (e.g., a custom-built airliner)

If none apply, recognize at a point in time — typically when the customer takes physical control. Most large construction and custom-build contracts pass the over-time tests.

§28.2Measuring progress — input vs. output methods

MethodHow it worksPros / cons
Input methods (most common: cost-to-cost)Measure progress by inputs consumed: costs incurred / total estimated costsEasy to measure, but may not reflect actual progress if early-stage costs (mobilization) are high
Output methodsMeasure progress by outputs produced: units delivered, milestones reached, surveys of work performedBetter reflects value transferred, but harder to measure objectively

§28.3Cost-to-cost mechanics — the workhorse

% Complete  =  Costs incurred to date  ÷  Total estimated costs at completion

Then:

Revenue this period  =  (% Complete  ×  Total Contract Price)  −  Revenue Previously Recognized

Worked example

A 3-year contract for $10M total. Estimated total cost $8M.

Year 1Year 2Year 3
Costs incurred this year$2,000,000$3,200,000$2,800,000
Cumulative costs$2,000,000$5,200,000$8,000,000
% Complete25%65%100%
Cumulative revenue (% × $10M)$2,500,000$6,500,000$10,000,000
Revenue this period$2,500,000$4,000,000$3,500,000
Gross profit this period$500,000$800,000$700,000

§28.4Anticipatory loss — the most-tested wrinkle

If at any point you realize the total contract is going to lose money, you recognize the entire estimated loss immediately — not over the remaining period. This is one of the few asymmetric treatments in ASC 606 (gains are spread; losses are accelerated).

Why immediate loss recognition?

Conservatism. Once a contract is known to be a loss, deferring recognition would overstate net income in the current period. The total loss must be booked at once, and any prior-period gross profit on this contract effectively unwinds.

Try it

Cost-to-cost percentage of completion

Set the contract price, total estimated cost, and costs to date. See where you stand and what gets recognized this period.

$10,000,000
$8,000,000
$5,200,000
$2,500,000
% Complete (cost-to-cost)0%
Cumulative revenue earned$0
Revenue THIS period$0
Estimated total profit (loss)$0
Status—

§28.5Contract assets and contract liabilities

For long-term contracts, the timing of revenue and billings rarely match. The difference goes to:

  • Contract asset (when revenue exceeds billings) — also called "Cost and estimated earnings in excess of billings"
  • Contract liability (when billings exceed revenue) — also called "Billings in excess of cost and estimated earnings"

Under ASC 606, contract assets and liabilities for the SAME contract are netted on the balance sheet. They are NOT netted across different contracts.

§28.6What about the old "completed contract" method?

Pre-606, U.S. construction companies could elect completed contract — defer all revenue and costs until the project finished. ASC 606 effectively eliminated this for most contracts. If your contract qualifies for over-time recognition (most do), you must use it. Only when none of the three criteria apply do you recognize at a point in time — and even then, that's typically at completion or delivery, not "completed contract" in the old sense.

Check your understanding

A 3-year construction contract has a total price of $20M and estimated cost of $16M. By end of Year 1, $4M of costs have been incurred. How much gross profit is recognized in Year 1?

  • $0 — wait for completion
  • $1,000,000 (25% × $4M total profit)
  • $4,000,000 (the costs incurred)
  • $5,000,000 (Year-1 revenue based on 25%)
Why: % Complete = $4M ÷ $16M = 25%. Total estimated profit = $20M − $16M = $4M. Profit recognized in Year 1 = 25% × $4M = $1M. (Equivalently: revenue 25% × $20M = $5M, minus costs $4M = $1M profit.)

§28.4Cost-to-cost percentage of completion — full mechanics

% Complete = Costs incurred to date ÷ Total estimated costs

Each period's revenue and gross profit:

  1. Update the % complete based on costs incurred to date.
  2. Cumulative revenue = % complete × total contract price.
  3. Cumulative profit = % complete × total estimated profit.
  4. Current period revenue = cumulative revenue − previously recognized revenue.
  5. Current period profit = cumulative profit − previously recognized profit.

§28.5Multi-year worked example

3-year construction contract

Contract price $30M. Total estimated cost initially $24M (estimated profit $6M).

Year 1Year 2Year 3
Costs incurred during year$6M$10M$8M
Cumulative costs$6M$16M$24M
Estimated remaining cost$18M$8M$0
Total estimated cost$24M$24M$24M
% Complete25%66.67%100%
Cumulative revenue$7.5M$20M$30M
Current revenue$7.5M$12.5M$10M
Cumulative profit$1.5M$4M$6M
Current profit$1.5M$2.5M$2M

§28.6Anticipatory loss recognition

If at any point the total expected cost exceeds the contract price (i.e., the contract is expected to lose money), recognize the entire expected loss immediately. This is asymmetric: profits spread, losses recognized at once.

Worked example — anticipatory loss

3-year contract, price $20M. After Year 1, costs incurred = $5M. New estimate: total cost = $24M (was $18M).

Total expected loss = $20M − $24M = $4M loss. Year 1 had been recognizing profit (assumed); now reverse and recognize entire $4M loss.
% complete = 5/24 ≈ 20.8%. Cumulative revenue = 20.8% × $20M = $4.17M.
Cumulative loss recognized = $4M (the entire anticipated loss).
Loss provision: $4M − cumulative profit previously recognized = $4M (assuming Year 1 had recognized $0 profit prior to this re-estimate).

§28.7Billed vs. unbilled — balance sheet accounts

Long-term contracts generate two key balance-sheet accounts:

AccountWhen it arisesB/S classification
Contract Asset (or "Costs and estimated earnings in excess of billings")Revenue recognized exceeds amounts billed to customer to dateCurrent asset
Contract Liability (or "Billings in excess of costs and estimated earnings")Amounts billed exceed revenue recognized to dateCurrent liability

The two accounts can exist for different contracts simultaneously — don't net across contracts. A construction company with 50 active contracts might have contract assets for some and contract liabilities for others.

§28.8Over-time vs. point-in-time recognition (general goods)

For non-construction long-term contracts, the over-time tests of ASC 606 still apply (covered in Unit 26 §26.8). If the over-time tests fail, recognize at a point in time — typically when the customer takes possession, accepts the asset, or has legal title.

§28.9Completed-contract method — when allowed

Under ASC 606, the previously-allowed "completed-contract" method (defer all revenue and cost until contract completion) is generally no longer permitted for new contracts. The over-time tests typically apply to long-term construction (asset has no alternative use + enforceable right to payment for performance to date), forcing percentage-of-completion treatment.

Limited exception: Very short contracts where outcome is highly uncertain. In practice, most large construction is recognized over time using cost-to-cost.

Check your understanding — Practice 2

A 3-year contract has price $50M, original cost estimate $40M. Year 1 costs = $10M. End of Year 1, cost estimate revised to $44M. What revenue and gross profit are recognized in Year 1?

  • $12.5M revenue, $2.5M profit (using original estimate)
  • $11.4M revenue, $1.4M profit
  • $11.36M revenue (10/44 × 50); $1.36M profit
  • $0 — wait until completion
Why: Use revised estimate. % complete = $10M / $44M = 22.73%. Revenue = 22.73% × $50M = $11.36M. Total expected profit = $50M − $44M = $6M. Profit Year 1 = 22.73% × $6M = $1.36M. The cost estimate revision flows through the percentage calculation.

Check your understanding — Practice 3

A 4-year contract: cumulative billings $25M; cumulative costs $30M; cumulative revenue recognized $35M. The B/S shows:

  • Contract Asset of $10M
  • Contract Liability of $5M
  • AR of $25M
  • Loss of $5M
Why: Recognized $35M revenue but only billed $25M → Contract Asset of $10M (have earned more than we've invoiced; future right to bill). Costs incurred $30M and revenue $35M → $5M cumulative profit (separate from balance sheet). The $10M is the unbilled portion of revenue earned.
Unit 29 · A.2 · Recognition & Measurement

US GAAP vs. IFRS — Master Difference List

A consolidated reference of every major GAAP/IFRS difference covered in Section A. Bookmark this — it's the most exam-tested cross-cutting topic.

§29.1Why these differences exist

US GAAP is generally more rules-based — specific guidance for many situations. IFRS is more principles-based — broader frameworks with more judgment. Beyond philosophy, decades of separate evolution mean dozens of specific points diverge.

§29.2By topic — the master list

Statement presentation

TopicUS GAAPIFRS
B/S orderingLiquidity (most liquid first)Reverse liquidity (least liquid first) — common in EU
Extraordinary itemsEliminated (since 2015)Never permitted
Comparative period required1 prior year typically2 prior years for some statements

Cash flow statement

TopicUS GAAPIFRS
Interest paidOperating (required)Operating OR Financing (choice)
Interest receivedOperating (required)Operating OR Investing (choice)
Dividends paidFinancing (required)Operating OR Financing (choice)
Dividends receivedOperating (required)Operating OR Investing (choice)
Bank overdraftsTreated as financing (short-term debt)Treated as cash equivalents (negative cash)

Inventory (ASC 330 / IAS 2)

TopicUS GAAPIFRS
LIFOPermittedProhibited
Lower of cost and...NRV (since 2015 — for FIFO/avg); Lower of cost or market for LIFONRV
Reversal of write-downsProhibitedPermitted up to original cost

Equity securities (non-significant)FV through net income (since 2018)FVPL or FVOCI (irrevocable election)

Deferred tax assetsRecognize all; use valuation allowance to reduce if not "more likely than not" realizableRecognize only to extent "probable" of realization
Tax rateEnacted rateEnacted or substantively enacted rate
B/S classification of DTA/DTLAll non-currentAll non-current

Revenue (ASC 606 / IFRS 15) — mostly converged

ASC 606 and IFRS 15 are substantially identical — they were issued jointly by FASB and IASB in 2014 as a converged standard. Minor differences exist (e.g., interim disclosures, contract modification specifics), but the 5-step model is the same.

§29.3Quick mnemonic for the biggest five

"L.I.D.D.R." — the most-tested differences
  • LIFO — GAAP yes, IFRS no
  • Impairment reversals — IFRS allows (except goodwill); GAAP doesn't
  • Development costs — IFRS capitalizes when criteria met; GAAP expenses
  • Dual lease model — GAAP keeps it; IFRS has single model for lessees
  • Revaluation model — IFRS allows for PP&E; GAAP cost only

Check your understanding

A company writes down inventory by $50,000 in 20X1 due to declining market value. In 20X2, market value recovers and the inventory could be sold above its written-down basis. Under IFRS, what's the treatment in 20X2?

  • No reversal allowed under any framework
  • Reverse the entire $50,000, even if it exceeds original cost
  • Reverse only if the same inventory units are still on hand
  • Reverse up to the lower of recovered value or original cost (so the carrying value never exceeds the original cost)
Why: IFRS permits reversal of inventory write-downs, but capped at the original cost — you can never write the inventory up beyond what you originally paid. Under US GAAP, the reversal would not be permitted at all (write-downs create a new cost basis). This is one of the cleanest GAAP/IFRS differences to remember.

§29.4Master comparison — the IMA's six required difference categories

The IMA outline (LOS A.2.ff) explicitly requires you to know six categories of GAAP/IFRS differences. This is the master reference table:

Category 1 — Expense recognition: share-based payments and employee benefits

TopicUS GAAPIFRS
Stock-based comp expense recognitionRecognize over service period (graded vesting can use straight-line)Recognize over service period — graded vesting MUST use accelerated (each tranche separately)
Forfeiture estimatesMay elect to estimate or account for as they occurMust estimate forfeitures
Pension net periodic costService cost in operating; interest, expected return, prior service cost, gains/losses below operatingService cost AND net interest in operating; remeasurements in OCI (no recycling)
Pension actuarial G/LRecognize in OCI; amortize to NI over future service period (corridor method)Recognize in OCI immediately; NEVER recycle to NI

Category 2 — Intangible assets: development costs and revaluation

TopicUS GAAPIFRS
Research costsExpense as incurredExpense as incurred
Development costsExpense as incurred (with narrow software exceptions)Capitalize when 6 PIRATE criteria met

§29.5Other commonly-tested differences (beyond the six core categories)

Cash flow — interest paidOperating onlyOperating OR financing (choice)
Cash flow — interest receivedOperating onlyOperating OR investing (choice)
Cash flow — dividends paidFinancing onlyOperating OR financing (choice)
Cash flow — dividends receivedOperating onlyOperating OR investing (choice)

Loss contingencies (ranges, no best estimate)Accrue LOW endAccrue MID-POINT (or expected value)
Discontinued operations criterionStrategic shift representing a major effectComponent held for sale or disposed
Revaluation surplus recyclingN/A (no revaluation)NEVER recycled to NI; goes directly to RE on disposal
Convergence projects ongoingSome standards converged (revenue, leases)Some converged; some still diverge significantly

§29.6Why these differences matter analytically

Multinational companies often present financial statements under one framework but operate globally. Analysts converting between frameworks face issues:

  • Inventory comparability: A LIFO US company has lower inventory and higher COGS than its IFRS peers. Add LIFO reserve to compare.
  • R&D companies (pharma, tech): US GAAP shows higher current expense; IFRS shows capitalized intangibles → differing ROA, equity, depreciation patterns.
  • Lease-heavy industries (retail, airlines): ASC 842 vs IFRS 16 produce different EBITDA (operating leases under GAAP show lease expense in EBITDA; IFRS 16 splits to interest + amort, raising EBITDA).
  • Asset-heavy (real estate, manufacturing): IFRS revaluation creates higher equity and asset values than US GAAP historical cost.

Check your understanding — Practice 2

A pharma company spends $100M on a clinical trial in 2024. Six PIRATE criteria are met. Under each framework:

  • Both GAAP and IFRS expense $100M
  • Both capitalize $100M
  • GAAP expense $100M; IFRS capitalize $100M as intangible asset
  • GAAP capitalize; IFRS expense
Why: Development costs treatment is the cleanest GAAP/IFRS difference. GAAP expenses (with narrow software exceptions). IFRS capitalizes when six criteria met. This makes IFRS-reporting pharma firms show higher assets and lower current expense, but higher amortization and impairment risk later.

Check your understanding — Practice 3

A retailer has 100 operating leases (5+ years, total cash payments $500M). Under US GAAP vs IFRS, which is true about EBITDA?

  • EBITDA is HIGHER under IFRS — operating lease expense becomes interest + depreciation, both excluded from EBITDA
  • EBITDA is LOWER under IFRS
  • EBITDA is the same — just classification differs
  • EBITDA is undefined under IFRS
Why: Under IFRS 16, all lessee leases are like finance leases — split into interest expense (excluded from EBITDA) and amortization (excluded from EBITDA). Under US GAAP operating lease, the single lease expense IS in operating expenses (included in EBITDA). Switching to IFRS mechanically raises EBITDA for retailers and airlines without changing the cash economics — a significant analytical difference.
Drill

Which framework? Drag each rule into US GAAP, IFRS, or Both

"GAAP" = US GAAP only · "IFRS" = IFRS only · "Both" = same treatment in both. Sort all sixteen items, then check.
