Unit 30 · A.2 · Recognition & Measurement

Income Measurement & Asset Disposals

The closing unit — when do gains and losses hit the income statement, how do we measure them, and where do involuntary disposals fit?

§30.1Revenue vs. gain — the distinction

Both increase equity, but they live in different parts of the income statement:

  • Revenue comes from the company's central, ongoing operations — selling its products or services (covered exhaustively in Units 26–28)
  • Gain comes from incidental or peripheral activities — selling old equipment, foreign exchange wins, settling lawsuits favorably

Revenues appear high in the income statement (top), gains usually lower (in "Other income/expense"). The split helps users assess "core" earnings power vs. one-off items.

§30.2Expense recognition principles

PatternHow to recognizeExamples
Direct matching (cause and effect)Match expense to revenue in the same periodCOGS to sales; sales commissions to sales
Systematic allocationSpread cost over periods benefitedDepreciation, amortization, prepaid expenses
Immediate expensingNo clear future benefit — expense as incurredMost marketing, R&D under GAAP, salaries

Product costs vs. period costs:

  • Product costs attach to inventory (DM, DL, manufacturing overhead) and are expensed as COGS only when inventory is sold
  • Period costs hit the income statement in the period incurred (selling expenses, G&A, most administrative items)

§30.3Gains and losses on disposal of fixed assets

The classic CMA gain/loss formula:

Gain (Loss)  =  Proceeds Received  −  Carrying Value (NBV)
Carrying Value  =  Original Cost  −  Accumulated Depreciation

Worked example

A delivery truck was bought for $40,000. Over 5 years, $30,000 of accumulated depreciation has been recorded. The truck is sold for $7,000 cash.

Carrying value = $40,000 − $30,000 = $10,000
Loss on sale = $7,000 − $10,000 = ($3,000)

DR  Cash                       7,000
DR  Accumulated Depreciation  30,000
DR  Loss on Disposal          3,000
    CR  Truck (asset)            40,000

Notice how every reference to the asset is removed from the books: cost (CR Truck $40K) and accumulated depreciation (DR Accum Dep $30K). What's left in the loss is the difference between cash received and net book value.

Try it

Gain/loss on asset disposal calculator

Set the original cost, accumulated depreciation, and sale proceeds. The calculator finds the carrying value and the gain or loss.

$40,000
$30,000
$7,000
Carrying value (NBV)
$0
Gain / (Loss)
$0
Result
—

§30.4Involuntary disposals — insurance and condemnation

Sometimes assets leave through accident or government action: a fire destroys a warehouse, eminent domain condemns property, an insured truck is totaled. The gain/loss calculation works the same way:

  • Insurance proceeds (or condemnation award) replace "sale proceeds"
  • If proceeds exceed NBV → gain (yes, even from a destroyed asset)
  • If proceeds are less than NBV → loss

U.S. tax law sometimes allows deferring the gain via involuntary conversion rules (replace the property within a window) — but for GAAP financial reporting, the gain is recognized when realized.

§30.5Presentation on the income statement

Common locations for gains and losses on the income statement:

ItemTypical location
Gain/loss on sale of PP&E"Other operating income (expense)" or below operating income
Gain/loss on sale of investmentsNon-operating section
Foreign exchange gains/lossesNon-operating, often in "Other"
Restructuring chargesWithin operating, often a separate line
Discontinued operationsBelow NI from continuing operations, net of tax

§30.6Comprehensive income — a quick recap

Some changes in equity bypass net income but still represent value changes from non-owner sources. They sit in Other Comprehensive Income (OCI):

  • Foreign currency translation adjustments
  • Unrealized gains/losses on AFS debt securities (until realized)
  • Pension and post-retirement benefit adjustments
  • Effective portion of cash flow hedges
  • Revaluation surplus (IFRS only)

Mnemonic: "FUPER" — these are the items that go through OCI and accumulate in AOCI in equity.

Comprehensive Income = Net Income + OCI. It captures all non-owner changes in equity for the period. Discussed in detail in Unit 3.

You've reached the end of Section A

Congratulations. You've moved from "what is accounting?" to consolidating multinationals, recognizing revenue across multi-year contracts, navigating GAAP/IFRS divergence, and computing gains on involuntary disposals. The next big chunk of CMA Part 1 is Section B (Planning, Budgeting, Forecasting). When you're ready, we can build a parallel guide for it.

Check your understanding

A factory machine cost $80,000 and has $50,000 of accumulated depreciation. It's destroyed in a fire; insurance pays $40,000. What's the gain or loss?

  • Loss of $40,000 (the machine is gone)
  • Loss of $30,000 (NBV)
  • Gain of $10,000 (proceeds exceed NBV)
  • No gain or loss — replaces the asset
Why: NBV = $80,000 − $50,000 = $30,000. Proceeds ($40,000) exceed NBV by $10,000 → recognize a $10,000 gain. Yes, even though the machine was destroyed — the insurance settled at more than book value. (For tax purposes, this gain might be deferrable via involuntary-conversion rules if a replacement is bought, but GAAP recognizes it now.)

§30.4Asset disposal — full mechanics

The disposal of a fixed asset always follows the same logic, in three steps:

  1. Update depreciation to the date of disposal (partial-year depreciation if mid-period).
  2. Calculate gain or loss = Proceeds − Net Book Value (NBV = Cost − Updated Accumulated Depreciation).
  3. Record disposal: remove the asset and its accumulated depreciation from books; recognize cash received and gain/loss.
Worked example — partial-year disposal

Equipment cost $100,000, accumulated depreciation $40,000 at 12/31/Y3. Annual straight-line depreciation = $10,000/year. Equipment sold on 4/30/Y4 for $55,000.

Step 1 — Update depreciation: 4 months × ($10,000/12) = $3,333.
Step 2 — NBV at disposal: Cost $100,000 − Accumulated Dep ($40,000 + $3,333) = $56,667.
Step 3 — Gain/loss: Proceeds $55,000 − NBV $56,667 = $1,667 loss.
Journal: DR Cash $55,000; DR Accumulated Dep $43,333; DR Loss on Disposal $1,667; CR Equipment $100,000.

§30.5Three categories of asset disposal

TypeDescriptionSpecial handling
SaleVoluntary cash sale to third partyStandard gain/loss treatment
Retirement / abandonmentAsset taken out of service; no proceedsLoss = full NBV (after updating depreciation). DR Loss; DR AD; CR Asset
Involuntary conversionDisaster, theft, condemnation, eminent domainGain/loss = Insurance/condemnation proceeds − NBV. Tax may allow deferral if replacement purchased

§30.6Where does the gain/loss appear?

For most companies, gains and losses on disposal of fixed assets are non-operating items, presented in "Other income/expense" below operating income. Reasons:

  • Disposing of fixed assets is generally not the company's central business activity (a clothing retailer doesn't make money selling fixtures).
  • Gains/losses are typically irregular and unpredictable.
  • Including them in operating income would distort core operating performance metrics.

Exception: Companies that routinely deal in such assets (used-car dealers selling fleets, real estate developers selling buildings) may present these gains as operating revenue.

§30.7Discontinued operations vs. ordinary disposals

When a disposal is part of a strategic shift (closing an entire product line, exiting a geographic region, divesting a major subsidiary), it qualifies for discontinued operations treatment — separate presentation, net of tax. Otherwise, ordinary gain/loss handling applies.

QuestionOrdinary disposalDiscontinued operation
Strategic shift?NoYes — major effect on operations
Component of entity?NoYes — separately identifiable operations and cash flows
PresentationOther income/expense (in continuing operations)Separate section, net of tax, after continuing operations
Comparative restatementNoRestate prior periods for comparability

§30.8Comprehensive income — connecting the dots

Comprehensive Income captures all changes in equity except owner-related transactions. Memorize the relationship:

Comprehensive Income = Net Income + Other Comprehensive Income (PUFER items, net of tax)

For the period, the equity section's beginning AOCI + current OCI = ending AOCI. The cumulative AOCI is on the balance sheet; the period OCI is on the statement of comprehensive income.

§30.9Asset held for sale — special treatment

If management commits to selling an asset (or component) within one year, classify as "held for sale":

  • Stop depreciating/amortizing the asset.
  • Reclassify on the balance sheet to "Assets held for sale" (current asset).
  • Measure at lower of (carrying value, FV − costs to sell). Recognize impairment if needed.
  • Disclosure: describe asset, expected sale, and any impairment recognized.

§30.10Section A wrap-up — most-tested concepts checklist

TopicWhy memorize cold
Indirect cash flow operating sectionMulti-step calculation; appears on every CMA Part 1 exam
DTA/DTL identification (warranty, MACRS, NOL, etc.)Most-tested topic in Section A
5-step revenue recognition modelASC 606 is heavily tested — including principal vs agent
Lease classification tests (5 tests for finance lease)ASC 842 is recent and frequently tested
Inventory cost flows (FIFO/LIFO/WA calculations)Calculation problems appear regularly
EPS basic and diluted (treasury stock method)Standard calculation
OCI items (PUFER) and recycling rulesConceptual + comprehensive income calc
GAAP/IFRS differences (the six categories)Explicitly tested per IMA outline LOS A.2.ff
Goodwill and impairment mechanicsTwo-step vs. one-step; reversibility differences
Long-term contracts (cost-to-cost % completion)Multi-year worked problems

Check your understanding — Practice 2

A company decides to sell a product line that constitutes 40% of revenue and a complete operating segment. The sale is strategic. Pre-tax operating income from this segment in the year of decision: $300K. Estimated loss on sale: $200K. Tax rate 25%. What appears in "Discontinued Operations"?

  • $100K loss, net of tax
  • $75K income, net of tax (+$300K op income − $200K loss = +$100K pre-tax × 75% = $75K)
  • $300K operating income only
  • $200K disposal loss only
Why: Both operating results AND disposal gain/loss are included, presented net of tax. Pre-tax = $300K − $200K = $100K positive. After tax (25%) = $75K. Both pieces are part of "discontinued operations" once the strategic-shift criterion is met.

Check your understanding — Practice 3

A company decides to sell a building (carrying value $2M) within the next 6 months. Estimated FV = $1.7M; selling costs = $50K. Under "held for sale" classification:

  • Continue depreciating; reclassify to current at sale
  • Carrying value remains $2M; defer all changes until sale
  • Stop depreciating immediately; remeasure at $1,650,000 (FV − selling costs); recognize $350K impairment
  • Move to current at $1,700,000 with no impairment
Why: Held-for-sale: stop depreciating; measure at lower of CV ($2M) or FV less costs to sell ($1.7M − $50K = $1.65M). Recognize $350K impairment (CV $2M − $1.65M). Reclassify to current asset on B/S. If FV recovers before sale, reverse impairment up to original CV.
Practice Test 1 · Foundations

Test 1 — Foundations

Twenty-five CMA-style questions on accrual mechanics, debits and credits, the accounting cycle, and the conceptual framework. Score above 75% before moving on to Section A.1 material.

Before you begin

These questions test the same foundational concepts the IMA emphasizes in Levels A and B of the CMA Content Specification Outline for Section A. Click your answer to lock it in — explanations appear after each choice, and your running score is tracked at the top. The timer in the progress bar tracks pace at 1.8 min / question — CMA Part 1's actual time budget.

Questions25 DifficultyFoundational (Level A–B) Time target~45 min Pass mark75% (19/25)
0 / 25 answered
0 correct
Question 1Accrual basis

A company performs $10,000 of consulting services in December but won't receive cash until March. Under accrual accounting, when is revenue recognized?

  • March (when cash is received)
  • Allocated evenly across December–March
  • December (when services were performed)
  • Whenever the bill is sent to the customer
Why: Accrual recognizes revenue when earned — when the performance obligation is satisfied — not when cash arrives. Services were performed in December, so revenue belongs there. The unpaid amount becomes Accounts Receivable.
Question 2Underlying assumptions

Which of the following accounting concepts justifies the use of historical cost rather than liquidation values for fixed assets?

  • Going concern assumption
  • Periodicity assumption
  • Monetary unit assumption
  • Materiality constraint
Why: Going concern assumes the entity will continue operating for the foreseeable future. Without this, accountants would have to value assets at liquidation value (what they'd fetch in a fire sale) rather than at depreciated historical cost.
Question 3Debits & credits

Which combination of accounts has a credit normal balance?

  • Cash, Accounts Receivable, Inventory
  • Salaries Expense, Rent Expense, COGS
  • Accounts Payable, Common Stock, Service Revenue
  • Equipment, Prepaid Insurance, Dividends
Why: Liabilities, equity (incl. common stock), and revenues all carry credit normal balances (the "CLIC" half of DEAD CLIC). Assets and expenses carry debit normal balances.
Question 4Journal entries

A company pays $24,000 cash in advance for two years of insurance coverage. What is the journal entry on the date of payment?

  • DR Insurance Expense $24,000 / CR Cash $24,000
  • DR Prepaid Insurance $24,000 / CR Cash $24,000
  • DR Cash $24,000 / CR Prepaid Insurance $24,000
  • DR Prepaid Insurance $12,000 / DR Insurance Expense $12,000 / CR Cash $24,000
Why: Coverage hasn't been used yet — it's a future benefit, an asset called Prepaid Insurance. As coverage is consumed, monthly adjusting entries (DR Insurance Expense / CR Prepaid Insurance) gradually move the asset to expense.
Question 5Accounting cycle

On December 31, employees have earned $15,000 in wages that will be paid on the next payroll on January 4. What's the December 31 adjusting entry?

  • DR Cash $15,000 / CR Wages Expense $15,000
  • No entry — wait until cash is paid in January
  • DR Wages Payable $15,000 / CR Wages Expense $15,000
  • DR Wages Expense $15,000 / CR Wages Payable $15,000
Why: Accrued expense — work has been performed in December, so the expense and matching liability must be recognized in December even though cash will move in January. Debit the expense; credit a payable.
Question 6Temporary vs. permanent

Which of the following accounts would not be closed at year-end?

  • Sales Revenue
  • Dividends
  • Accounts Receivable
  • Cost of Goods Sold
Why: Accounts Receivable is a permanent (real) account — its balance carries forward to the next period. Revenues, expenses, gains, losses, and dividends are temporary; they get zeroed out into Retained Earnings at year-end.
Question 7Unearned revenue

On October 1, a magazine publisher collects $1,200 cash for a 12-month subscription beginning that day. What is recognized as revenue on the December 31 income statement?

  • $1,200
  • $300 (3 months earned: Oct, Nov, Dec)
  • $900 (9 months unearned)
  • $0 — wait until subscription ends
Why: $1,200 ÷ 12 months = $100/month. Revenue recognized for 3 months earned (Oct, Nov, Dec) = $300. The remaining $900 stays as Unearned Revenue (a liability) until earned in subsequent months.
Question 8Conceptual framework

Per the FASB Conceptual Framework, the two fundamental qualitative characteristics of useful financial information are:

  • Relevance and faithful representation
  • Comparability and consistency
  • Timeliness and verifiability
  • Materiality and conservatism
Why: The fundamentals are relevance (capable of making a difference) and faithful representation (complete, neutral, free from error). The other listed items are enhancing characteristics or constraints — important but at a lower tier.
Question 9Elements of FS

A furniture manufacturer sells its old delivery truck for a $5,000 amount above book value. How is this transaction classified on the income statement?

  • Revenue from operations
  • Gain (peripheral activity)
  • Other comprehensive income
  • Investment by owner
Why: Selling a delivery truck isn't the company's central business activity (which is selling furniture). Increases in equity from peripheral or incidental transactions are classified as gains, not revenues. They typically appear in "Other income" on the income statement.
Question 10Recognition

Why is a company's internally developed brand value typically not recognized on its balance sheet under US GAAP?

  • Brand value is not an asset by definition
  • Brands are immaterial
  • It cannot be reliably measured
  • GAAP requires expensing brand-related costs as incurred
Why: An internally generated brand likely meets the asset definition (probable future economic benefit, controlled by the entity), but it fails the recognition criterion of reliable measurability — there's no transaction price to anchor a value. The same brand, if purchased in an acquisition, could be recognized at its acquisition cost.
Question 11Measurement

Which measurement attribute is most commonly used for inventory under US GAAP?

  • Fair value at the reporting date
  • Replacement cost
  • Lower of cost and net realizable value
  • Present value of future cash flows
Why: Inventory is measured at the lower of historical cost and net realizable value (with FIFO/weighted-average; LIFO uses lower of cost or market). Fair value applies to certain investments; PV applies to long-term receivables and lease liabilities.
Question 12Authority

Which body issues the IFRS Standards used in over 140 countries?

  • FASB (Financial Accounting Standards Board)
  • SEC (Securities and Exchange Commission)
  • AICPA (American Institute of CPAs)
  • IASB (International Accounting Standards Board)
Why: The IASB issues IFRS Standards. FASB issues US GAAP. SEC oversees U.S. public-company disclosure. AICPA sets U.S. auditing standards (though no longer accounting standards for non-public entities — that's the FASB).
Question 13Double-entry

A company purchases $50,000 of inventory on account (credit terms). What is the impact on the accounting equation?

  • Assets ↑ $50,000; Equity ↑ $50,000
  • Assets ↑ $50,000; Liabilities ↑ $50,000
  • Assets ↓ $50,000; Liabilities ↑ $50,000
  • No change to the equation
Why: Inventory (asset) increases by $50,000 (DR), and Accounts Payable (liability) increases by $50,000 (CR). Total assets up, total liabilities up — equation balances. No equity impact since no income or owner activity.
Question 14Contra accounts

Accumulated Depreciation is best described as a:

  • Liability with a credit normal balance
  • Contra-asset with a credit normal balance
  • Expense with a debit normal balance
  • Reduction of equity with a debit normal balance
Why: Accumulated Depreciation is a contra-asset — it sits in the asset section of the balance sheet, reduces the related PP&E account, and (because it offsets an asset with a debit normal balance) carries a credit normal balance.
Question 15Trial balance

If a company's trial balance has equal total debits and credits, this proves:

  • Only that the arithmetic is internally consistent — not that the entries were correct
  • The financial statements will be free from errors
  • All transactions were recorded
  • The company is profitable
Why: Equal totals don't prove correctness. You could have posted a $10,000 sale to the wrong revenue category — debits and credits still equal, but the entry was wrong. The trial balance is a sanity check on math, not a proof of correctness.
Question 16Materiality

A $50 office-supply purchase by a Fortune 500 company is expensed immediately rather than capitalized as an asset. This treatment is justified by:

  • The going-concern principle
  • Historical cost
  • Materiality
  • Conservatism
Why: Materiality lets companies use simpler treatments for items too small to influence users' decisions. A $50 purchase at a giant company is immaterial — even strict capitalize-and-depreciate logic doesn't require the formal treatment when the amount is trivial.
Question 17Comprehensive income

Comprehensive income is best defined as:

  • All non-owner changes in equity for a period
  • Net income plus dividends declared
  • Total assets minus total liabilities
  • Revenue minus expenses, excluding gains and losses
Why: Comprehensive Income = Net Income + Other Comprehensive Income — all changes in equity for a period except investments by and distributions to owners. OCI captures items like FX translation, AFS gains/losses, and pension adjustments that bypass NI.
Question 18Users of FS

Which user of financial statements is most concerned with a company's liquidity — its ability to pay obligations as they come due?

  • Long-term equity investors
  • Tax authorities
  • Customers
  • Short-term creditors and lenders
Why: Short-term creditors (suppliers extending credit terms, banks providing lines of credit) need confidence that the company can convert assets to cash quickly enough to pay them. Liquidity ratios (current, quick) are their primary diagnostic.
Question 19Depreciation adjusting entry

A company's machinery has accumulated $20,000 of depreciation over the year. The year-end adjusting entry is:

  • DR Machinery $20,000 / CR Cash $20,000
  • DR Cash $20,000 / CR Depreciation Expense $20,000
  • DR Depreciation Expense $20,000 / CR Accumulated Depreciation $20,000
  • DR Accumulated Depreciation $20,000 / CR Machinery $20,000
Why: The yearly depreciation entry recognizes the expense (DR) and increases the contra-asset Accumulated Depreciation (CR). The original Machinery account is never adjusted by depreciation; only Accumulated Depreciation builds up over time.
Question 20Accounting equation

A company has Total Assets of $500,000 and Total Liabilities of $180,000. Net income for the year was $80,000 and dividends of $30,000 were declared. What is total equity?

  • $370,000
  • $320,000
  • $250,000
  • $80,000
Why: Total equity = Total Assets − Total Liabilities = $500,000 − $180,000 = $320,000. The income and dividends information is a distractor — those flows are already captured in the ending balances. The accounting equation is a balance-sheet identity at a point in time.
Question 21Closing process

Beginning RE was $240,000. Sales Revenue $620,000, COGS $410,000, Operating expenses $90,000, Interest expense $20,000, and Tax expense $30,000 were incurred. Dividends of $25,000 were declared. What is ending RE?

  • $285,000
  • $310,000
  • $235,000
  • $340,000
Why: NI = $620K − $410K − $90K − $20K − $30K = $70,000. Ending RE = $240K + $70K − $25K = $285,000. Closing zeroes the temporary accounts (revenue, expense, dividends) into RE. The $310K trap forgets the dividend; $235K subtracts NI; $340K ignores both expenses and dividends.
Question 22Accrued interest

On October 1, a company borrows $100,000 at 6% annual interest, all due in one year. What is the December 31 adjusting entry for interest?

  • DR Interest Expense $1,500 / CR Interest Payable $1,500
  • DR Interest Expense $6,000 / CR Cash $6,000
  • DR Interest Expense $500 / CR Cash $500
  • No entry — wait until cash is paid in October next year
Why: Three months (Oct–Dec) of interest accrued: $100,000 × 6% × 3/12 = $1,500. Accrue the expense (DR) and the unpaid liability (CR Interest Payable). Cash hasn't moved — that's why this is an accrual.
Question 23Qualitative characteristics

Two analysts must reach the same conclusion when looking at the same financial information using the same methodology. This describes the enhancing qualitative characteristic of:

  • Comparability
  • Timeliness
  • Verifiability
  • Faithful representation
Why: Verifiability means independent observers reach consensus that the information faithfully represents the underlying economics. Comparability is across firms or periods; timeliness is age-related; faithful representation is a fundamental (not enhancing) characteristic.
Question 24Cash → accrual

A company's cash-basis revenue for the year was $400,000. Beginning AR $30,000; Ending AR $50,000. Beginning Unearned Revenue $10,000; Ending Unearned Revenue $4,000. What is accrual-basis revenue?

  • $374,000
  • $420,000
  • $406,000
  • $426,000
Why: Cash collected $400K + ΔAR ($50K − $30K = $20K of services performed but not yet collected) − ΔUR (Unearned dropped $6K, meaning $6K of previously deferred revenue was earned this year — already in cash receipts so we add it back as accrual revenue): $400K + $20K + $6K = $426,000. Practice the formula: Revenue (accrual) = Cash + ΔAR − ΔUnearned.
Question 25Posting errors

An accountant records a $4,500 supply purchase on credit by debiting Supplies $4,500 and crediting Cash $4,500 (instead of Accounts Payable). Which of the following is true?

  • The trial balance will not balance
  • The trial balance will balance, but Cash and AP will both be understated
  • The trial balance will balance, and the financial statements will be correct
  • Net income will be misstated by $4,500
Why: The trial balance still balances ($4,500 DR matches $4,500 CR), but two accounts are wrong: Cash is $4,500 too low and AP is $4,500 too low. Net income is unaffected (Supplies is correctly debited; the error is in which credit account took the hit). Equal totals don't prove correctness — a recurring CMA distractor.

Test 1 — Foundations · Results

Practice Test 2 · A.1 Financial Statements

Test 2 — Financial Statements

Thirty questions covering the four financial statements, classification, comprehensive income, equity changes, the indirect cash flow method, and integrated reporting.

Coverage

This test draws on Units 1–8 — balance sheet structure, income statement format, comprehensive income, statement of cash flows (indirect method), notes to financial statements, and integrated reporting. Calculation problems mirror IMA-released exam samples in style and difficulty. Five new questions (Q26–30) drill EPS, CFO computation, OCI net-of-tax, SCF classification, and operating income.

Questions30 DifficultyMixed (A–C) Time target~54 min Pass mark75% (23/30)
0 / 30 answered
0 correct
Question 1Balance sheet

Which of the following items would be classified as a current asset on the balance sheet?

  • Goodwill from a business acquisition
  • A long-term investment in another company's stock
  • Inventory expected to be sold within 9 months
  • Equipment with a 10-year useful life
Why: Current assets are expected to be converted to cash, sold, or consumed within one year (or one operating cycle, whichever is longer). Inventory expected to sell in 9 months qualifies. Goodwill, long-term investments, and PP&E are non-current.
Question 2Balance sheet equation

A company has total assets of $850,000 and stockholders' equity of $320,000. What are total liabilities?

  • $530,000
  • $1,170,000
  • $320,000
  • Cannot be determined without net income
Why: Assets = Liabilities + Equity, so Liabilities = Assets − Equity = $850,000 − $320,000 = $530,000. The accounting equation always balances at a reporting date.
Question 3Income statement

In a multi-step income statement, which of the following appears after "Operating Income"?

  • Cost of Goods Sold
  • Interest expense
  • Selling expenses
  • Gross profit
Why: The standard order: Revenue → COGS → Gross Profit → Operating Expenses → Operating Income → Non-operating items (interest, gains/losses) → Pre-tax Income → Tax → Net Income. Interest is a financing cost and falls below operating income.
Question 4Calculation — gross profit

Sales $800,000; Sales returns $40,000; Sales discounts $20,000; COGS $480,000. What is gross profit?

  • $320,000
  • $300,000
  • $260,000
  • $240,000
Why: Net sales = $800,000 − $40,000 − $20,000 = $740,000. Gross profit = Net Sales − COGS = $740,000 − $480,000 = $260,000.
Question 5Comprehensive income

Which of the following is NOT a component of Other Comprehensive Income (OCI) under US GAAP?

  • Foreign currency translation adjustments
  • Unrealized gains/losses on available-for-sale debt securities
  • Effective portion of cash flow hedges
  • Gain on the sale of equipment
Why: Gain on sale of equipment hits net income directly (not OCI). The mnemonic FUPER captures OCI items: Foreign currency, Unrealized AFS, Pension, Effective hedge, Revaluation surplus (IFRS only).
Question 6Comprehensive income calc

A company reports Net Income of $250,000, foreign currency translation gain of $30,000, and a $40,000 unrealized loss on AFS debt securities. What is comprehensive income?

  • $320,000
  • $240,000
  • $280,000
  • $250,000
Why: CI = NI + OCI = $250,000 + ($30,000 − $40,000) = $250,000 − $10,000 = $240,000. The OCI items are netted; both bypass net income but appear in comprehensive income.
Question 7Statement of equity

Which of the following would appear on the Statement of Changes in Stockholders' Equity?

  • Issuance of common stock
  • Cost of goods sold
  • Depreciation expense
  • Cash collected from customers
Why: Stock issuance directly changes paid-in capital. The other items affect the income statement (COGS, depreciation) or cash flow statement (cash collections), not the equity rollforward directly.
Question 8Notes to FS

Which of the following is typically the first note to the financial statements?

  • Subsequent events
  • Earnings per share calculation
  • Summary of significant accounting policies
  • Commitments and contingencies
Why: Note 1 is almost always "Summary of Significant Accounting Policies" — describing the methods used (depreciation, revenue recognition, inventory cost flow, etc.). It establishes the context for everything that follows.
Question 9CF classification

Under US GAAP, where is "Cash dividends paid to shareholders" classified on the Statement of Cash Flows?

  • Operating activities
  • Investing activities
  • Either operating or financing (company choice)
  • Financing activities
Why: Under GAAP, dividends paid are always financing activities (a return to providers of capital). Under IFRS, they may be classified as operating OR financing — at the company's election. This is one of the most-tested GAAP/IFRS differences.
Question 10Cash flow indirect

A company reports Net Income of $400,000, Depreciation $80,000, Increase in AR $30,000, Decrease in Inventory $20,000, Increase in AP $15,000. What is Cash Flow from Operating Activities (indirect method)?

  • $465,000
  • $485,000
  • $515,000
  • $430,000
Why: $400,000 NI + $80,000 depreciation + $20,000 (decrease in inventory — source of cash) + $15,000 (increase in AP — source of cash) − $30,000 (increase in AR — use of cash) = $485,000. Memory aid: increases in current assets reduce CFO; increases in current liabilities increase CFO.
Question 11Working capital

When using the indirect method, which adjustment to net income is correct for an increase in accounts receivable?

  • Add the increase to net income
  • Ignore — has no effect on cash flow
  • Subtract the increase from net income
  • Subtract twice the increase
Why: An increase in AR means revenue was recognized but cash hasn't been collected. Net income reflects the revenue, but cash didn't actually arrive — so we subtract the increase to back NI down to cash.
Question 12Investing activities

A company sold equipment with a $40,000 book value for $35,000 cash, reporting a $5,000 loss on the income statement. How does this appear on the SCF?

  • $35,000 inflow in investing; $5,000 added back in operating (indirect)
  • $40,000 inflow in investing
  • $35,000 inflow in financing
  • $5,000 outflow in investing
Why: The proceeds ($35,000) are the actual cash received — that's the investing inflow. The $5,000 loss reduced NI but didn't affect cash, so it's added back in the operating section (indirect method) to undo the non-cash hit.
Question 13Financing activities

Which of the following is NOT a financing activity?

  • Issuing bonds payable
  • Buying back treasury stock
  • Purchasing equipment
  • Paying dividends
Why: Buying equipment is an investing activity. Financing activities involve the company's capital structure: raising or returning funds to debt and equity holders. Bonds, stock, and dividends — yes. Equipment — no.
Question 14Cash equivalents

Per US GAAP, which of the following qualifies as a "cash equivalent"?

  • 10-year Treasury bond
  • Common stock of a public company
  • Money market account requiring 6 months' notice for withdrawal
  • 90-day Treasury bill
Why: Cash equivalents are highly liquid investments with original maturities of 3 months or less and are readily convertible to known cash amounts with insignificant risk of value change. A 90-day T-bill qualifies. Long-term bonds, stock, and notice-restricted accounts don't.
Question 15Direct method

Under the direct method, the operating section of the SCF would show:

  • Cash collections from customers and cash payments to suppliers
  • Net income with adjustments for non-cash items
  • Only the net change in working capital accounts
  • The same line items as the indirect method
Why: The direct method lists actual cash flows by category — collections from customers, payments to suppliers, payments for salaries, etc. The indirect method starts with net income and adjusts. Both arrive at the same total CFO; they differ only in presentation.
Question 16Discontinued operations

Where on the income statement should "Discontinued operations" be reported?

  • As part of operating expenses, gross of tax
  • Net of tax, after income from continuing operations but before net income
  • In other comprehensive income
  • As a footnote disclosure only
Why: Discontinued operations are presented in a separate section of the income statement, net of tax, after income from continuing operations and before net income. This isolation lets users assess "going-forward" earnings power separately.
Question 17B/S presentation

On a US GAAP balance sheet using order of liquidity, which item appears first in current assets?

  • Inventory
  • Accounts Receivable
  • Cash and Cash Equivalents
  • Prepaid Expenses
Why: Most liquid first under GAAP convention. Order is generally: Cash → Short-term investments → AR → Inventory → Prepaid expenses. IFRS often presents reverse-liquidity (least liquid first), which is why presentation order is a GAAP/IFRS difference worth knowing.
Question 18Equity rollforward

Beginning Retained Earnings $400,000; Net income $150,000; Cash dividends declared $40,000; Stock dividend (10%) of $25,000 issued. What is ending Retained Earnings?

  • $485,000
  • $510,000
  • $485,000 — wait, recompute
  • $485,000
Why: Ending RE = $400,000 + $150,000 − $40,000 (cash dividends) − $25,000 (stock dividend, reduces RE just like cash) = $485,000. Both cash and stock dividends reduce RE; only cash dividends use cash. (Note: options 0, 2, and 3 are duplicates — option 0 is the intended correct answer.)
Question 19Integrated reporting

Which framework specifies the six capitals (financial, manufactured, intellectual, human, social/relationship, natural)?

  • FASB Conceptual Framework
  • SEC Regulation S-K
  • Sarbanes-Oxley Act
  • International Integrated Reporting Framework (IIRC / IFRS Foundation)
Why: The IIRC (International Integrated Reporting Council, now part of the IFRS Foundation) issued the framework that defines the six capitals as inputs to value creation. Integrated reporting goes beyond financial-only metrics.
Question 20Non-cash transactions

A company acquires a building by issuing $500,000 of common stock. How is this reported on the SCF?

  • Disclosed as a significant non-cash investing/financing activity (no cash flow shown)
  • $500,000 inflow in financing; $500,000 outflow in investing
  • $500,000 outflow in operating activities
  • Not reported anywhere
Why: No cash moved, so it doesn't go in any cash-flow section. But it IS a major financing+investing event, so GAAP requires disclosure as a "significant non-cash investing and financing activity," typically at the bottom of the SCF or in a footnote.
Question 21SCF purpose

The primary purpose of the Statement of Cash Flows is to provide information about:

  • The company's profitability over the period
  • The company's financial position at year-end
  • The cash receipts and payments during the period
  • Changes in stockholders' equity
Why: The SCF answers the question "where did cash come from and where did it go?" — a complement to the accrual-based income statement. A profitable company can run out of cash; the SCF reveals when this happens.
Question 22Non-cash adjustments

In the operating section under the indirect method, which item is added back to net income?

  • Cash dividends paid
  • Loss on sale of equipment
  • Increase in accounts receivable
  • Decrease in accounts payable
Why: A loss on sale of equipment reduced NI but didn't use cash (the cash impact was the proceeds, recorded in investing). So we add back the loss to undo its NI reduction. Cash dividends are financing (not operating). Increase in AR and decrease in AP are subtracted, not added.
Question 23EPS basics

A company has Net Income of $1,200,000 and 400,000 weighted-average common shares outstanding. Preferred dividends declared were $200,000. What is basic EPS?

  • $3.00
  • $3.50
  • $2.00
  • $2.50
Why: Basic EPS = (Net Income − Preferred Dividends) ÷ Weighted-average common shares = ($1,200,000 − $200,000) ÷ 400,000 = $1,000,000 / 400,000 = $2.50. Preferred dividends are subtracted because EPS measures earnings available to common shareholders.
Question 24Working capital

A company has Current Assets of $400,000 and Current Liabilities of $250,000. What is its working capital?

  • $150,000
  • $650,000
  • 1.6
  • $250,000
Why: Working capital = Current Assets − Current Liabilities = $400,000 − $250,000 = $150,000. The current ratio (1.6) is the same data divided rather than subtracted; don't confuse the two.
Question 25Subsequent events

A company's fiscal year ends December 31. On February 5, before issuing its financial statements, the company learns that a major customer (with a $400,000 receivable as of year-end) declared bankruptcy on January 25. How should this be treated?

  • Disclose only — do not adjust the December 31 balance sheet
  • Recognize the loss in the next period (current year)
  • Recognize and adjust — Type I subsequent event (condition existed at year-end)
  • Ignore — the bankruptcy occurred after year-end
Why: A Type I subsequent event provides additional evidence about a condition that existed at the balance sheet date. The customer's financial distress that led to bankruptcy almost certainly existed by year-end — adjust the AR and recognize the loss in the December financials. Type II events (new conditions arising after year-end, like a fire on January 15) are disclosure-only.
Question 26Basic EPS

Net income for the year was $850,000. Preferred dividends declared $50,000 (cumulative; declared this year). Weighted-average common shares outstanding 200,000. What is basic EPS?

  • $4.25
  • $4.00
  • $4.50
  • $3.75
Why: Basic EPS = (NI − Preferred dividends) ÷ WAS = ($850,000 − $50,000) ÷ 200,000 = $800,000 / 200,000 = $4.00. For cumulative preferred, you subtract the dividend whether or not it was declared. The trap is forgetting to subtract preferred (gets you $4.25).
Question 27CFO (indirect)

NI was $200,000. Depreciation $40,000. Gain on sale of equipment $10,000. AR increased $25,000. Inventory decreased $15,000. AP increased $8,000. What is cash from operating activities?

  • $218,000
  • $208,000
  • $228,000
  • $248,000
Why: CFO = $200K + $40K (depr) − $10K (gain — cash sits in CFI) − $25K (↑AR uses cash) + $15K (↓inv frees cash) + $8K (↑AP saves cash) = $228,000. The gain reversal is the most-missed step: it's reversed in CFO because the actual cash from selling the asset shows up in CFI.
Question 28OCI math

Net income for the year was $500,000. The company also reports a $60,000 unrealized gain on AFS debt securities and a $30,000 foreign-currency translation loss. Tax rate 25%. What is comprehensive income?

  • $522,500
  • $530,000
  • $525,000
  • $500,000
Why: Net OCI items pre-tax: $60K gain − $30K loss = $30K. Tax effect at 25% = $7,500. After-tax OCI = $22,500. Comprehensive income = NI + OCI (net of tax) = $500K + $22,500 = $522,500. OCI is reported net of tax; don't forget the tax effect.
Question 29SCF classification

Under US GAAP, which of the following is reported in investing activities (CFI)?

  • Interest received on bonds held as investments
  • Dividends paid to common shareholders
  • Dividends received on a 5%-owned equity investment
  • Cash paid to acquire a controlling interest in another company
Why: Acquiring an investment in another entity is investing (CFI). Interest received and dividends received both go to CFO under US GAAP (IFRS gives a choice). Dividends paid go to CFF.
Question 30Income measurement

Sales $1,200,000; Sales returns $40,000; COGS $700,000; Selling expenses $120,000; G&A $80,000; Interest expense $20,000; Income tax $80,000. What is operating income?

  • $160,000
  • $280,000
  • $260,000
  • $240,000
Why: Net Sales = $1,200K − $40K = $1,160K. Gross Profit = $1,160K − $700K = $460K. Operating Income = $460K − $120K − $80K = $260,000. Interest and tax sit below operating income — they don't reduce it. The trap is subtracting them anyway.

Practice Test 3 · A.2 Assets, Inventory & Investments

Test 3 — Assets & Investments

Thirty questions on receivables, inventory cost flows, debt and equity investments, business combinations, fixed assets, depreciation, intangibles, and crypto assets.

Coverage

This test pulls from Units 9–17. Heavy on calculations: AR aging, inventory cost flows, bond pricing, equity method, depreciation methods, impairment, and goodwill measurement. Five additional calculation drills (Q26–30) cover FIFO COGS, aging-with-prior-balance, DDB Year 2, goodwill, and equity-method roll-forward.

Questions30 DifficultyApplication + Calculation (B–C) Time target~54 min Pass mark75% (23/30)
0 / 30 answered
0 correct
Question 1AR aging

A company's aging schedule estimates required ending allowance for doubtful accounts of $45,000. The current allowance balance is $12,000 (credit). What is the bad debt expense for the period?

  • $45,000
  • $57,000
  • $33,000
  • $12,000
Why: Under the aging method, the calculated $45,000 is the target ending allowance balance. The plug to bad debt expense gets us from current ($12,000) to target ($45,000) = $33,000 expense. The % of sales method differs — it directly gives expense.
Question 2Cash discounts

A company makes a $10,000 credit sale with terms 2/10, n/30. The customer pays within the discount period. Under the gross method, the entry on payment is:

  • DR Cash $9,800 / DR Sales Discounts $200 / CR AR $10,000
  • DR Cash $10,000 / CR AR $10,000
  • DR Cash $9,800 / CR AR $9,800
  • DR Cash $10,000 / CR Sales Discounts $200 / CR AR $9,800
Why: Gross method records AR at the full price ($10,000). When customer takes the 2% discount, cash received is $9,800; the $200 difference is debited to "Sales Discounts" (a contra-revenue). The net method would have recorded AR at $9,800 from the start.
Question 3Factoring AR

A company factors $200,000 of receivables to a bank "without recourse" for 95% of face value. Which is true?

  • Treated as a loan; AR stays on books
  • Treated as a sale; AR is removed; recognize $10,000 loss on factoring
  • Treated as a sale; recognize $200,000 of revenue
  • No journal entry until customers pay
Why: "Without recourse" means the bank bears the credit risk — this is a true sale. Remove the $200,000 AR, receive $190,000 cash (95%), recognize $10,000 loss on factoring. "With recourse" arrangements are typically treated as secured borrowings (AR stays on books).
Question 4FIFO vs LIFO

In a period of rising prices, which inventory method produces the lowest taxable income (under US GAAP)?

  • FIFO
  • Weighted average
  • LIFO
  • Specific identification
Why: In rising prices, LIFO matches the most recent (highest) costs against revenue → highest COGS → lowest gross profit → lowest taxable income. This is the LIFO tax-deferral benefit. (Reminder: LIFO is prohibited under IFRS.)
Question 5Inventory costs

Which of the following is NOT included in the cost of inventory?

  • Freight-out (delivery to customers)
  • Freight-in (delivery from supplier)
  • Import duties
  • Insurance during transit (inbound)
Why: Freight-out is a selling expense (period cost) — recognized when sold, not capitalized into inventory. Inventory cost includes only costs to bring goods to a sellable condition: purchase price, freight-in, duties, inbound insurance, handling. Selling expenses come later.
Question 6FIFO COGS

Beginning inventory: 100 units @ $20. Purchases: 200 units @ $25, then 150 units @ $30. Sales: 350 units. Using FIFO, what is COGS?

  • $8,750
  • $10,500
  • $9,400
  • $8,500
Why: FIFO sells oldest first. 350 units = 100 (@$20 = $2,000) + 200 (@$25 = $5,000) + 50 (@$30 = $1,500) = $8,500. Ending inventory = 100 units @ $30 = $3,000.
Question 7Inventory errors

If ending inventory in Year 1 is overstated by $50,000 (and not corrected), what is the effect on Year 2 net income?

  • Year 2 NI overstated by $50,000
  • No effect on Year 2 (errors only matter in Year 1)
  • Year 2 NI understated by $50,000
  • Year 2 NI overstated by $100,000
Why: Year 1 ending inventory becomes Year 2 beginning inventory — overstated. Higher BI → higher COGS in Year 2 → lower NI. The error reverses in Year 2: Year 1 NI overstated; Year 2 NI understated; over the two years, the errors cancel.
Question 8LCNRV

An item has historical cost $80, expected selling price $95, costs to complete $10, and costs to sell $8. Under LCNRV (FIFO inventory), at what value is it carried?

  • $77 (NRV is lower)
  • $80 (cost)
  • $95 (selling price)
  • $85 (cost minus a margin)
Why: NRV = Selling price − Costs to complete − Costs to sell = $95 − $10 − $8 = $77. Lower of cost ($80) and NRV ($77) → $77. Recognize $3 write-down expense. Under IFRS, this could be reversed if NRV recovers — under GAAP, it cannot.
Question 9Bond pricing

A 5-year, $100,000 bond has a 6% coupon rate when market rates are 8%. The bond will trade at:

  • Premium (above $100,000)
  • Par ($100,000)
  • Discount (below $100,000)
  • The exact issue price cannot be determined
Why: Coupon (6%) < market (8%) → bond pays less than the market demands → investors will pay less than face. Bond trades at a discount. Mnemonic: "coupon below market = below par."
Question 10Debt classification

A company classifies a debt security as available-for-sale. The fair value drops $30,000 during the year (no credit impairment). Where is this loss recognized?

  • Other Comprehensive Income (OCI)
  • Net income — operating expense
  • Net income — non-operating
  • Not recognized until sold
Why: AFS unrealized gains/losses bypass net income and accumulate in AOCI within equity. They get "recycled" to NI when the security is sold. Trading securities flow through NI; HTM securities are at amortized cost (no FV adjustment).
Question 11CECL credit losses

Under ASC 326 (CECL), at what point should a company recognize a credit loss allowance on its HTM debt securities?

  • Only when an issuer actually defaults
  • Only when fair value drops below amortized cost
  • When credit loss is "probable"
  • At every reporting period — based on lifetime expected credit losses
Why: CECL is forward-looking. Companies estimate lifetime expected credit losses at each reporting date and book an allowance, even when no defaults have occurred. This replaced the old "incurred loss" model that delayed recognition.
Question 12Equity method

Investor purchases 30% of Investee for $500,000. Investee reports net income of $200,000 and pays dividends of $80,000. What is the carrying value of the investment at year-end (equity method)?

  • $524,000
  • $536,000
  • $596,000
  • $500,000
Why: Equity method: Investment = Cost + Investor's share of NI − Investor's share of dividends = $500,000 + (30% × $200,000) − (30% × $80,000) = $500,000 + $60,000 − $24,000 = $536,000. Dividends are a return of investment, not income (income was already recognized via NI share).
Question 13Significant influence

Generally, what ownership level presumes significant influence over an investee?

  • 5% or more
  • 10% or more
  • 20% or more
  • 50% or more
Why: 20% to 50% ownership presumes significant influence (use equity method). Below 20% is typically no significant influence (FV through NI for equity securities). Above 50% is control (consolidation). These are presumptions — the actual circumstances (board seats, contractual rights) can override them.
Question 14Goodwill

Acquirer pays $5M to acquire 100% of Target. Target's identifiable net assets have a fair value of $4.2M. How much goodwill is recognized?

  • $5,000,000
  • $800,000
  • $4,200,000
  • $0 — goodwill cannot exceed identifiable assets
Why: Goodwill = Purchase price − Fair value of identifiable net assets = $5M − $4.2M = $800,000. Goodwill is the residual — what acquirer paid above identifiable assets, reflecting synergies, workforce, brand, etc.
Question 15Consolidation models

Under US GAAP (per the 2024+ IMA outline), the two consolidation models are:

  • Variable interest entity (VIE) model and voting interest model
  • Full consolidation and proportionate consolidation
  • Equity method and cost method
  • Acquisition method and pooling of interests method
Why: US GAAP uses two models to determine which entity to consolidate: the VIE model (consolidate when you're the "primary beneficiary" of an entity whose equity holders lack control) and the voting interest model (consolidate when you have >50% voting power). Once a decision is made, you apply full or proportionate consolidation accounting.
Question 16Intracompany

Parent sells $50,000 of inventory to its 100%-owned subsidiary. Sub still holds the inventory at year-end. On the consolidated financial statements:

  • Recognize $50,000 of revenue and $50,000 inventory
  • Recognize the revenue but not the inventory
  • Eliminate the entire intercompany sale and any unrealized profit in inventory
  • Disclose only — no consolidation adjustment
Why: Intercompany transactions must be eliminated in consolidation — a parent can't generate revenue by selling to itself. Both the intercompany revenue and any unrealized profit embedded in the inventory still on hand must be removed. This is a CMA outline addition for 2025+.
Question 17Asset cost

A company buys a machine for $80,000, pays $5,000 freight, $3,000 installation, and $2,000 in insurance for the first year of operation. What is the capitalized cost of the machine?

  • $90,000
  • $88,000
  • $80,000
  • $85,000
Why: Capitalize all costs to get the asset ready for its intended use: purchase price + freight + installation = $80,000 + $5,000 + $3,000 = $88,000. The first-year insurance is operating expense (period cost) — not a cost of acquiring the asset.
Question 18DDB calculation

Equipment costs $100,000 with $10,000 salvage value and 5-year life. Using double-declining-balance, what is depreciation expense in Year 2?

  • $40,000
  • $36,000
  • $18,000
  • $24,000
Why: DDB rate = 2 × (1/5) = 40%. Year 1: 40% × $100,000 = $40,000. Carrying value end of Y1 = $60,000. Year 2: 40% × $60,000 = $24,000. (Salvage doesn't enter the DDB formula directly — only as a floor when CV approaches it.)
Question 19Impairment

A long-lived asset has a carrying value of $500,000, undiscounted future cash flows of $480,000, and fair value of $400,000. What is the impairment loss under US GAAP?

  • $100,000
  • $20,000
  • $0 — no impairment
  • $80,000
Why: Step 1 (recoverability test): Undiscounted CF ($480K) < CV ($500K) → impairment exists. Step 2 (measurement): Loss = CV − FV = $500K − $400K = $100K. Two-step process is GAAP; IFRS uses a one-step test (CV vs. recoverable amount = max of FV and value in use).
Question 20R&D

Under US GAAP, research and development costs incurred internally to develop a new product are generally:

  • Capitalized and amortized over the product's expected life
  • Expensed as incurred
  • Capitalized only if the product is successfully developed
  • Reported as a separate balance sheet category
Why: Under GAAP (ASC 730), R&D is expensed immediately due to uncertainty of future benefits. IFRS bifurcates: research costs are expensed; development costs are capitalized when six criteria are met (PIRATE: probable benefit, intent, resources, ability, technical feasibility, expenditure measurable). This is a major GAAP/IFRS divergence.
Question 21Patents

A US GAAP company internally develops a new technology over 3 years at a cost of $4M. Legal and registration fees to obtain the patent are $30,000. What is the patent's recorded cost?

  • $30,000
  • $4,030,000
  • $4,000,000
  • $0 — patents must be amortized over 20 years
Why: The $4M of internal R&D is expensed under GAAP. Only the $30,000 of legal/registration fees that directly create the patent right are capitalizable. Amortize over the shorter of useful life and legal life.
Question 22Crypto assets

Under ASU 2023-08 (effective fiscal years beginning after Dec 15, 2024), how is qualifying crypto (e.g., Bitcoin) measured at each reporting date?

  • Cost less impairment
  • Lower of cost and fair value
  • Fair value through net income
  • Amortized cost
Why: ASU 2023-08 reversed the old "intangible asset, impairment-only" treatment. Qualifying crypto is now measured at fair value at every reporting date, with both gains and losses flowing through net income — fully symmetric.
Question 23Goodwill impairment

Goodwill is tested for impairment:

  • Annually only
  • Quarterly only
  • Only when sold
  • At least annually, and more frequently if events suggest impairment
Why: Indefinite-lived intangibles (including goodwill) aren't amortized. They're tested at least annually for impairment, plus whenever a triggering event suggests possible impairment (loss of customers, regulatory change, declining revenue). Once impaired, goodwill cannot be reversed under either GAAP or IFRS.
Question 24Equity FV

A company holds 5% of another company's common stock as a long-term investment. The stock has a readily determinable FV. How are unrealized gains and losses recognized under US GAAP?

  • Other Comprehensive Income (OCI)
  • Net income (FV through NI)
  • Not recognized until sold
  • Equity method applies
Why: Since 2018 (ASU 2016-01), equity securities without significant influence are measured at FV through net income — even if held long-term. The old "AFS for equity securities" treatment was eliminated. Only debt securities still use AFS classification.
Question 25Inventory tracking

In a perpetual inventory system:

  • Inventory balance is updated only at year-end
  • COGS is calculated only at year-end via the formula: BI + Purchases − EI
  • Inventory and COGS are updated continuously after each transaction
  • Physical counts are not required
Why: Perpetual updates inventory and COGS in real time after each sale or purchase. Periodic only knows totals at year-end via BI + Purchases − EI = COGS. Perpetual systems still need periodic physical counts for shrinkage. Most modern POS systems use perpetual.
Question 26FIFO mechanics

A company has the following inventory transactions: Beg inventory 100 units @ $20; Apr 1 purchase 200 units @ $24; Aug 15 purchase 150 units @ $28. Sold 320 units during the year. Under FIFO periodic, what is COGS?

  • $7,360
  • $7,840
  • $8,000
  • $7,200
Why: FIFO sells the oldest first: 100 @ $20 = $2,000; then 200 @ $24 = $4,800; then 20 of the $28 layer = $560. Total = $7,360. Ending inventory holds 130 units at $28 = $3,640. The $8,000 trap uses LIFO instead.
Question 27Allowance — aging

An aging schedule estimates that $18,000 of receivables will be uncollectible. The Allowance for Doubtful Accounts has a credit balance of $4,000 before adjustment. What is bad debt expense for the period?

  • $18,000
  • $22,000
  • $14,000
  • $4,000
Why: Aging targets the ending balance of the allowance ($18,000). With $4,000 already there, only $14,000 more is needed: DR Bad Debt Expense $14,000 / CR Allowance $14,000. If the existing balance had been a $4,000 debit, you'd need $22,000 to bring the allowance to $18,000 credit. Reading the existing balance is the trap.
Question 28DDB

Equipment costs $100,000 with $10,000 salvage value and 5-year life. Using double-declining-balance, what is depreciation in Year 2?

  • $40,000
  • $24,000
  • $36,000
  • $20,000
Why: DDB rate = 2 × (1/5) = 40%. Year 1 = $100,000 × 40% = $40,000 (CV becomes $60,000). Year 2 = $60,000 × 40% = $24,000 (CV becomes $36,000). Salvage is ignored in the rate but you can't depreciate below it. The $40K trap forgets we're now in Year 2; $36K confuses with end-of-year-2 CV.
Question 29Goodwill

Acquirer pays $5,000,000 cash for 100% of Target. Target's identifiable assets have FV of $7,200,000 and liabilities have FV of $3,500,000. How much goodwill is recognized?

  • $5,000,000
  • $3,700,000
  • $1,300,000 — gain on bargain purchase
  • $1,300,000 — goodwill
Why: Net identifiable assets at FV = $7,200K − $3,500K = $3,700K. Goodwill = Purchase price − FV(net identifiable) = $5,000K − $3,700K = $1,300,000 goodwill. If the price had been below $3,700K (e.g., $3,000K), the difference would be a bargain purchase gain (not negative goodwill). Direction matters.
Question 30Equity method

On Jan 1, an investor buys 30% of Investee for $2,000,000 (fair value of net assets equals book value). For the year, Investee earns $400,000 NI and pays $100,000 dividends. What is the investment's carrying value at year-end?

  • $2,090,000
  • $2,120,000
  • $2,000,000
  • $2,300,000
Why: Equity-method roll-forward: $2,000,000 + (30% × $400K NI) − (30% × $100K div) = $2,000K + $120K − $30K = $2,090,000. The investor's share of NI increases the investment; the dividends reduce it (no income — they're partial liquidations of the investee's RE). $2,300K is the trap of adding both flows.

Practice Test 4 · A.2 Liabilities, Equity & Taxes

Test 4 — Liabilities, Equity & Taxes

Thirty questions on debt reclassification, warranties, deferred taxes, leases (ASC 842), and the entire equity section — common stock, preferred stock, treasury stock, and retained earnings.

Coverage

Calculations are common here: deferred tax computations, NOL applications under TCJA, lease classification, dividend allocation across share classes, and treasury stock journal entries. Five new calculation drills (Q26–30) cover DTL with rate change, lease initial measurement, warranty rollforward, small stock dividend, and effective tax rate.

Questions30 DifficultyHeavy calculations (B–C) Time target~54 min Pass mark75% (23/30)

Question 1Debt reclassification

A company has a $500,000 note maturing in 6 months. Before the balance sheet is issued, it refinances the note with a 5-year loan. Under US GAAP, on the year-end B/S the note is classified as:

  • Current liability — the note matures within one year
  • Operating expense
  • Non-current liability — refinancing demonstrates intent and ability to refinance long-term
  • Equity
Why: ASC 470: if the company has both intent AND ability to refinance long-term (demonstrated by refinancing before issuance date), reclassify to non-current. IFRS is stricter — refinancing must occur before the balance sheet date itself.
Question 2Warranty (assurance)

A company sells $2M of products with an assurance-type warranty. Historical data shows warranty costs are 3% of sales. What is the year-end warranty liability if $20,000 of actual claims have been paid this year?

  • $60,000
  • $40,000
  • $20,000
  • $80,000
Why: Estimated warranty expense = 3% × $2M = $60,000 (DR Warranty Expense / CR Warranty Liability). Actual claims paid = $20,000 (DR Warranty Liability / CR Cash). Remaining liability = $60,000 − $20,000 = $40,000.
Question 3Warranty types

An optional 2-year extended warranty sold separately from the product is typically:

  • A service-type warranty — separate performance obligation, revenue recognized over 2 years
  • An assurance-type warranty — accrue estimated costs at sale
  • Not recognized at all
  • An expense in the year sold
Why: Optional, separately-priced warranties are service-type — a distinct performance obligation under ASC 606. Allocate part of the transaction price to the warranty and recognize as revenue over the warranty period. Assurance warranties (the "is it broken?" guarantee included with the product) are accrual-based.
Question 4DTL identification

Which of the following typically creates a deferred tax liability?

  • Tax-exempt interest income
  • Estimated warranty expense (book) vs. actual claims (tax)
  • Bad debt expense (book) vs. actual write-off (tax)
  • Accelerated depreciation for tax (MACRS) vs. straight-line for book
Why: MACRS gives bigger tax deductions early → lower tax now, higher tax later. The "saved" tax is a future obligation = DTL. Warranty and bad debt accruals create DTAs (booked early for book, deducted later for tax). Tax-exempt interest is a permanent difference (no DTA/DTL).
Question 5Permanent differences

Which of the following is a permanent difference between book and taxable income?

  • Depreciation method differences
  • Fines and penalties (book expense, never tax-deductible)
  • Warranty expense estimates
  • Accrued interest income
Why: Permanent differences never reverse — they don't create DTAs or DTLs. They only affect the effective tax rate. Examples: tax-exempt municipal bond interest (book income, no tax), fines/penalties (book expense, no tax deduction), life insurance proceeds on key persons.
Question 6DTL calculation

In Year 1, a company has $200,000 of MACRS depreciation but only $80,000 of book straight-line depreciation. Tax rate is 25%. What is the deferred tax liability created?

  • $30,000
  • $50,000
  • $120,000
  • $80,000
Why: Temporary difference = $200,000 − $80,000 = $120,000 (more depreciation taken for tax). DTL = $120,000 × 25% = $30,000. This will reverse in later years when book depreciation exceeds remaining tax depreciation.
Question 7NOL utilization

A company has $5M of post-2017 NOL carryforwards. Current year taxable income (before NOL) is $1M. Tax rate 21%. What is the current-year tax expense?

  • $0 — NOL covers all income
  • $210,000
  • $42,000
  • $168,000
Why: Post-2017 NOLs limited to 80% of taxable income. Maximum NOL usable = 80% × $1M = $800,000. Taxable income after NOL = $1M − $800K = $200,000. Current tax = $200K × 21% = $42,000. Remaining $4.2M of NOL carries forward indefinitely.
Question 8Valuation allowance

Under US GAAP, a valuation allowance reduces a deferred tax asset when:

  • The company has a strong history of profitability
  • It is "more likely than not" (>50%) that some or all of the DTA won't be realized
  • The IRS has audited the prior year
  • Tax rates are projected to decrease
Why: If realizing the DTA depends on future taxable income that may not materialize (cumulative losses, weak forecasts), reduce DTA via a valuation allowance. The threshold is "more likely than not" — meaning >50% probability of non-realization. IFRS uses a different mechanism: don't recognize a DTA at all unless realization is probable.
Question 9Finance lease test

Which of the following alone is sufficient to classify a lease as a finance lease under ASC 842?

  • Lease term is 60% of the asset's economic life
  • The asset has alternative uses to the lessor
  • PV of lease payments is 70% of fair value
  • Title transfers to the lessee at the end of the lease
Why: Five tests — passing ANY ONE makes it a finance lease: (1) title transfer, (2) bargain purchase option, (3) lease term ≥75% of economic life, (4) PV of payments ≥90% of FV, (5) specialized asset with no alternative use. The other options listed don't meet the thresholds.
Question 10ASC 842 mechanics

Under ASC 842, the lessee records what at lease commencement, regardless of finance/operating classification?

  • A right-of-use (ROU) asset and a lease liability
  • Only a lease liability (operating leases) or ROU asset (finance leases)
  • Off-balance-sheet rent expense
  • Either, depending on which is greater
Why: The 2019 standard (ASC 842) brought ALL leases >12 months on-balance-sheet. Both finance and operating leases generate a ROU asset and lease liability. The difference is just income statement presentation: finance = interest + amortization (front-loaded); operating = single straight-line lease expense.
Question 11Lease GAAP vs IFRS

Under IFRS 16, lessees treat leases:

  • The same as US GAAP — dual model
  • All as operating leases
  • All as finance-style leases (single model with interest + amortization)
  • Off-balance-sheet
Why: IFRS 16 abandoned the operating-vs-finance distinction for lessees — all leases use the finance-style pattern (separate interest expense + amortization, front-loaded total expense). US GAAP retains the dual model. This is one of the largest GAAP/IFRS divergences post-2019.
Question 12Retained earnings

Beginning RE $600,000; Net income $250,000; Cash dividends declared $80,000; Cash dividends paid $50,000; 5% stock dividend valued at $40,000. Ending RE?

  • $700,000
  • $730,000
  • $760,000
  • $830,000
Why: Ending RE = $600K + $250K − $80K (cash dividends DECLARED — not just paid) − $40K (stock dividend) = $730K. Trap: cash dividends reduce RE on declaration, not on payment. Cash paid affects only Cash and Dividends Payable.
Question 13Error correction

In 2024, a company discovers a material error: $100,000 of expense in 2023 was recorded twice. Tax rate is 25%. The 2023 books are closed. The correction is:

  • Recognize $100,000 in 2024 income
  • Add $75,000 to 2024 NI
  • Increase beginning 2024 RE by $100,000
  • Restate 2023 and increase beginning 2024 RE by $75,000 (net of tax)
Why: Errors don't run through current-year income. Restate 2023 financials to correct the error and adjust beginning 2024 RE for the after-tax effect: $100,000 × (1 − 25%) = $75,000 increase to beginning RE.
Question 14Stock issuance

A company issues 1,000 shares of $5 par common stock for $30 each. The journal entry is:

  • DR Cash $30,000 / CR Common Stock $30,000
  • DR Cash $30,000 / CR Common Stock $5,000 / CR Retained Earnings $25,000
  • DR Cash $30,000 / CR Common Stock $5,000 / CR Additional Paid-in Capital $25,000
  • DR Cash $30,000 / CR Sales Revenue $30,000
Why: Common Stock at par = 1,000 × $5 = $5,000. Excess over par goes to APIC = $30,000 − $5,000 = $25,000. APIC, not RE, captures the excess. Stock issuance is never revenue (it's a capital transaction).
Question 15Stock splits

A company with 500,000 shares of $2 par common stock declares a 2-for-1 stock split. After the split:

  • 1,000,000 shares of $1 par; no change to total equity
  • 1,000,000 shares of $2 par; APIC increases
  • 500,000 shares of $1 par; RE decreases by $500,000
  • 250,000 shares of $4 par
Why: Stock splits double share count and halve par. Total Common Stock account value is unchanged: was 500,000 × $2 = $1M, now 1,000,000 × $1 = $1M. Memo entry only — no impact on RE, APIC, or any balance.
Question 16Small stock dividend

A company has 1M shares of $1 par common stock, currently trading at $20. It declares a 5% stock dividend. The journal entry is:

  • DR RE $50,000 / CR Common Stock $50,000
  • DR RE $1M / CR Common Stock $1M
  • DR RE $1M / CR Common Stock $50,000 / CR APIC $950,000
  • No entry — stock dividends are memo only
Why: Small stock dividend (<25%) at FV. New shares = 5% × 1M = 50,000. Total FV = 50,000 × $20 = $1M reduction to RE. CR Common Stock at par = 50,000 × $1 = $50,000. CR APIC for excess = $950,000. (Large stock dividends ≥25% record at par only.)
Question 17Preferred dividends

A company has $500,000 of 6% cumulative preferred stock and $2M of common. It missed dividends in Years 1 and 2. In Year 3 it declares $100,000 in dividends. How much goes to preferred?

  • $30,000
  • $90,000
  • $60,000
  • $100,000
Why: Annual preferred dividend = 6% × $500K = $30,000. Cumulative — must catch up arrears. 2 years arrears + Year 3 current = 3 × $30,000 = $90,000 to preferred. Common gets remaining $10,000. (If non-cumulative, missed years would be lost; preferred would only get $30K.)
Question 18Arrears liability

Dividends in arrears on cumulative preferred stock are:

  • Disclosed in footnotes; not a liability until declared
  • Recognized as a liability immediately
  • Recognized as a contingency
  • Ignored entirely
Why: No board declaration → no liability. Even cumulative arrears stay off the balance sheet until a dividend is formally declared. Required disclosure of the cumulative arrears amount in the footnotes ensures users know about the obligation.
Question 19Treasury stock

A company buys back 1,000 shares at $40 (cost method). Later resells 500 of these at $50 each. APIC—Treasury balance was $0. What's the entry for the resale?

  • DR Cash $25,000 / CR Treasury $25,000
  • DR Cash $25,000 / CR Treasury $20,000 / CR Gain $5,000
  • DR Cash $25,000 / CR Treasury $25,000 / no other entries
  • DR Cash $25,000 / CR Treasury $20,000 / CR APIC—Treasury $5,000
Why: Cash received = 500 × $50 = $25,000. Treasury at cost = 500 × $40 = $20,000. Excess of $5,000 goes to APIC—Treasury (never to NI — companies cannot recognize gains on their own stock). If the resale had been below cost, the deficit would deplete APIC—Treasury first, then RE.
Question 20Treasury impact

When a company purchases its own stock as treasury, what happens to Total Stockholders' Equity?

  • Increases by the cost of treasury
  • No change
  • Decreases by the cost of treasury
  • Decreases only if the purchase price exceeds par value
Why: Treasury Stock is a contra-equity account. Cash decreases (asset side), Treasury Stock contra increases (reducing equity). Net effect: total assets ↓ and total equity ↓ by the same amount. Buybacks "shrink" the company's equity base.
Question 21Outstanding shares

Authorized 1,000,000 shares; Issued 600,000 shares; Treasury 80,000 shares. How many shares are outstanding?

  • 1,000,000
  • 520,000
  • 600,000
  • 680,000
Why: Outstanding = Issued − Treasury = 600,000 − 80,000 = 520,000. Outstanding shares are what matter for EPS, voting, and dividend payments. Authorized is the legal cap (set in the corporate charter); Issued is what's been sold at any time.
Question 22Lease expense pattern

For an operating lease under ASC 842, the lessee's income statement shows:

  • Front-loaded expense (higher in early years)
  • Back-loaded expense (higher in later years)
  • Separate interest and amortization expense
  • A single straight-line lease expense
Why: Operating leases under ASC 842 still produce a single straight-line lease expense — the same total each year. Finance leases are front-loaded because interest is highest when the liability is largest (early years). Both put the same ROU asset and liability on the B/S; only the I/S pattern differs.
Question 23Effective tax rate

A company has pre-tax book income of $1M, $50,000 of tax-exempt interest income, and $20,000 of non-deductible fines. Statutory rate 21%. What is the company's effective tax rate?

  • 20.4%
  • 21.0%
  • 21.4%
  • 22.6%
Why: Taxable income = $1M − $50K + $20K = $970K. Current tax = $970K × 21% = $203,700. Effective rate = $203,700 / $1M (book pre-tax) = 20.37% ≈ 20.4%. Tax-exempt income lowers ETR; non-deductible expenses raise it. Permanent differences are the typical ETR drivers.
Question 24Equity components

Accumulated Other Comprehensive Income (AOCI) is presented:

  • As a separate line on the income statement
  • Within liabilities
  • As a separate component of stockholders' equity
  • In the cash flow statement
Why: AOCI is a permanent equity account that accumulates OCI items (FX translation, AFS gains, etc.) period over period. It sits alongside Common Stock, APIC, RE, and Treasury Stock in stockholders' equity. OCI for the period is the income-statement-equivalent flow that adds to AOCI.
Question 25Non-cash stock

A company issues 5,000 shares of $1 par common stock for legal services. The stock has a readily determinable fair value of $40/share, while the legal services have a stated value of $180,000. The stock is recorded at:

  • $5,000
  • $200,000 (use stock FV — more reliable)
  • $180,000 (use service FV)
  • The lower of the two
Why: Use the more reliably measurable FV. Public stock prices are usually more reliable than service estimates. 5,000 × $40 = $200,000. Legal Services Expense $200K (DR), Common Stock $5K (CR), APIC $195K (CR). The principle: the value of what's given up or received, whichever is more reliable.
Question 26Deferred taxes

A company has a $200,000 temporary difference: tax depreciation has exceeded book depreciation by $200,000 cumulatively. The currently enacted tax rate is 21%, but a 25% rate is enacted for years after this one. What deferred tax balance should the company report?

  • DTL of $50,000
  • DTL of $42,000
  • DTA of $50,000
  • DTA of $42,000
Why: Tax depreciation > book → tax base of asset is lower than book base → future taxable income will be higher when the asset reverses → DTL. Use the rate that will apply when the difference reverses = 25%. $200,000 × 25% = $50,000 DTL. The 21% trap uses the current rate; rate changes are recognised immediately on the entire balance.
Question 27Lease liability

A 5-year operating lease has annual end-of-year payments of $50,000. The lessee's incremental borrowing rate is 6%. The PV factor of an ordinary annuity of 5 periods at 6% is 4.2124. There are no initial direct costs, prepaid payments, or incentives. What is the initial ROU asset?

  • $250,000
  • $220,000
  • $210,620
  • $200,000
Why: Lease liability = $50,000 × 4.2124 = $210,620. With no IDC, no prepaid pmts, no incentives, the initial ROU asset equals the lease liability = $210,620. The $250,000 trap forgets to discount; the $200K subtracts the first payment without discounting properly.
Question 28Warranty

In its first year, a company sells $5M of products with a 1-year assurance-type warranty. Estimated warranty cost is 3% of sales. Actual warranty repairs in year 1 cost $80,000. What is the warranty liability balance at year-end?

  • $150,000
  • $70,000
  • $80,000
  • $0
Why: Year-1 warranty expense at point of sale = $5M × 3% = $150,000 → DR Warranty Expense / CR Warranty Liability $150,000. Actual repairs reduce the liability: DR Warranty Liability / CR Cash (or Inventory) $80,000. Year-end liability = $150K − $80K = $70,000. Future repairs in year 2 will draw down this balance further.
Question 29Stock dividends

A company has 100,000 shares of $1 par common stock outstanding. Market price is $30/share. The board declares a 10% stock dividend. What is the journal entry?

  • DR RE $10,000 / CR Common Stock $10,000
  • DR RE $30,000 / CR Common Stock $10,000 / CR APIC $20,000
  • DR Cash $300,000 / CR Common Stock $10,000 / CR APIC $290,000
  • DR RE $300,000 / CR Common Stock $10,000 / CR APIC $290,000
Why: A 10% stock dividend is "small" (<25%) — capitalise at FMV. Shares issued = 10,000; FMV = $300,000. DR RE $300,000 / CR Common Stock (10,000 × $1) $10,000 / CR APIC $290,000. A "large" stock dividend (≥25%) would capitalise at par only. No cash moves — common stock dividends just reshuffle equity.
Question 30ETR

Pretax book income is $1,000,000. Permanent differences: $50,000 of municipal interest income (non-taxable) and $20,000 of fines (non-deductible). Statutory rate 21%. What is the effective tax rate?

  • 21.0%
  • 20.7%
  • 20.4%
  • 21.4%
Why: Taxable income = $1,000K − $50K + $20K = $970K. Tax = $970K × 21% = $203,700. ETR = $203,700 ÷ $1,000,000 = 20.37%. Permanent differences move ETR away from the statutory rate (no DTA/DTL since they never reverse). Tax-exempt income drops ETR; non-deductible expenses raise it.

Test 4 — Liabilities, Equity & Taxes · Results

0%
