Practice Test 5 · A.2 Revenue & Income

Test 5 — Revenue & Income Measurement

Twenty-five questions on the ASC 606 5-step model, right of return, consignment, long-term contracts, GAAP/IFRS reconciliation, and gain/loss on disposals.

Coverage

Heavy emphasis on the revenue recognition standard and the asymmetric treatments around long-term contract losses, returns, and consignments. Five new calculation drills (Q21–25) cover % of completion, loss-contract reversal, expected-value variable consideration, discontinued-ops measurement, and right-of-return refund liability.

Questions25 DifficultyApplication + GAAP/IFRS (B–C) Time target~45 min Pass mark75% (19/25)
0 / 25 answered
0 correct
Question 15-step model

In the ASC 606 5-step model, what is Step 4?

  • Identify performance obligations
  • Determine the transaction price
  • Allocate the transaction price to performance obligations
  • Recognize revenue when obligations are satisfied
Why: Step 1 identify contract → 2 identify performance obligations → 3 determine transaction price → 4 allocate to obligations → 5 recognize as satisfied. Allocation uses relative standalone selling prices (SSP).
Question 2Distinct goods/services

A telecommunications company sells a phone bundled with 24 months of service for $1,200. A phone alone costs $700; service alone costs $40/month. How many performance obligations are in the contract?

  • One — bundled offering
  • Two — phone (point in time) and service (over time)
  • Three — phone, service, accessories implied
  • Twenty-five — phone plus 24 months
Why: Each performance obligation is a distinct good/service. Phone and service are sold separately, so each is distinct. Two performance obligations. Service is a single performance obligation despite spanning 24 months — the customer benefits continuously.
Question 3SSP allocation

Bundle price $1,200; SSP of phone $800; SSP of service $400. What revenue is allocated to the service?

  • $300
  • $200
  • $400
  • $1,200
Why: SSP-based allocation. Service share = 400/(800+400) = 33.3%. Allocated revenue = 33.3% × $1,200 = $400. Phone gets $800. (In this case the bundle = sum of SSPs, so allocation equals SSP — no implicit discount to allocate.)
Question 4Variable consideration

When a contract includes variable consideration (volume rebates, performance bonuses), the transaction price should:

  • Include the estimated amount, constrained to amounts highly probable not to reverse
  • Be left blank until the variability resolves
  • Use only the maximum possible amount
  • Be recognized only as cash is received
Why: ASC 606 requires estimating variable consideration (expected value or most likely amount) but constrains it to the amount that's highly probable to not reverse. This prevents companies from front-loading uncertain revenue. As estimates change, revenue is updated.
Question 5Principal vs agent

A ride-sharing app facilitates rides between drivers and customers. Customers pay $20 for a ride; the app keeps $5 as a commission. How should the app recognize revenue?

  • $20 — gross basis (principal)
  • $15 — net of payment to driver
  • $25 — gross plus markup
  • $5 — agent commission only (net basis)
Why: The app doesn't control the ride service before transfer (the driver does). It's an agent — recognize only the commission ($5) as revenue. Marketplaces (Uber, Airbnb, eBay) typically recognize fees, not the underlying transaction value.
Question 6Right of return

A retailer sells $200,000 of goods (cost $120,000) with a return policy. Historical experience shows 10% will be returned. What revenue is recognized at sale?

  • $200,000 — book returns when they happen
  • $180,000 — net of estimated returns
  • $80,000 — net of cost
  • $0 — wait for return window to close
Why: Recognize revenue net of expected returns: $200K × 90% = $180K. Also book a $20K refund liability (consideration expected to refund) and a $12K return asset (cost of inventory expected to come back, $120K × 10%). Adjust at each reporting date if estimates change.
Question 7Consignment

Manufacturer ships $500,000 of goods to a retailer on consignment. At year-end, the retailer has sold $300,000 to end customers; $200,000 remains in the retailer's store. On the manufacturer's year-end balance sheet, the unsold consigned goods are:

  • Included in the manufacturer's inventory at $200,000
  • Not on either party's books
  • Included in the retailer's inventory
  • Recognized as deferred revenue by the manufacturer
Why: Title doesn't pass on consignment shipment. The consignor (manufacturer) keeps the goods on its inventory until the consignee sells them to end customers. Manufacturer recognizes revenue only on the $300K sold-through portion; $200K stays in inventory.
Question 8Bill-and-hold

A bill-and-hold arrangement is acceptable for revenue recognition before shipment if all of the following are true EXCEPT:

  • The arrangement has a substantive reason
  • The product is identified as belonging to the customer
  • The seller can redirect the product to another customer at any time
  • The product is ready for physical transfer
Why: All four criteria must be met to recognize revenue before shipment. The seller must NOT have ability to redirect to another customer — that would mean control hasn't really transferred. The other three criteria support that the customer effectively controls the product even though it's still at seller's location.
Question 9Long-term contracts

For over-time revenue recognition under ASC 606, which of the following criteria must be met (any one is sufficient)?

  • Customer takes physical possession at completion
  • The asset has no alternative use AND there's an enforceable right to payment for work to date
  • The contract spans more than 12 months
  • The asset is over $1 million in value
Why: The three over-time tests (any one): (1) customer simultaneously receives/consumes benefits; (2) the entity creates/enhances an asset the customer controls; (3) no alternative use AND enforceable right to payment for performance to date. Time spans and monetary thresholds aren't relevant to the over-time decision.
Question 10% completion calculation

A 4-year construction contract has a total price of $30M and total estimated cost of $25M. By end of Year 2, $15M of costs have been incurred. Revenue recognized in Year 2 (assume Year 1 was $9M revenue):

  • $9,000,000
  • $15,000,000
  • $9,000,000 (cumulative $18M, less prior $9M)
  • $3,000,000
Why: % complete = $15M / $25M = 60%. Cumulative revenue earned = 60% × $30M = $18M. Year 2 revenue = $18M cumulative − $9M previously recognized = $9M. The cost-to-cost method uses inputs to measure progress.
Question 11Loss on contract

A 3-year contract for $10M was estimated to cost $8M (expected $2M profit). After Year 1 ($3M costs incurred), the company revises total cost estimate to $11M. What's the effect in Year 1?

  • Recognize the entire $1M expected loss immediately
  • Spread the $1M loss over remaining 2 years
  • Continue using the original 25% complete and recognize $500K profit
  • Wait until contract completion
Why: When a contract becomes a foreseen loss, recognize the ENTIRE expected loss immediately. Asymmetric treatment: profits spread over the contract; losses recognized at once. Total expected loss = $11M cost − $10M revenue = $1M loss recognized in Year 1.
Question 12LIFO

A US company prepares financial statements under both US GAAP and IFRS. Under which framework is LIFO permitted for inventory?

  • Both
  • Neither
  • US GAAP only
  • IFRS only
Why: LIFO is permitted under US GAAP but prohibited under IFRS. This is one of the most-tested GAAP/IFRS differences. A company using LIFO for tax purposes (LIFO conformity rule) must also use it for book — meaning it cannot also report under IFRS without adjustment.
Question 13Impairment reversal

In Year 1, a company impairs a building by $100,000 (excluding goodwill). In Year 3, conditions improve and the building's recoverable amount fully recovers. Under IFRS:

  • No reversal — impairments are permanent
  • Reverse the impairment in OCI
  • Reverse only if directly traced to operating activities
  • Reverse the impairment up to (but not exceeding) the original carrying value
Why: IFRS allows impairment reversals for non-goodwill assets, but capped at the original carrying value (i.e., never write the asset above original cost less normal depreciation). US GAAP doesn't allow reversals at all (impairment creates a new cost basis). Goodwill impairment is never reversed under either framework.
Question 14R&D — IFRS

Under IFRS, development costs (as opposed to research costs) are:

  • Always expensed as incurred
  • Capitalized when six specific criteria are met
  • Capitalized only if the project is successful
  • Treated the same as US GAAP — always expensed
Why: IFRS bifurcates: research = expense; development = capitalize when 6 PIRATE criteria met (Probable benefit, Intent, Resources, Ability, Technical feasibility, Expenditure measurable). US GAAP expenses both phases. This creates major reporting differences for technology and pharma companies.
Question 15Gain calculation

A company sells equipment for $80,000. Original cost $200,000; accumulated depreciation $150,000. What is the gain or loss?

  • $30,000 gain
  • $50,000 loss
  • $120,000 loss
  • $80,000 gain
Why: Carrying value = $200K − $150K = $50K. Gain/(loss) = Proceeds − CV = $80K − $50K = $30K gain. Journal: DR Cash $80K, DR Accum Dep $150K, CR Equipment $200K, CR Gain on Disposal $30K. The gain appears in non-operating income.
Question 16Below book value sale

A company sells equipment for less than book value. The transaction results in a $20,000 loss. On the cash flow statement (indirect method), this loss is:

  • Subtracted from net income in the operating section
  • Shown as a $20,000 outflow in investing
  • Added back to net income in the operating section
  • Shown only in non-operating items
Why: The loss reduced net income but didn't use cash directly (the cash impact was the proceeds, not the loss itself). Add it back in operating to undo the non-cash NI hit. The actual cash proceeds appear in investing as an inflow.
Question 17Insurance proceeds

A factory machine (NBV $40,000) is destroyed in a fire. Insurance pays $60,000. For financial reporting under US GAAP:

  • $0 — proceeds offset book value, no NI impact
  • $20,000 gain in current period
  • $60,000 gain
  • $40,000 loss
Why: Same gain/loss formula applies for involuntary disposals: proceeds ($60K) − NBV ($40K) = $20K gain. Recognize in NI in the period of the event. Tax law sometimes allows deferring this gain via involuntary-conversion rules if a replacement is purchased — but that doesn't affect GAAP financial reporting.
Question 18Gain vs revenue

A retail clothing chain sells off old store fixtures for $50,000 above their book value. The $50,000 is properly classified as:

  • Sales revenue
  • Other comprehensive income
  • An equity transaction
  • A gain
Why: Revenue comes from central business activities. For a clothing retailer, that's selling clothes — not store fixtures. Selling fixtures is peripheral, so the income is a "gain," typically reported in "Other income" rather than the top-line revenue.
Question 19CF interest classification

Under IFRS, interest paid on debt may be classified on the cash flow statement as:

  • Operating OR financing (company choice)
  • Operating only — same as US GAAP
  • Financing only
  • Investing
Why: IFRS gives companies a choice for interest paid: operating OR financing. US GAAP requires operating. This is a common GAAP/IFRS difference and affects comparability of cash flows from operations between firms reporting under different frameworks.
Question 20Contract liability

A company collects $100,000 cash from a customer in advance for services to be provided over the next 6 months. At the time of cash collection, the company should record:

  • $100,000 of revenue immediately
  • A contract asset of $100,000
  • A contract liability (deferred revenue) of $100,000
  • $50,000 of revenue and a $50,000 liability
Why: Cash collected before performance creates a contract liability (often called deferred revenue or unearned revenue). DR Cash $100K, CR Contract Liability $100K. As services are performed, recognize revenue and reduce the liability proportionally.
Question 21Long-term contracts

A 3-year construction contract is priced at $10,000,000. Total estimated cost is $8,000,000. Costs incurred to date $2,400,000. Revenue recognized in prior periods $0. Using the cost-to-cost input method, revenue recognized this year is:

  • $2,400,000
  • $3,000,000
  • $8,000,000
  • $10,000,000
Why: % complete = $2,400K ÷ $8,000K = 30%. Revenue earned to date = 30% × $10,000K = $3,000K. Subtract revenue recognized prior ($0) → revenue this year = $3,000,000. Don't confuse the cost ($2.4M) with revenue. Gross profit recognised this year = $3,000K − $2,400K = $600K.
Question 22Loss contracts

A 5-year contract priced at $4,000,000 was originally estimated to cost $3,200,000. After 2 years (40% complete by cost), the company revises total estimated cost to $4,500,000. Revenue and profit recognised in years 1+2 totalled $1,600,000 and $320,000 respectively. What loss must be recognised in year 2?

  • $200,000 — only the new period's portion
  • $320,000 — reverse only the previously recognised gross profit
  • $820,000 — reverse prior $320K profit + recognise full remaining $500K expected loss
  • $500,000 — only the future expected loss
Why: Loss contract rule: recognise the entire expected loss in the period it becomes evident. Total expected loss = $4,500K − $4,000K = $500K. Plus reverse the previously recognised $320K profit. Year-2 loss = $320K + $500K = $820,000. Conservatism — losses recognised immediately, gains as performance progresses.
Question 23ASC 606

A consultant has a contract with a base fee of $200,000 plus a $50,000 bonus if the project finishes early. Past data: similar bonus targets are met 70% of the time. The expected-value method gives the most predictive estimate. What transaction price should be used at inception?

  • $235,000 — base + (70% × $50,000)
  • $250,000 — base + full bonus
  • $200,000 — exclude the variable portion entirely
  • $215,000 — base + (30% × $50,000)
Why: Expected value: $200K + (0.70 × $50K) + (0.30 × $0) = $200K + $35K = $235,000. The constraint asks whether a significant reversal is unlikely — in this scenario the 70% probability supports including the expected amount. The most-likely-amount method (alternative) would say $250K (since 70% > 50%) — different method, different answer.
Question 24Discontinued ops

A division held for sale has a carrying value of $8,000,000 and a fair value less costs to sell of $7,200,000. Pretax operating loss for the year was $300,000. Tax rate 25%. What appears on the income statement as "loss from discontinued operations, net of tax"?

  • $1,100,000
  • $300,000
  • $825,000
  • $1,400,000
Why: Held for sale: write down to lower of CV vs. FV − costs to sell → impairment loss = $8,000K − $7,200K = $800K. Total pretax loss = $800K (impairment) + $300K (operating) = $1,100K. Net of tax = $1,100K × (1 − 0.25) = $825,000. Discontinued ops are presented net of tax, separately from continuing operations.
Question 25Right of return

A retailer sells 2,000 units at $80 each (cost $48 each) on December 28. The 30-day return window straddles year-end. Historical experience: 8% of sales are returned. What is the December 31 balance in the refund liability account?

  • $0
  • $160,000
  • $7,680
  • $12,800
Why: Expected returns = 2,000 × 8% = 160 units. Refund liability (at selling price) = 160 × $80 = $12,800. Revenue recognised today = (2,000 − 160) × $80 = $147,200 (not the full $160,000). The corresponding return asset = 160 × $48 = $7,680 (a separate asset, not the liability).

Practice Test 6 · A.2 Comprehensive

Test 6 — A.2 Comprehensive · Recognition, Measurement, Valuation

Fifty CMA-style questions covering the full breadth of A.2 — receivables, inventory, investments, business combinations, fixed assets, intangibles, leases, taxes, equity, and revenue. Mixed in random-ish order, like the real exam.

Coverage

This is the section-level summary test for A.2 (Recognition, Measurement, Valuation) — the largest and most calculation-heavy slice of Section A. It mirrors how the actual CMA exam mixes topics within a section rather than drilling one area at a time. Use Tests 3, 4, and 5 for topic-focused practice; use this one to confirm cross-topic mastery before sitting the real thing.

Questions50 DifficultyApplication + Calculation (B–C) Time target~90 min Pass mark75% (38/50)
0 / 50 answered

Question 1A.2 · AR aging

An aging schedule estimates required ending allowance of $35,000. Allowance currently has a $4,000 debit balance (write-offs exceeded prior estimates). What is bad debt expense for the period?

  • $31,000
  • $35,000
  • $39,000
  • $4,000
Why: Aging targets the ending balance ($35K credit). With a $4K debit balance, you need $35K + $4K = $39,000 to bring the allowance to the required $35K credit. The trap: if the existing balance had been a credit, you'd add $31K. Read the sign carefully.
Question 2A.2 · FIFO

Beg inventory 200 units @ $10. Mar purchase 300 @ $12. Aug purchase 400 @ $15. Sold 600 units. FIFO periodic COGS is:

  • $6,800
  • $7,500
  • $7,200
  • $9,000
Why: FIFO sells oldest first: 200×$10 = $2,000; 300×$12 = $3,600; remaining 100 from $15 layer = $1,500. Total = $6,800. The 300 units left at $15 = $4,500 ending inventory.
Question 3A.2 · LIFO

Same data as Q2: 200 @ $10, 300 @ $12, 400 @ $15. Sold 600 units. Under LIFO periodic, COGS is:

  • $6,800
  • $7,200
  • $7,500
  • $8,400
Why: LIFO sells newest first: 400×$15 = $6,000; 200×$12 = $2,400. Total = $8,400. Compare to FIFO ($6,800) — LIFO matches recent costs to COGS, leaving older costs in inventory.
Question 4A.2 · LCNRV

Inventory item: cost $50/unit, selling price $60, costs to complete & sell $15. Under US GAAP LCNRV (FIFO), the inventory is reported at:

  • $60
  • $50
  • $45 — write-down required
  • $15
Why: NRV = Selling price − Costs to complete/sell = $60 − $15 = $45. Lower of Cost ($50) and NRV ($45) = $45. Write-down of $5/unit; record DR Loss / CR Inventory.
Question 5A.2 · Inv error

Year 1 ending inventory was OVERSTATED by $20,000 (uncorrected). The effect on Year 2 net income (assuming no further error in Y2) is:

  • Overstated by $20,000
  • Understated by $20,000
  • Unaffected
  • Overstated by $40,000
Why: Year 1's overstated ending inventory becomes Year 2's overstated beginning inventory. Higher beg inventory → higher COGS in Y2 → Y2 NI understated by $20K. Errors self-correct over two periods, but each year's NI is misstated.
Question 6A.2 · AFS

An AFS debt security purchased for $100,000 has a fair value of $108,000 at year-end. Under US GAAP, the $8,000 unrealised gain (pre-tax) is reported in:

  • OCI (and AOCI on the balance sheet)
  • Net income
  • Retained earnings directly
  • Not recognised until sold
Why: AFS debt securities → fair value through OCI. Realised gains/losses on sale recycle from OCI to NI. (Trading goes through NI; HTM stays at amortised cost.)
Question 7A.2 · Equity method

On Jan 1, Investor buys 25% of Investee for $500,000 (book = fair value). Investee earns $200,000 NI and pays $80,000 dividends. Year-end carrying value:

  • $500,000
  • $520,000
  • $530,000
  • $550,000
Why: Roll-forward: $500K + (25% × $200K) − (25% × $80K) = $500K + $50K − $20K = $530,000. Dividends reduce the investment; %·NI raises it.
Question 8A.2 · Goodwill

Acquirer pays $4M for 100% of Target. Target's identifiable assets at FV = $6M; liabilities at FV = $3M. Goodwill recognised:

  • $4,000,000
  • $1,000,000
  • $3,000,000
  • $0 — bargain purchase gain $1M
Why: Net identifiable = $6M − $3M = $3M. Goodwill = Price − Net identifiable = $4M − $3M = $1M. If price had been below $3M, the difference would be a bargain purchase gain (recognised in NI).
Question 9A.2 · Cap interest

A self-constructed asset took $2M of average accumulated expenditures during construction. Specific construction debt $1.5M at 8%; general debt $3M at 6%. Maximum capitalised interest:

  • $120,000
  • $140,000
  • $180,000
  • $150,000
Why: Specific debt covers $1.5M × 8% = $120K. Excess expenditure $500K × 6% (general rate) = $30K. Avoidable interest = $150K. Cap to lower of avoidable and total actual interest. Total actual = $1.5M×8% + $3M×6% = $120K + $180K = $300K. Cap the lower = $150,000.
Question 10A.2 · Depreciation

Asset cost $80,000, salvage $10,000, life 4 years. Year-2 depreciation under double-declining balance:

  • $40,000
  • $17,500
  • $20,000
  • $10,000
Why: DDB rate = 2/4 = 50%. Y1 depr = $80K × 50% = $40K. Y1 ending CV = $40K. Y2 depr = $40K × 50% = $20,000. Salvage ignored in rate but bounds the floor — don't take CV below $10K. Y3 partial depr applies.
Question 11A.2 · Impairment

Equipment carrying value $500K. Undiscounted future cash flows $480K. Fair value $440K. Under US GAAP, the impairment loss recognised is:

  • $60,000 — write down to fair value
  • $20,000
  • $0 — recoverable
  • $500,000
Why: US GAAP two-step. Step 1: undiscounted CFs ($480K) < CV ($500K) → impaired. Step 2: write down to FV ($440K). Loss = $500K − $440K = $60,000. The trap of $20K uses CFs vs. CV (Step 1 trigger only).
Question 12A.2 · R&D

A biotech spent $10M on internal R&D for a new drug, plus $200K of legal fees to register the resulting patent. Under US GAAP, the patent is recorded at:

  • $10,200,000
  • $10,000,000
  • $200,000
  • $0 — fully expensed
Why: US GAAP expenses internal R&D ($10M) as incurred. Only the directly capitalisable legal/registration costs ($200K) form the patent's recorded basis. IFRS would capitalise development costs that meet six criteria — a major GAAP/IFRS divergence.
Question 13A.2 · ST debt

A note matures Mar 31. On Feb 1, before the FS are issued, the company refinances it with a 5-year loan. Under US GAAP, the year-end (Dec 31) classification is:

  • Current — note matures within one year
  • Equity
  • Non-current — refinancing demonstrates intent + ability before issuance
  • Off balance sheet
Why: US GAAP allows reclassification to non-current if the refinancing happens (or non-cancellable agreement is in place) before FS issuance. IFRS is stricter: refinancing must be COMPLETED by the balance sheet date.
Question 14A.2 · Warranty

Year 1 sales $4M with 2.5% estimated warranty rate. Actual Y1 repairs cost $60K. Year-end warranty liability balance:

  • $100,000
  • $40,000
  • $60,000
  • $160,000
Why: Y1 warranty expense at sale = $4M × 2.5% = $100K (DR Expense / CR Liability). Repairs draw down the liability: $100K − $60K = $40K. Future repairs in Y2 will reduce the balance further.
Question 15A.2 · Deferred tax

A $300,000 temporary difference will reverse next year. Current rate 21%; enacted rate for next year is 25%. Required deferred tax balance:

  • $75,000
  • $63,000
  • $12,000
  • $0
Why: Use the enacted rate that will apply when the difference reverses = 25%. $300K × 25% = $75,000. The $63K trap uses the current 21% rate.
Question 16A.2 · Leases

A 4-year lease has annual end-of-year payments of $80,000. Lessee's incremental borrowing rate 5%. PV factor of an ordinary annuity of 4 periods at 5% = 3.5460. Initial lease liability:

  • $320,000
  • $300,000
  • $283,680
  • $80,000
Why: Lease liability = $80,000 × 3.5460 = $283,680. The $320K trap forgets to discount; $80K is just one payment.
Question 17A.2 · Stock dividends

200,000 shares of $1 par stock outstanding. Market $40. Board declares a 10% stock dividend. Journal entry:

  • DR RE $20K / CR Common Stock $20K
  • DR RE $200K / CR Common Stock $20K / CR APIC $180K
  • DR Cash $800K / CR Common Stock $20K / CR APIC $780K
  • DR RE $800K / CR Common Stock $20K / CR APIC $780K
Why: 10% < 25% = small stock dividend → capitalise at FMV. Shares issued = 20,000. FMV = 20,000 × $40 = $800K. DR RE $800K / CR Common Stock $20K (par) / CR APIC $780K (excess). Large stock dividend (≥25%) capitalises at par only.
Question 18A.2 · Common stock

A company issues 5,000 shares of $1 par stock for legal services. Stock has readily determinable FV of $30/share; the legal services are separately quoted at $140K. The stock is recorded at:

  • $5,000
  • $150,000 — use the more reliably measurable FV (publicly-traded stock)
  • $140,000
  • The lower of the two
Why: Use the more reliably measurable FV. Public stock prices are usually more reliable than service estimates. 5,000 × $30 = $150K. DR Legal Services Expense $150K / CR Common Stock $5K / CR APIC $145K.
Question 19A.2 · Treasury

Company buys back 1,000 shares ($1 par) at $40. Later resells all 1,000 at $30. APIC—Treasury balance was $0 before. The reissue entry includes:

  • DR Cash $30K / DR RE $10K / CR Treasury $40K
  • DR Cash $30K / CR Treasury $30K / CR Loss $10K
  • DR Cash $30K / DR Loss $10K / CR Treasury $40K
  • DR Cash $30K / CR Treasury $30K
Why: Treasury never affects NI — no gain/loss on own stock. With $0 APIC—Treasury, the $10K reissue deficit goes straight to Retained Earnings: DR Cash $30K / DR RE $10K / CR Treasury at cost $40K.
Question 20A.2 · Revenue rec

In the ASC 606 5-step model, allocating the transaction price to performance obligations is BASED on:

  • Total contract value divided by number of POs
  • Customer-stated values
  • Standalone selling prices of each PO (estimated if not observable)
  • Historical pricing
Why: Step 4 allocates based on standalone selling prices. If a price isn't observable, it's estimated (adjusted-market, expected-cost-plus, or residual approach). Step 5 then recognises revenue when (point in time) or as (over time) each PO is satisfied.
Question 21A.2 · Right of return

Retailer sells 1,500 units at $80 each (cost $50). Expected return rate 10%. Revenue recognised today:

  • $120,000
  • $12,000
  • $108,000 — wait, that's $90 × 1,200
  • $108,000 — 1,350 expected kept × $80
Why: Revenue = (1 − return rate) × Total = (1 − 0.10) × 1,500 × $80 = 0.90 × $120K = $108K. Refund liability = 10% × $120K = $12K. Return asset = 150 × $50 = $7.5K.
Question 22A.2 · LTC

$8M contract, total estimated cost $6M. Costs to date $1.5M. Revenue recognised in prior periods $0. Revenue this year (cost-to-cost):

  • $1,500,000
  • $2,000,000
  • $8,000,000
  • $6,000,000
Why: % complete = $1.5M / $6M = 25%. Revenue earned to date = 25% × $8M = $2M. Less prior recognised ($0) = $2M. Profit this year = $2M − $1.5M = $500K.
Question 23A.2 · Loss contract

Same contract as Q22, but year 2 cost estimate revises to $9M (versus $8M price). Year-2 cumulative recognition: revenue, costs, gross profit. The proper Y2 entry recognises:

  • The proportional Y2 revenue minus Y2 costs as a small profit
  • No loss yet — wait until contract ends
  • The entire $1M expected loss in Y2 immediately
  • Half the loss in Y2 and half in remaining periods
Why: Loss-contract rule: recognise the entire expected loss ($9M − $8M = $1M) in the period it becomes evident. Conservatism — recognise losses immediately, gains as performance progresses.
Question 24A.2 · GAAP/IFRS

Under IFRS, inventory write-downs are:

  • Reversible up to original cost when value recovers
  • Permanent — same as US GAAP
  • Not allowed
  • Reversible without limit
Why: IFRS allows reversals capped at original cost. US GAAP does NOT allow reversals — write-downs create a new cost basis. One of the cleanest GAAP/IFRS deltas.
Question 25A.2 · Disposal

Equipment cost $200K, accumulated depreciation $140K. Sold for $80K cash. Gain or loss recognised:

  • $80,000 gain
  • $60,000 loss
  • $20,000 gain
  • $0
Why: Carrying value = $200K − $140K = $60K. Gain = Cash − CV = $80K − $60K = $20K gain. The $80K trap ignores CV; $60K loss reverses cash and CV.
Question 26A.2 · Write-off

A specific $5,000 receivable is written off. Under the allowance method, the impact on net AR (gross AR − allowance) is:

  • $0 — both sides drop by $5K
  • Decrease by $5,000
  • Increase by $5,000
  • Decrease by $10,000
Why: DR Allowance / CR AR. Both gross AR and the allowance fall by $5K, leaving net AR unchanged. The expense was already recognised when the allowance was set up — write-off is just a balance-sheet movement.
Question 27A.2 · FOB

$50,000 of goods are in transit at Dec 31, shipped FOB shipping point. The goods belong on the:

  • Seller's books only
  • Carrier's books
  • Buyer's books — title passed at shipment
  • Neither's books until delivery
Why: FOB shipping point: title passes when shipped (buyer pays freight, owns goods in transit). FOB destination: title passes on arrival (seller pays freight). The Dec 31 inventory count must reflect the title rule.
Question 28A.2 · Equity invest

A 15% investment in a publicly-traded company with no significant influence is measured at:

  • Equity method
  • Fair value through net income
  • Cost (no remeasurement)
  • FV through OCI
Why: Post-2018 (ASU 2016-01), equity securities without significant influence go to FV through NI — even long-term holdings. The old AFS-for-equity treatment was eliminated. Only debt securities can use AFS now.
Question 29A.2 · NCI

Acquirer buys 80% of Target. Under US GAAP (ASC 805), the 20% non-controlling interest (NCI) is measured at:

  • Acquisition-date fair value — full goodwill method always applies
  • Proportionate share of the acquiree's identifiable net assets
  • Either fair value or proportionate share, at the acquirer's election
  • Original cost only
Why: ASC 805 requires NCI at acquisition-date fair value — there is NO election under US GAAP, so full goodwill is the only outcome. IFRS 3 permits a choice, transaction-by-transaction, between fair value (full goodwill) and proportionate share of net identifiable assets (partial goodwill). The proportionate-share alternative is therefore an IFRS-only option.
Question 30A.2 · IFRS impair

Under IFRS, a previously-recognised impairment loss on a non-goodwill long-lived asset is:

  • Reversible if the recoverable amount recovers — capped at original carrying value (less depreciation)
  • Reversible without limit
  • Never reversible
  • Reversible only on disposal
Why: IFRS permits reversal up to original carrying value (less normal depreciation). US GAAP does NOT permit reversal — once impaired, the new lower CV is the basis. Goodwill impairment is non-reversible under both frameworks.
Question 31A.2 · ETR

Pretax book income $2M. Permanent differences: $80K muni interest (non-taxable), $40K fines (non-deductible). Statutory rate 21%. Effective tax rate:

  • 21.0%
  • 20.6%
  • 21.4%
  • 22.0%
Why: Taxable = $2M − $80K + $40K = $1.96M. Tax = $1.96M × 21% = $411,600. ETR = $411,600 / $2M = 20.58% (≈ 20.6%). Permanents move ETR from statutory; temporaries don't (they create DTA/DTL but ETR matches statutory eventually).
Question 32A.2 · Lease class

A 5-year lease on equipment with 6-year economic life. PV of payments = 92% of FV. No transfer of ownership, no bargain purchase, equipment is general-purpose. Under US GAAP (ASC 842), the lessee classifies as:

  • Operating
  • Short-term
  • Finance — meets the 75% life test (5/6 = 83%) AND the 90% PV test
  • Either operating or finance, lessee's choice
Why: Five tests for finance: ownership transfer, bargain purchase, ≥75% life, ≥90% PV, specialised asset. ANY ONE trips finance. 5/6 = 83% > 75% → finance. 92% > 90% → also finance. Multiple tests independently confirm finance.
Question 33A.2 · RE rollforward

Beg RE $400K. NI $180K. Cash dividends declared $50K. Stock dividend (small, FMV $30K, par $5K). End RE:

  • $500,000
  • $525,000
  • $555,000
  • $485,000
Why: End RE = Beg + NI − Cash div − Stock div (at FMV for small) = $400K + $180K − $50K − $30K = $500K. The $5K par is NOT the charge to RE — small stock dividends capitalise at FMV.
Question 34A.2 · Pref dividend

100,000 shares of $100 par cumulative 6% preferred. Two years of dividends are in arrears. To pay any common dividend this year, total preferred dividends required first:

  • $600,000 — current year only
  • $1,200,000 — only arrears
  • $600,000 — only one year of arrears + current
  • $1,800,000 — 2 years arrears + current year
Why: Cumulative preferred: pay all arrears + current year before any common. Annual = 100,000 × $100 × 6% = $600K. 2 years arrears + current = 3 × $600K = $1.8M. Non-cumulative would only require the current year's $600K.
Question 35A.2 · Treasury

Authorised 1,000,000 shares; issued 600,000; treasury 80,000. Outstanding shares for EPS denominator:

  • 1,000,000
  • 520,000
  • 600,000
  • 680,000
Why: Outstanding = Issued − Treasury = 600K − 80K = 520K. Treasury shares don't vote and aren't in EPS denominator.
Question 36A.2 · POs

A bundled software license + 12-month support contract for $120K. Standalone: software $100K, support $40K (combined SSP $140K). Revenue recognised at contract inception (point-in-time license, time-based support):

  • ~$85,714 (software allocated portion at point in time)
  • $120,000 (entire contract at inception)
  • $60,000 (half)
  • $0 (deferred)
Why: Allocate $120K by SSP ratio. Software = $120K × ($100/$140) = $85.7K (point-in-time, recognised at delivery). Support = $120K × ($40/$140) = $34.3K (over 12 months). Day-1 revenue ≈ $85.7K; the rest unfolds over time.
Question 37A.2 · Consignment

A consignor ships $50,000 of goods to a consignee. The consignee has not yet sold any. Year-end, on the consignor's books:

  • Recognise $50K revenue (delivered)
  • Recognise $50K AR
  • Goods stay in inventory (often as "Inventory on consignment")
  • Goods are derecognised completely
Why: Title doesn't pass until the consignee sells to an end customer. Consignor keeps the inventory on their books (typically reclassified to a separate "Inventory on consignment" line). No revenue recognised yet.
Question 38A.2 · Factoring

Factoring AR without recourse is most appropriately treated as:

  • A loan (with the receivable as collateral)
  • An off-balance-sheet item
  • An equity transaction
  • A sale — the receivable is derecognised; gain/loss on the difference between proceeds and CV
Why: Without-recourse factoring transfers the credit risk to the factor → derecognition. With-recourse factoring (seller bears credit loss risk) is typically treated as a secured borrowing — AR stays on the books.
Question 39A.2 · Inv equation

Beg inventory $80K. Purchases $300K. End inventory $90K. COGS:

  • $290,000
  • $310,000
  • $380,000
  • $220,000
Why: Beg + Purchases − End = COGS → $80K + $300K − $90K = $290K. The $310K trap reverses ending and beginning inventory.
Question 40A.2 · Bond price

A 5-year bond with $1,000 face value, 6% coupon, market rate 8%. The bond will sell at:

  • Premium (above face)
  • Par ($1,000)
  • Discount (below face)
  • Cannot determine
Why: Coupon (6%) < Market (8%) → bond's cash flows are less attractive than alternatives → priced at a discount. Coupon > market = premium. Coupon = market = par.
Question 41A.2 · Equity method

An investor uses the equity method. The investee reports cumulative losses that exceed the investment's carrying value. Without other commitments, the investor:

  • Continues recognising losses, taking the investment negative
  • Stops recognising losses once CV reaches zero; resumes when future profits cover the gap
  • Switches to fair-value method
  • Recognises a one-time impairment
Why: Investment carrying value can't go below zero (without other commitments to fund losses). Stop applying losses; track them off-book. Resume recognising losses only after the investee's profits restore equity, then continue from where you stopped.
Question 42A.2 · Bargain purchase

Acquirer pays $3M for net identifiable assets at FV of $4M. After re-checking the FV measurement, the $1M difference is:

  • Negative goodwill capitalised on B/S
  • Deferred and amortised over 10 years
  • Pushed against goodwill of other acquisitions
  • A bargain purchase gain — recognised in NI immediately (after re-verifying the FV measurement)
Why: When price < FV of net identifiable, no negative goodwill is recorded. After confirming FV measurement is correct, the difference is a bargain purchase gain — recognised through NI in the period of acquisition.
Question 43A.2 · Basket purchase

$500K paid for land + building + equipment in a single transaction. Appraised FVs: land $200K, building $300K, equipment $100K. Building's recorded cost:

  • $300,000
  • $166,667
  • $250,000
  • $500,000
Why: Allocate by relative FV. Total FV = $600K; building's share = $300K / $600K = 50%. Building cost = 50% × $500K = $250K. Land = $166.7K; Equipment = $83.3K. Sum: $500K ✓.
Question 44A.2 · Crypto

Under ASU 2023-08, qualifying crypto assets (e.g., Bitcoin) are measured at:

  • Fair value through net income — both gains and losses
  • Cost less impairment only
  • Lower of cost and fair value
  • Amortised cost
Why: Pre-2024 GAAP treated crypto as indefinite-lived intangibles (impairment-only, no upward remeasurement) — asymmetric. ASU 2023-08 reversed this for in-scope crypto: FV through NI, both up and down. Effective for fiscal years beginning after Dec 15, 2024.
Question 45A.2 · Callable debt

A long-term bond has a covenant that the lender can call (demand immediate repayment). The covenant is violated at year-end. Under US GAAP, classification is:

  • Non-current — original maturity > 1 year
  • Equity
  • Current — unless lender waives or grants 12-month grace
  • Off balance sheet
Why: Covenant violation → lender's call right is active → debt is callable on demand → current. The exception: lender grants a 12-month waiver or grace period before FS issuance. IFRS is similar but with different timing requirements.
Question 46A.2 · Service warranty

A 3-year service warranty is sold separately for $300 per item, alongside a $1,000 product sale (with 1-year assurance warranty). Treatment of the $300:

  • Recognise as revenue at sale
  • Recognise immediately as warranty liability
  • Net against the product price
  • Defer revenue and recognise over the 3-year service period (it's a separate performance obligation)
Why: Service-type warranty (separately priced extra) is a distinct performance obligation under ASC 606. Defer revenue and recognise over the service period. Assurance-type (works as promised, included in product) is treated as a loss contingency at sale, not a separate PO.
Question 47A.2 · NOL

Under post-TCJA US tax law, net operating losses (NOLs) generated in 2024 can be:

  • Carried back 2 years
  • Carried forward indefinitely without limit
  • Carried forward indefinitely, but offset only 80% of future taxable income
  • Used in the year generated only
Why: Post-TCJA: most NOLs have indefinite carryforward but are capped at 80% of future taxable income. No general carryback (some narrow exceptions exist). Pre-TCJA was 2-year carryback / 20-year carryforward without the 80% cap.
Question 48A.2 · IFRS lease

Under IFRS 16, the lessee model for non-short-term leases is:

  • Dual model: operating vs. finance, like US GAAP
  • Single model: all leases on B/S, finance-style (interest + depreciation)
  • Off balance sheet for most operating leases
  • Optional based on lease length
Why: IFRS 16 has ONE lessee model — all leases (except short-term < 12 months and low-value < ~$5K) on balance sheet, with interest expense + depreciation, front-loaded P&L pattern. US GAAP retains the operating/finance distinction. Affects EBITDA comparability.
Question 49A.2 · Stock split

A 2-for-1 stock split affects which of the following equity components?

  • Par value halves; share count doubles; total equity unchanged
  • Par stays; share count doubles; total equity doubles
  • RE decreases by FMV of new shares
  • APIC increases by FMV of new shares
Why: Stock split: par halves (from say $1 to $0.50), share count doubles, NO net change to total equity components. It's a memo entry only. Stock dividends, by contrast, capitalise RE into Common Stock and APIC.
Question 50A.2 · Variable consideration

A consultant has a $200K base fee + $50K bonus if the project finishes early (70% probability). Using the most-likely-amount method, the transaction price is:

  • $200,000
  • $215,000
  • $235,000 — expected value
  • $250,000 — most likely outcome includes the bonus
Why: Most-likely-amount picks the single most probable outcome. 70% > 50% → most likely is bonus paid → $250K. Expected-value method (the alternative) would give $200K + 0.70 × $50K = $235K. Both are acceptable per ASC 606; pick whichever better predicts the outcome.

Question 1Foundations

A company pays off $50,000 of accounts payable with cash. The effect on the accounting equation is:

  • Assets ↓ $50,000; Equity ↓ $50,000
  • No change to total assets, liabilities, or equity
  • Assets ↓ $50,000; Liabilities ↓ $50,000
  • Assets ↑ $50,000; Liabilities ↓ $50,000
Why: Cash (asset) decreases by $50K; AP (liability) decreases by $50K. Total assets ↓, total liabilities ↓ — equation stays balanced. Equity is unaffected since this is a balance-sheet-only transaction (no income or owner activity).
Question 2Foundations

A company has performed $15,000 of services in December but won't bill the customer until January. The December 31 adjusting entry is:

  • DR Accounts Receivable / CR Service Revenue $15,000
  • DR Cash / CR Service Revenue $15,000
  • DR Service Revenue / CR Accounts Receivable $15,000
  • No entry until billed
Why: Accrued revenue: services were performed (so revenue earned) but no cash or invoice has been generated yet. Recognize the receivable as an asset (DR) and the revenue (CR). When invoiced and collected later, AR reduces and Cash increases.
Question 3Foundations

Which of the following is an enhancing qualitative characteristic per the FASB Conceptual Framework?

  • Faithful representation
  • Comparability
  • Relevance
  • Materiality
Why: The fundamentals are relevance and faithful representation. Enhancing characteristics improve usefulness when fundamentals are met: Comparability, Verifiability, Timeliness, Understandability (mnemonic: "CVTU"). Materiality is a constraint, not an enhancing characteristic.
Question 4A.1 · Financial Statements

A 2-year prepaid insurance policy was paid in full on January 1. On the balance sheet 18 months later, the remaining $5,000 of unexpired insurance should be classified as:

  • Long-term asset
  • Liability
  • Current asset
  • Expense
Why: 18 months in, only 6 months remain — within one year, so current. Prepaid expense classification depends on the unexpired portion's timing. As that portion comes within 12 months, it converts from non-current to current.
Question 5A.1 · Financial Statements

In which order does a multi-step income statement present these items?

  • Net sales → COGS → Operating expenses → Gross profit → Operating income
  • Operating income → COGS → Net sales → Tax
  • Net sales → Operating expenses → Gross profit → COGS
  • Net sales → COGS → Gross profit → Operating expenses → Operating income
Why: Standard sequence: revenue first, then COGS to get gross profit, then operating expenses to get operating income, then non-operating items (interest, gains/losses), then tax to get net income. This logical "stepping down" is why it's called "multi-step."
Question 6A.1 · Financial Statements

Net income $456,900; Depreciation $45,600; Increase in AR $20,000; Decrease in Inventory $15,000; Increase in AP $10,000. What is cash from operations under the indirect method?

  • $507,500
  • $487,500
  • $521,500
  • $456,900
Why: $456,900 NI + $45,600 depreciation (non-cash add-back) − $20,000 (AR increase = use of cash) + $15,000 (inventory decrease = source) + $10,000 (AP increase = source) = $507,500. This question is patterned on a released IMA sample — practice this exact format.
Question 7A.1 · Financial Statements

Three years ago, a company purchased stock at $100,000. The stock was sold during the current year for $150,000. On the SCF, this transaction appears as:

  • $100,000 inflow in operating; $50,000 inflow in investing
  • $150,000 inflow in financing
  • $50,000 inflow in operating only
  • $150,000 inflow in investing; $50,000 gain subtracted from operating (indirect method)
Why: Cash actually received from the sale ($150K) is the investing inflow. The $50K gain hit net income, but didn't separately use or generate cash — subtract from NI in operating to avoid double-counting. This question pattern appears verbatim on IMA released materials.
Question 8A.1 · Financial Statements

A company holds AFS debt securities. Fair value declined $40,000 during the year (no credit impairment). For tax-affected reporting (tax rate 25%), what is recognized in OCI?

  • $40,000 loss
  • $30,000 loss (net of tax)
  • $10,000 loss
  • $0 — recognized only on sale
Why: AFS unrealized losses go to OCI (not NI), and OCI is presented net of tax: $40,000 × (1 − 25%) = $30,000 loss in OCI. The $10,000 tax benefit is a deferred tax asset on the balance sheet. When the security is sold, the cumulative AOCI gets recycled to NI.
Question 9A.1 · Financial Statements

Which transaction does NOT appear on the statement of changes in stockholders' equity?

  • Net income for the year
  • Issuance of common stock
  • Repayment of long-term debt
  • Treasury stock purchase
Why: Repaying debt is a financing transaction that affects only cash and the liability — no equity impact. The other three all change equity components: NI flows into RE; stock issuance increases CS and APIC; treasury purchase reduces total equity (contra account).
Question 10A.1 · Financial Statements

Under US GAAP, dividends RECEIVED are classified on the SCF as:

  • Operating activity
  • Investing activity
  • Financing activity
  • Either operating or investing (company choice)
Why: GAAP: dividends received and interest received → operating; dividends paid → financing; interest paid → operating. IFRS gives more flexibility: dividends/interest received can be operating or investing; dividends/interest paid can be operating or financing. Memorize the GAAP rules cold — they're frequently tested.
Question 11A.1 · Financial Statements

In the IIRC's Integrated Reporting Framework, which of the following is NOT one of the six capitals?

  • Natural capital
  • Legal capital
  • Human capital
  • Manufactured capital
Why: The six capitals are: Financial, Manufactured, Intellectual, Human, Social/Relationship, Natural. "Legal capital" isn't one — that term refers to par-value-based stock requirements in corporate law. Integrated reporting tracks how these inputs are transformed into outputs and outcomes.
Question 12A.2 · Assets

An aging schedule indicates that the required ending Allowance for Doubtful Accounts is $80,000. The unadjusted balance is a $5,000 debit (write-offs exceeded prior year's accrual). What is bad debt expense?

  • $80,000
  • $75,000
  • $85,000
  • $5,000
Why: Going from a $5,000 debit balance to a target $80,000 credit balance requires an $85,000 expense. The trick: don't just compute target − current, since "current" can be a debit (deficit) or credit (excess). Always ask: "what credit must I add to get from where I am to where I need to be?"
Question 13A.2 · Assets

A company normally stores inventory in its warehouse. Due to a fire, this month's inventory was stored offsite ($3,000), import duty ($2,000), and unreimbursed freight on goods sold this month ($4,000). What amount should be charged to inventory (the rest is expense)?

  • $2,000 (import duty only)
  • $5,000 (offsite storage + import duty)
  • $9,000 (all three items)
  • $0 — all are period costs
Why: Only ordinary, necessary costs to bring goods to a sellable condition are inventoriable. Import duty (yes — necessary). Abnormal storage from a fire (no — period cost). Freight-out on goods sold (no — selling expense). This pattern appears on UWorld practice CMA materials.
Question 14A.2 · Assets

A company uses LIFO. Its LIFO inventory is $2M; LIFO reserve is $400,000. What would the inventory value be under FIFO?

  • $1,600,000
  • $2,400,000
  • $2,000,000
  • $400,000
Why: FIFO inventory = LIFO inventory + LIFO reserve = $2M + $400K = $2.4M. The LIFO reserve is the cumulative difference, used to convert LIFO companies to a comparable basis. In rising prices, FIFO inventory > LIFO inventory.
Question 15A.2 · Assets

Year 1 ending inventory was understated by $30,000 (and not corrected). What is the effect on Year 2 net income?

  • Year 2 NI understated by $30,000
  • Year 2 NI unchanged
  • Year 2 NI overstated by $60,000
  • Year 2 NI overstated by $30,000
Why: Understated Year 1 ending inventory = understated Year 2 beginning inventory → understated COGS in Year 2 → overstated NI in Year 2. The error reverses (Year 1 NI was understated; Year 2 NI is overstated). Over both years, the errors cancel.
Question 16A.2 · Investments

A company classifies a debt security as "trading." Fair value increased $50,000 during the year. The treatment under US GAAP is:

  • Recognize $50,000 in OCI
  • Recognize $50,000 in OCI, recycle to NI on sale
  • Recognize $50,000 unrealized gain in net income
  • No recognition until sold
Why: Trading securities are marked to FV at every reporting date with both gains and losses through net income. The "intent" classification matters: trading = NI; AFS = OCI; HTM = amortized cost (no FV adjustment).
Question 17A.2 · Investments

Investor owns 25% of Investee. In the current year, Investee reports net income of $400,000 and pays total dividends of $100,000 (so Investor receives $25,000). What income does Investor recognize?

  • $100,000 (25% of NI)
  • $25,000 (dividends received)
  • $125,000 (NI share + dividends)
  • $300,000 (NI minus dividends)
Why: Equity method recognizes investor's share of Investee's NI = 25% × $400K = $100K. Dividends are a reduction of investment carrying value (return of capital), not income. Recognizing both would double-count.
Question 18A.2 · Investments

Under the VIE consolidation model, a company should consolidate another entity if it is the entity's:

  • Largest shareholder
  • First investor
  • Founder
  • Primary beneficiary — has power to direct activities AND obligation to absorb losses or right to receive benefits
Why: Under the VIE model, "control" isn't about voting equity — it's about which party is the "primary beneficiary," meeting both criteria: (1) power to direct the activities most significantly affecting economic performance, and (2) exposure to losses or right to benefits. This is one of the IMA 2024+ outline additions.
Question 19A.2 · Fixed assets

Which of the following should be capitalized as part of a building's cost?

  • Property taxes during the building's first year of operation
  • Routine repairs after construction completes
  • Architect fees and construction-period interest
  • Janitorial services during the first month
Why: Capitalize all costs to get the building ready for use: purchase/construction cost, architect fees, permits, interest during construction (avoidable interest method). Property taxes, repairs, and operating services after the asset is in service are period expenses.
Question 20A.2 · Depreciation

Equipment costs $60,000 (no salvage), 5-year life. Using sum-of-the-years'-digits, what is depreciation expense in Year 1?

  • $12,000
  • $20,000
  • $24,000
  • $15,000
Why: Sum of digits = 1+2+3+4+5 = 15. Year 1 fraction = 5/15 = 1/3. Year 1 depreciation = $60,000 × (5/15) = $20,000. SYD is an accelerated method (more depreciation early, less later). DDB is even more accelerated than SYD.
Question 21A.2 · Intangibles

A patent has a 20-year legal life, but a company estimates it can only commercialize the underlying technology for 8 years before competitors innovate around it. The patent should be amortized over:

  • 8 years
  • 20 years
  • 14 years (average)
  • It depends on management's preference
Why: Use the shorter of legal life and useful life. The 8-year economic useful life governs because that's how long the asset will generate benefits. Amortization shouldn't outlast the period over which the asset produces value.
Question 22A.2 · Liabilities

A current obligation is refinanced on a long-term basis BEFORE the balance sheet is issued (but AFTER the balance sheet date). Under IFRS, the obligation is classified as:

  • Long-term liability
  • Either, at company election
  • Current liability
  • Off-balance-sheet
Why: IFRS is stricter than GAAP: refinancing must be in place AT the balance sheet date for non-current classification. GAAP allows reclassification if intent and ability are demonstrated before issuance. Same set of facts can produce different classifications under the two frameworks.
Question 23A.2 · Contingencies

A lawsuit's outcome is "probable" with an estimated loss range of $300,000 to $700,000. Under US GAAP, what amount is accrued?

  • $700,000 (highest estimate)
  • $500,000 (midpoint)
  • No accrual; disclose only
  • $300,000 (best estimate / low end of range when no single estimate is best)
Why: US GAAP: when a loss is probable and a range is estimable, accrue the best estimate; if no single point is more likely, accrue the LOWEST end of the range (with disclosure of the range). IFRS would use the midpoint or expected value. Disclosure-only is for "reasonably possible" loss.
Question 24A.2 · Income taxes

In Year 1, a company books $40,000 of warranty expense (book) but $0 actual claims (tax). Tax rate 25%. What is the deferred tax effect?

  • $10,000 DTL
  • $10,000 DTA
  • $40,000 DTA
  • No deferred effect
Why: Warranty: book expense recognized early; tax deduction comes when claim is paid. Future tax benefits (when claim paid) are higher → DTA. Calculation: $40,000 × 25% = $10,000 DTA. Pattern: future deductible amounts → DTA; future taxable amounts → DTL.
Question 25A.2 · Income taxes

A company generates a $200,000 NOL in 2024 (post-TCJA). Tax rate 25%. Assuming full DTA recognition (no valuation allowance), what is recognized?

  • $200,000 reduction in current tax expense
  • $200,000 DTA
  • $50,000 DTA (NOL × tax rate); $50,000 reduction in tax expense
  • No effect — NOLs are recognized only when used
Why: NOL × tax rate = future tax benefit = DTA = $200,000 × 25% = $50,000. Recognized immediately as a DTA on the balance sheet. Reduces tax expense (income tax benefit) on the income statement. Carryforward indefinite (post-2017) but use limited to 80% of future taxable income.
Question 26A.2 · Leases

A 5-year operating lease has annual payments of $30,000 (paid at year-end). The discount rate is 6%. What is the initial ROU asset value (assume no initial direct costs or prepayments)?

  • $150,000
  • $130,000
  • $126,372 (PV of $30,000 × 4.2124 PVA factor at 6%, 5 yrs)
  • $30,000
Why: ROU asset = present value of lease payments (= initial lease liability when no prepayments/direct costs). PV of ordinary annuity factor at 6%, n=5: 4.2124. PV = $30,000 × 4.2124 ≈ $126,372. Both finance and operating leases use this same initial measurement under ASC 842.
Question 27A.2 · Equity

A company appropriates $500,000 of retained earnings for plant expansion. What is the effect on total stockholders' equity?

  • No change
  • Increases by $500,000
  • Decreases by $500,000
  • Depends on tax rate
Why: Appropriation reclassifies WITHIN retained earnings (e.g., from "Unrestricted RE" to "Appropriated RE for plant expansion"). It signals that the board has set aside a portion for a specific purpose, but no cash, asset, or total equity changes. It's purely a labeling/segregation entry.
Question 28A.2 · Equity

A company issues 2,000 shares of $1 par stock to acquire equipment. The stock has a quoted market price of $25/share. The equipment's appraised value is $48,000. The equipment is recorded at:

  • $2,000 (par value)
  • $48,000 (appraised)
  • $25,000 (par + APIC nominal)
  • $50,000 (FV of stock — more reliable)
Why: Use the more reliably measurable FV. Public market stock prices are typically more reliable than appraisals. Equipment recorded at 2,000 × $25 = $50,000. Common Stock at par = $2,000; APIC = $48,000.
Question 29A.2 · Preferred stock

"Fully participating" preferred stock means:

  • Preferred shareholders share in all corporate decisions
  • Preferred shareholders are guaranteed a fixed dividend
  • Preferred shareholders share pro rata with common in dividends beyond their basic rate
  • Preferred shareholders are converted to common after a specified period
Why: Participating preferred gets its base dividend AND shares with common in any extra dividends declared, often pro-rata or per a contractual formula. "Fully" participating shares without limit. "Partially" participating caps at a specified percentage.
Question 30A.2 · Equity

Under the par value method for treasury stock, when a company repurchases its own shares, the treasury stock account is debited at:

  • Repurchase price
  • Par value
  • Original issuance price
  • Fair market value
Why: Par value method treats the buyback as a "retirement" — DR Treasury at par, DR APIC for the original APIC associated with those shares, plug to RE for any deficit. Cost method (more common) just records Treasury at full repurchase cost. Know both methods — they generate different account balances.
Question 31A.2 · Equity

A company has $40,000 of retained earnings and declares a $60,000 cash dividend. The $20,000 excess is treated as:

  • A liquidating dividend (return of capital, reduces APIC)
  • An expense on the income statement
  • A loss on the income statement
  • Not allowed — dividends cannot exceed RE
Why: Dividends in excess of retained earnings are considered "liquidating" — a return of investment to shareholders, reducing paid-in capital (typically APIC). This must be disclosed. Some states' corporate laws restrict the practice (capital impairment rules), but the accounting treatment when allowed is APIC reduction.
Question 32A.2 · Revenue

Under ASC 606 Step 1 (Identify the contract), all of the following criteria must be met EXCEPT:

  • Parties have approved the contract and are committed to perform
  • Each party's rights and payment terms are identifiable
  • The contract must be in writing
  • The contract has commercial substance and collection is probable
Why: A contract can be oral, written, or implied by customary business practices. Five Step-1 criteria: (1) approved by parties, (2) rights identifiable, (3) payment terms identifiable, (4) commercial substance, (5) collection probable. Written form isn't required.
Question 33A.2 · Revenue

A customer pays $90,000 today for a product to be delivered in 3 years. Cash price (if delivered today) would be $80,000. The $10,000 difference represents:

  • An immaterial item to ignore
  • A discount on the customer
  • Variable consideration
  • A significant financing component — recognized as interest expense over time
Why: When timing of payment vs. transfer differs by more than 12 months, ASC 606 requires separating the financing component. The customer is essentially lending $80,000 today; the $10,000 is interest. Recognize $80K as revenue at delivery, $10K as interest expense over the 3-year period.
Question 34A.2 · Returns

A retailer expects 8% returns on this month's $1,200,000 in sales (cost $700,000). What journal entry is recorded for the refund liability and return asset?

  • DR Refund Asset $96,000 / CR Refund Liability $96,000
  • DR Sales Returns $96,000 / CR Refund Liability $96,000; DR Return Asset $56,000 / CR COGS $56,000
  • No entry until returns occur
  • DR Sales $96,000 / CR Cash $96,000
Why: Refund Liability = expected returns × sales price = 8% × $1.2M = $96,000 (DR Sales Returns / CR Refund Liability). Return Asset = expected returns × cost = 8% × $700K = $56,000 (DR Return Asset / CR COGS). The "asset/liability" approach replaced the old "net" presentation under ASC 606.
Question 35A.2 · Long-term contracts

A 3-year construction contract has price $20M. After Year 1, costs incurred = $5M, estimated remaining cost = $18M. What revenue and gross profit/(loss) is recognized in Year 1?

  • $5M revenue; $3M loss (full anticipated loss recognized)
  • $5M revenue; $0 profit/loss
  • $4.35M revenue (5/23 × 20)
  • Wait until completion to recognize
Why: Total estimated cost = $5M + $18M = $23M; price = $20M → $3M total expected loss. Recognize entire loss immediately. Year 1: revenue = % complete × price = (5/23) × $20M = $4.35M; cost = $5M (incurred) + write-down to bring CIP to expected outcome → $3M loss recognized in Year 1.
Question 36A.2 · GAAP/IFRS

Under IFRS, PP&E may be carried under the revaluation model. Under US GAAP:

  • Same option exists under GAAP
  • Revaluation is required for all PP&E
  • Revaluation is not allowed — historical cost (less depreciation and impairment) is required
  • Revaluation is allowed only for real estate
Why: US GAAP does not permit upward revaluation of PP&E. Once impaired, GAAP creates a new cost basis. IFRS allows the revaluation model (carry at FV), with revaluation gains in OCI (in a "revaluation surplus" reserve) and losses in NI unless reversing prior surplus. This GAAP/IFRS difference is frequently tested.
Question 37A.2 · Disposals

Equipment (cost $80,000, accumulated depreciation $30,000 at last year-end) is sold on April 1 for $42,000. Annual straight-line depreciation = $8,000. What is the gain or loss on sale?

  • $8,000 loss
  • $6,000 loss
  • $12,000 loss
  • $2,000 gain
Why: First, record partial-year depreciation: 3 months × ($8,000/12) = $2,000. Updated AD = $32,000; CV = $80,000 − $32,000 = $48,000. Loss = $42,000 − $48,000 = $6,000. ALWAYS update depreciation to date of sale before computing gain/loss — this trips up many candidates.
Question 38A.2 · Revenue

A customer buys 1,000 units at $50 each but asks the seller to hold the units in the seller's warehouse for 60 days. Which condition would prevent the seller from recognizing revenue at sale?

  • The reason is substantive (customer doesn't have warehouse space)
  • Product is segregated and identified as the customer's
  • Product is ready for physical transfer
  • The seller retains the ability to use the product to fill another customer's order
Why: Bill-and-hold criteria all must be met. The seller must NOT have the ability to redirect the product — that suggests control hasn't transferred. The customer must have effective control even though the goods are physically still at the seller's location.
Question 39A.2 · GAAP/IFRS

Under IFRS, inventory is measured at:

  • Lower of cost and net realizable value (LCNRV)
  • Lower of cost or market (LCM)
  • Fair value
  • Specific identification only
Why: IFRS uses LCNRV exclusively — and reversals are allowed if NRV recovers. US GAAP uses LCNRV for non-LIFO/non-retail inventory and LCM for LIFO inventory; reversals are not allowed. This is a frequently tested distinction.
Question 40Integrative

A company owns 60% of a subsidiary. Under US GAAP, what consolidation accounting method applies?

  • Equity method (60% × subsidiary's net assets)
  • Proportionate consolidation (60% of each line item)
  • Full consolidation with non-controlling interest (100% of all line items, with NCI for the 40%)
  • No consolidation — disclose only
Why: Per the 2024+ IMA outline addition: with control (>50%), use full consolidation — bring in 100% of subsidiary's assets, liabilities, revenues, expenses; recognize "non-controlling interest" (NCI) within equity to represent the 40% the parent doesn't own. Proportionate consolidation isn't typically used under US GAAP. Equity method applies at 20–50% (significant influence without control).
